The glow's static size/position (`glowStaticStyle`) offsets itself by `(glowSize - particle.size) / 2` so it's centered on the same point as the particle despite being bigger.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): overall size was too big once the glow was added.** The 2.5x glow multiplier was applied to the *same* `size: 13, sizeVariance: 9` core used before the glow existed, so the visible footprint (core + glow) ballooned well past the original particle size. Fixed by shrinking the core itself (`size` 13→7, `sizeVariance` 9→5) and slightly lowering `GLOW_SIZE_MULTIPLIER` 2.5→2.2, so the glow now lands back around the *original* pre-glow core size (or a little past it) instead of stacking a 2.5x-bigger halo on top of an unchanged core. That first pass then read as too small — nudged back up to `size: 9, sizeVariance: 6` (between the original 13/9 and the overshot 7/5).
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): split core/glow colors for a more graduated, "flamey" look.** Previously both the solid core and its glow drew from the same `colors` palette/`color` fallback (`particle.color` used for both `staticStyle`/`glowStaticStyle`'s `backgroundColor`) — visually just one hue at two sizes, not really a heat gradient. `Particle` gained its own `glowColor` (alongside the existing `color`) and `ParticleConfig` gained matching `glowColor`/`glowColors` fields, mirroring `color`/`colors`' single-vs-palette shape; `createParticle`'s inline base-color lookup was factored into a small `pickBaseColor(colors, fallback)` helper reused for both. Defaults: the core's `colors` moved to an orange-forward hot palette (`['#FF6B00', '#FF3B1F', '#FF3B1F', '#FF1E1E']`, no yellow/gold — still the "too much yellow" lesson from earlier), while `glowColors` stays a deeper red (`['#FF1E1E', '#FF1E1E', '#D50000', '#FF3B1F']`) closer to `TaskCard`'s own streak-badge "fire" red family — a hot orange-red core with a cooler red haze around it, rather than one flat color at two sizes.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): color now shifts hot-to-cool over each particle's own life, not just varying particle-to-particle.** User asked whether animating this would hurt performance first — answer: no, since Reanimated's `interpolateColor` runs on the UI thread as part of the same per-frame worklet already computing swirl/flicker for a particle, not a new category of work or a bridge crossing. `backgroundColor` moved out of `staticStyle`/`glowStaticStyle` (now truly static — just size/shape/position) into the `particleAnimatedStyle`/`glowAnimatedStyle` worklets (renamed from `particleOpacityStyle`/`glowOpacityStyle` since they now carry color too), each calling `interpolateColor(t, [0, 1], [start, end])` alongside their existing opacity/flicker math. `Particle` gained `colorStart`/`colorEnd` and `glowColorStart`/`glowColorEnd` (from a single `color`/`glowColor` before).
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): replaced the computed cool-down with hand-picked [hot, cooled] color pairs**, per explicit user direction ("you can do better manually picking a collection of colors than dynamically calculating"). The first pass derived each end color via `getCoolerColor` (a plain `tinycolor(...).darken(amount)` off the start color) — same hue, just darker, which undersold the "cooling ember" feel. `ParticleConfig`'s `color`/`colors`/`glowColor`/`glowColors`/`coolAmount`/`coolAmountVariance` were all replaced with `colorPairs`/`glowColorPairs: [string, string][]` — each entry is an authored `[hot, cooled]` pair where the cooled end is a *different hue* (a near-black red/maroon) rather than a darker version of the same hot color, e.g. `['#FFA500', '#4A0404']` (orange → near-black red) or `['#FF4500', '#450000']` (orange-red → deep maroon). `createParticle` now picks one whole pair at random per particle (`pickColorPair`) — separately for the core (`colorPairs`, orange-forward hot ends) and glow (`glowColorPairs`, staying in the deeper red family, ending even darker since the glow should fade toward nothing) — then still runs each end through `getRandomColor`/`colorVariance` for a small per-particle jitter on top of the authored hues, rather than deriving the shift itself.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): particles now grow in fast rather than appearing instantly at full size.** Scale had been driven entirely by `progress` (`{ scale: progress.value }`, 1→0 over the particle's whole `life`), so a particle was always at its full size the instant it mounted. Added a second, independent shared value, `growth` (starts at 0, `withTiming(1, { duration: GROWTH_DURATION = 120, easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic) })`, fired in the same mount effect as `progress`'s own countdown), and changed the wrapper's `scale` transform to `growth.value * progress.value`. `GROWTH_DURATION` is a small fraction of `life` (1000ms+), so drift/swirl (still governed by `t = 1 - progress.value`, tiny this early) stay negligible while growth plays out — no need to delay the main life countdown itself to get "grow in, then drift off and die out"; the two just run in parallel and the short growth window naturally reads as a distinct beat before the rest takes over.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): faster grow-in, and opacity now guaranteed to start at true 100%.** `GROWTH_DURATION` 120→70ms. Separately, the flicker formula (`0.85 + 0.15·sin(t·22 + phase·3)`) wasn't actually guaranteed to equal 1 at `t = 0` for every particle -- with a random `phase`, some particles' opacity started as low as 0.7 rather than fully opaque, which read like growth was fading *in* opacity too rather than being purely a size effect. Fixed by switching to `cos` and moving the per-particle variety from a phase *offset* to a frequency multiplier instead (`0.85 + 0.15·cos(t·(18 + phase·2))`) -- `cos(0) = 1` regardless of what `phase` is, so `flicker` (and therefore opacity, since `progress.value` is also 1 at spawn) is now exactly 1 at the start for every particle, while still flickering at a per-particle-unique rate as `t` increases.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): re-tuned the actual hues, per explicit user direction.** The hot/birth end of `colorPairs` moved from orange (`#FFA500`/`#FF6B00`/etc.) to bright yellow (`#FFEB3B`/`#FFD700`/`#FFC107`/`#FFF176`/`#FFE066`) — a deliberate reversal of the earlier "no yellow" call, which was about a yellow-heavy palette applied flatly across every particle for its whole life, not a brief birth-moment flash; the cooled/death end stayed deep red (`#8B0000`/`#660000`/`#4A0404`/etc.), so the hue shift is now yellow→red rather than orange→red. `glowColorPairs` stayed a mix of oranges and reds (unchanged in spirit, values adjusted slightly), now reading as a warm halo that complements whichever yellow-to-red shade its own particle is showing rather than matching it. `colorVariance` bumped 8→10 for a bit more per-particle spread now that the base palette itself does more of the hue work, and `spawnWindow` widened 250→450ms so the burst trickles in over a longer stretch.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): "not enough red."** Two compounding causes, both fixed. First, `colorPairs`' cooled ends were near-black (`#8B0000`/`#4A0404`/etc.), and since the color transition ran on the same `t` schedule as the opacity fade, the reddest point only ever arrived right as the particle was already nearly invisible — barely readable as red at all. Fixed by (a) brightening the cooled ends to vivid, readable reds (`#D32F2F`/`#C62828`/`#B71C1C`/`#E53935` — Material red shades, still clearly "deep red" but not near-black) and (b) decoupling the color transition's pacing from the opacity fade via a new `COLOR_SHIFT_SPEED = 1.6` multiplier — `interpolateColor` now runs on `Math.min(t * COLOR_SHIFT_SPEED, 1)` rather than raw `t`, so it finishes shifting to the cooled color at ~63% of life instead of 100%, while the particle is still fairly opaque. Second, per explicit user direction for "a little more reddish variation at birth," three of the six `colorPairs` now start orange/red-orange (`#FFA500`/`#FF6B00`/`#FF4500`) instead of yellow, so not every particle is born looking yellow.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): the glow's own cooled ends were still near-black, undoing the "not enough red" fix above.** `colorPairs`' cooled ends had already been brightened to vivid, readable reds (see above), but `glowColorPairs` still ended near-black (`#4A0404`/`#3B0000`/`#450000`/`#2B0000`/`#1F0000`/`#1A0000`) — since the glow is a larger, more visually dominant color field than the small core, this alone made the whole burst still read as "fading to very dark" overall. Per explicit user direction ("opacity will handle the fading out, I just want it to fade to deeper red colors"), every `glowColorPairs` cooled end was brightened to the same deep-red-but-readable territory `colorPairs` already uses (`#C62828`/`#B71C1C`/`#D32F2F` — no color darker than that), so darkening is now opacity's job alone, not the color itself.
    - **Follow-up (2026-08-08): particles now spawn across the badge's actual bounding rect instead of a fixed radial ring.** Previously every particle's origin was a random angle/distance around wherever `<ParticleSystem>` itself renders (a random point on a `distance ± distanceVariance` ring). `ParticleSystemProps` gained an optional `spawnArea?: { width: number; height: number }`; when provided with real (already-measured) dimensions, `createParticle` picks `originX`/`originY` as `Math.random() * width` / `Math.random() * height` instead — a uniformly random point across that rect, same coordinate space (relative to `ParticleSystem`'s own position) the radial spread already used. Falls back to the original radial method if `spawnArea` is omitted or still `{ width: 0, height: 0 }` (not yet measured), so nothing breaks before a parent's first layout pass completes. Initially wired up via `onLayout` measurement on `TaskCard`'s streak badge (a `badgeSize` state + `handleBadgeLayout`), but per follow-up user direction ("the spawn area can be estimated... the badge size is generally a consistent size") that was judged overkill and simplified: the `onLayout`/`badgeSize` state was removed entirely in favor of a plain module-level constant, `ESTIMATED_BADGE_SIZE = { width: 64, height: 28 }` (a rough eyeball of the badge's own styles — icon + streak count text + padding, occasionally + trophy icon — which only varies by a few pixels either way), passed directly as `spawnArea={ESTIMATED_BADGE_SIZE}`. `ParticleSystem`'s own `spawnArea` support (the fallback-to-radial-spread logic, the one-time-per-particle computation) is unchanged — only how `TaskCard` supplies the rect changed, from measured to estimated.
  - **Longer-living**: `life` 1000ms → 1600ms, `lifeVariance` 500ms → 600ms (particles now live roughly 1.0–2.2s, versus 0.5–1.5s before). `ParticleSystem`'s `onComplete` timeout (`config.life + config.lifeVariance`, see above) scales with this automatically — no separate change needed.
- **One minor, low-priority note, not acted on**: `TaskCard.tsx`'s `CardStats` (`getWeeklyStats`/`getMonthlyStats`) each independently `.filter()` the full `task.completions` array over two different (overlapping) date windows — a real duplicate scan, but `CardStats` is already `React.memo`'d (only recomputes when *this* task changes) and completions arrays are realistically bounded, so combining these into one pass wasn't judged worth the added complexity.

**Bug (2026-08-08): `CardStats`'s "Streak: X" / "Best: Y" row wrapped and broke the card's layout once a streak hit double digits.** Neither `<Text>` in that row (`TaskCard.tsx`'s `statRow`) had `numberOfLines` set, so on a narrow card (a smaller phone, or a 4-column grid) the wider two-digit number could push the text past the row's available width — and since RN `Text` wraps by default rather than truncating, it dropped onto a second line instead of staying put, throwing off the whole stats face's height. First fix was `numberOfLines={1}` on all three labels, but per explicit user direction ("I don't want truncation... is there a way to make this fit?") that was superseded by a more thorough one: the verbose `Streak: X`/`Best: Y` text labels were dropped entirely in favor of compact icon+number pairs (a `fire` icon + the current streak, a `trophy` icon + the best streak, both using the existing `statValueRow` layout) — far narrower than the text-label version, so it genuinely fits rather than needing a truncation fallback, and it's more consistent with the flame/trophy icon language the streak badge elsewhere already uses. `bestStreakContainer` (the old wrapper for the `Best!` trophy text) became unused and was deleted rather than left orphaned.

**Follow-up (2026-08-08): the "BEST!" text is back, but only replacing the number, not the whole row.** Per explicit user direction, when the current streak *is* the best (`isBestStreak`), the best-streak side now shows small, compact, all-caps `BEST!` text in place of just the number (the trophy icon stays either way) — `bestStreakText` (previously the `Best!` label's own style before the icon+number redesign, effectively dead until this) was reused and restyled: `fontSize: 11`, `textTransform: 'uppercase'`, `letterSpacing: 0.5`, gold.

**Follow-up (2026-08-08): vertical spacing — "the labels need to be closer to the bars."** `statsContainer`'s `gap: 12` applied uniformly to all six of its effective children (three label-rows and three progress bars, since `PeriodStatRow` returned a bare `<>...</>` Fragment whose two children flattened straight into the parent's flex flow) — meaning a label sat exactly as far from its *own* bar as from the *next* section's bar above it, so nothing read as visually grouped, and the extra height from six uniformly-12px gaps pushed the last bar uncomfortably close to the card's bottom edge. Fixed by wrapping each label-row+bar pair (`PeriodStatRow`'s pair, and the top streak/best pair, which wasn't previously grouped either) in a new `periodStatGroup` (`gap: 4`) — `statsContainer`'s own `gap: 12` now only applies *between* these three group wrappers, not within them. This both pulls each label snug against its own bar and shrinks the total vertical footprint (three 4px internal gaps + two 12px between-group gaps, vs. the old five uniform 12px gaps), giving the bottom bar breathing room it didn't have before.

**Follow-up (2026-08-08): the stats face's current-streak icon+number now match the front face's badge color, per explicit user direction.** `getStreakBadgeStyle` (the status → icon/color mapping: red `fire` for `up_to_date`, orange `clock-outline` for anything else with `currentStreak > 0`, gray `sleep` when `lastStreak > 0` but the current streak has lapsed, `null` otherwise) used to live only inside `CardTask` as a local closure. Extracted to `app/utils/streaks.ts` as a pure `getStreakBadgeStyle(task: Task)` export (field renamed `backgroundColor` → `color`, since it's now used both as a badge pill's background *and*, in `CardStats`, as an icon/text tint — a background-specific name would've been misleading in the second context) so both faces derive the same mapping instead of `CardStats` needing its own copy that could drift out of sync. `CardStats`'s current-streak indicator now uses `streakBadgeStyle?.icon ?? 'fire'` and `streakBadgeStyle?.color ?? colors.textSecondary` for the icon — falling back to the original neutral treatment only when there's no streak history at all (`never_started`, `getStreakBadgeStyle` returns `null`). The icon swaps along with the color (not just a recolored static flame) since the color alone isn't legible as a status signal without the icon that gives it meaning on the badge itself — a gray flame doesn't read as "dormant" the way a gray sleep icon does. Per a follow-up correction, the number itself stays the default `statValue` color (not tinted) — only the icon carries the status color, echoing the badge without recoloring the number too. The best-streak side (trophy/`BEST!`) is unrelated to current status and was left as its own gold/neutral treatment.

**Fixed (2026-08-09): the current-streak progress bar's 100% mark was a flat, meaningless constant.** It scaled `currentStreak` against a hardcoded `10` (`Math.min(currentStreak / 10 * 100, 100)`) — completely disconnected from the task's own history, so a task whose best is 4 days showed the same "mostly empty" bar at a 3-day current streak as a task whose best is 40. Per explicit user direction, it now scales against that task's own `bestStreak` instead: `streakBarFraction = bestStreak > 0 ? Math.min(currentStreak / bestStreak, 1) : 0` — 100% now means "matching or beating your personal record," and lines up exactly with `isBestStreak` (the same condition already driving the `BEST!` label and gold trophy) rather than an arbitrary unrelated threshold. `currentStreak`/`bestStreak` were also pulled into local variables at the top of `CardStats` (previously `task.stats?.currentStreak || 0` was repeated inline four separate times) to keep the new fraction calc and the existing icon/label JSX from re-deriving the same optional-chained values.

**`TaskHeader` now shows a frequency badge (2026-08-09), per explicit user direction ("a nice clean succinct badge/label, readable more than technical").** New `app/utils/formatFrequency.ts` export, `formatFrequencyLabel(task: StreakScheduleInfo): string`, turns the raw schedule fields into a short human phrase instead of the underlying data model's own terminology — no `"specific_days_of_week"`/`"days_per_month"` anywhere in the output, no raw day-index arrays:
- `daily` → `"Daily"`.
- `specific_days_of_week` → `"Weekdays"` / `"Weekends"` for exactly those two common patterns (order-independent), a sorted comma-separated abbreviation list for anything else (e.g. `"Mon, Wed, Fri"`), and `"Daily"` for an empty-or-all-7 selection — matching `isDueOnDate`'s own "no days selected = always due" fallback (`streaks.ts`), so the label never contradicts what the streak engine actually treats as due.
- `days_per_week` / `days_per_month` → `"Nx/week"` / `"Nx/month"`.
- `timesPerDay > 1` appends `" · Nx/day"` to whichever of the above applies (e.g. `"3x/week · 2x/day"`); `timesPerDay === 1` (the common case) adds nothing.

**Follow-up (2026-08-09), per explicit user direction ("'Daily · 4x/day' can just be '4x/day'. We only need the combination for non-daily >1-times-per-day"):** when the base label resolves to exactly `"Daily"` (the plain `daily` frequency, or a `specific_days_of_week` selection that collapses to it), a `timesPerDay > 1` suffix now *replaces* the base instead of combining with it — `"4x/day"`, not `"Daily · 4x/day"` — since "4x/day" already implies every day on its own, so "Daily" adds no information. Every other base (a specific day selection, or a week/month quota) still combines normally, since none of those imply "every day" by themselves.

**Follow-up (2026-08-10), per explicit user direction ("I'd actually like it formatted like '4x/daily' (or the best readble way to print that), and 'weekly' and 'monthly'"):** the slash-abbreviated quantity phrasing (`"3x/week"`, `"10x/month"`, `"4x/day"`) was replaced with the more natural, space-separated adverb form — `"3x weekly"`, `"10x monthly"`, `"4x daily"` — matching pharmacy-label-style English ("take 2x daily") rather than a terser slash notation, per the user's own "or the best readable way" invitation to pick the clearer of the two over their literal `"4x/daily"` suggestion (a slash directly against an adverb reads oddly). The `"Daily · Nx daily"` → `"Nx daily"` collapse from the prior follow-up carries over unchanged, just with the reworded suffix.

Pure and unit-tested (`__tests__/utils/formatFrequency.test.ts`) — deliberately does **not** import `app/constants/task.tsx`'s `DAY_ABBREVIATIONS` despite it being the obvious shared source, since that module pulls in `@expo/vector-icons` (for `ALL_ICONS`' glyph map), which broke jest resolution when pulled into this otherwise-pure file (`Cannot find module` from `expo-font`'s native loader). Keeps its own identical local copy of the day-abbreviation array instead — a small, deliberate duplication over a cross-module import that would compromise testability. `AddTaskScreen.tsx`'s day-of-week picker was separately refactored to import the shared `DAY_ABBREVIATIONS` from `constants/task.tsx` (previously its own private local array of the same 7 strings) purely as a minor consolidation — that file has no testability constraint, so it's fine for it to depend on the icons-importing module it already depends on anyway.

Rendered in `TaskHeader.tsx` as a new small pill (`frequencyBadge`, translucent white background, `repeat` icon) directly under the task name and above the existing streak-status badge row — deliberately smaller/quieter (`fontSize: 12` vs. the streak badge's `16`) since it's supporting context ("what the schedule is"), not the headline number ("how the streak is doing right now").


`app/context/ToastContext.tsx` (`useToast().showToast({ message, action?, duration? })`) + `app/components/ToastBanner.tsx` (rendered once at the root, in `_layout.tsx`, as a sibling of `AppGate` — so it overlays every screen and survives navigation) replace `Alert.alert` as the app's default feedback mechanism. Only one toast shows at a time; a new one replaces whatever's currently up. The banner is a solid, theme-independent dark bar with white text and an accent-colored action label, same reasoning as `OnboardingHint`'s bubble — needs to read clearly regardless of the current theme. Default duration is 8s (2026-08-07, up from 4s — long enough to read the message and reach for Undo without rushing); a toast can also be swiped away early via `Gesture.Pan()`/`GestureDetector` (react-native-gesture-handler) — past either a distance or velocity threshold it animates off in the swiped direction (fading out as it goes, layered on top of the entrance fade-in rather than replacing it) before calling `onDismiss`. `GestureDetector` requires a `GestureHandlerRootView` ancestor, which Expo Router's own `Stack` doesn't provide — added at the true root in `_layout.tsx` (wrapping everything, including the providers) specifically for this; omitting it throws at runtime on native ("GestureDetector must be used as descendent of GestureHandlerRootView"), a bug only surfaced testing on an actual Android device since it's silent on web.

The policy, per the user's explicit direction (2026-08-07): **no modal confirmations** — perform the action immediately and offer "Undo" via toast — for every action *except* Archive/Delete Task, which the user asked back into an `Alert.alert` confirm specifically because they now live at the bottom of the Edit Task form (denser, easier to mis-tap) rather than their own dedicated screen. Concretely:
- **Complete a task** (`HomeScreen.handleLongPressTask`) → toast + Undo (`undoCompleteTask`).
- **Toggle a day on `TaskCalendarScreen`** (tap a day to complete/clear it) → toast + Undo either way: completing uses `undoCompleteTask`, clearing snapshots the day's `TaskCompletion` before calling `uncompleteTask` and restores it via `restoreCompletion` (needed for multi-rep tasks, where clearing loses a `timesCompleted` > 1 that a plain re-complete wouldn't recover). Originally left off the calendar on the reasoning that tapping again was already its own undo, but the user asked for it back in for consistency with the rest of the app (2026-08-06).
- **Delete / Archive Task** (`AddTaskScreen`, editing mode only) → `Alert.alert` confirm first, *then* toast + Undo (`restoreDeletedTask` / `restoreTask`) after — belt-and-suspenders for anyone who confirms on autopilot. Restoring an archived task doesn't get a toast; re-archiving is the obvious reverse action, always one tap away.
- **Import Data** → no more merge-vs-replace `Alert.alert` prompt. "Import Data" always merges; "Import & Replace All Data" is its own explicit, separately-labeled Settings row (destructive-red icon/text). Either way the full previous `tasks` array is snapshotted before `importTasks` runs, so the toast's Undo is just `importTasks(previousTasks, { mode: 'replace' })` regardless of which mode the original import used. The old "this file looks newer/older/already-imported" advisory copy (which existed to inform the pre-action Merge/Replace choice) was dropped since that choice no longer happens at runtime; "already imported before" now folds into the post-import toast message instead.
- **Export, validation errors, generic failures** → plain info toast, no action — these were always single-dismiss notices, never true confirmations, so converting them was just consistency cleanup.

**Web-testing caveat:** `Alert.alert` (the Archive/Delete confirm) is a no-op on react-native-web in this project's setup — tapping Archive/Delete in a web build shows nothing and does nothing (verified: neither the task's `archived` flag nor its existence changes), rather than silently skipping the confirmation and acting anyway. This isn't a bug introduced here; react-native-web simply doesn't implement native `Alert.alert`. It works normally on Android (the actual target platform). Do one manual smoke test on-device: Edit Task → Archive Task / Delete Task → confirm the modal appears and both Cancel and the destructive action behave as expected.

**A real bug this surfaced (fixed, worth knowing about):** every `TaskContext` mutator used to read the `tasks` array via closure. That's fine for handlers invoked synchronously off a fresh render, but toast "Undo" actions are captured once and invoked *later* — by then the closure's `tasks` could predate the very state update the undo is supposed to reverse (e.g. `undoCompleteTask` couldn't find the completion it was told to remove, since its closure was from before `completeTask`'s update had landed). Fixed by having every mutator build its next array from `saveTasks`'s functional-updater `prev` parameter instead of the outer `tasks` variable — see `withUpdatedStats` and the `saveTasks(prev => ...)` pattern used throughout. Caught by manually driving the full press-toast-undo cycle in a browser rather than just checking the toast rendered.

## Haptics

`expo-haptics` (2026-08-07 — installed since the project's start but unused until now) is called directly at each interaction site rather than wrapped in a shared helper — every call site already needed a different feedback type/style, so a wrapper would just be a thin pass-through. Scoped deliberately to *meaningful* moments (completions, destructive confirms, the long-press gesture threshold, undo) rather than every tap — routine navigation (FAB, header buttons, filter bubbles, tab/toggle switches, month navigation, Dashboard row taps) intentionally has none, to avoid it reading as spammy. Safe on web: `expo-haptics`' web shim silently no-ops when the Vibration API isn't available rather than throwing, so no platform guard was needed.

- **`TaskCard.handleLongPress`** — `impactAsync(Light)` fires immediately once the long-press threshold registers, before whatever it triggers (opening Calendar/Stats, starting the completion pop, or opening task-detail for an already-completed task) actually plays out. Same call for all four long-press outcomes, since this is acknowledging the *gesture*, not its result.
- **`HomeScreen.handleLongPressTask`** — the actual completion commit gets `notificationAsync(Success)` (the "reward" moment, arriving ~`COMPLETION_HOLD_DURATION` after the `Light` tap above, once the pop animation settles and `onCompleted` fires). This handler is now only ever reached for a not-yet-completed task (see below) — long-pressing an already-completed card gets no additional haptic beyond `TaskCard`'s own gesture-acknowledgment `impactAsync(Light)`, since nothing is being completed or confirmed.
- **`TaskCalendarScreen.handleDayPress`** — completing a day that reaches its full `timesPerDay` quota gets `notificationAsync(Success)`; logging one of several reps on a >1x/day task (still short of quota) gets `impactAsync(Light)` instead, mirroring the toast's own full-vs-partial message; clearing a completed day gets `impactAsync(Light)`.
- **`ToastBanner`'s Undo button** — `impactAsync(Light)`, fired before `action.onPress()` runs.
- **`AddTaskScreen`** — the destructive confirm itself (tapping "Delete" or "Archive" inside the `Alert.alert`, not the button that opens it) gets `notificationAsync(Warning)`; restoring an already-archived task (no confirm needed, per the Toasts policy above) gets `impactAsync(Light)`.

## Accessibility

(2026-08-07) `accessibilityLabel`/`Role`/`Hint`/`State` added across every screen and component — previously nowhere in `app/`, so icon-only buttons (most of the app's header buttons, FAB, steppers, toggles) were silent to screen readers. General approach:
- Every icon-only `TouchableOpacity`/`Pressable` got an explicit `accessibilityLabel` describing the action (e.g. "Back", "Settings", "Decrease days per week") rather than relying on the icon glyph, which screen readers can't interpret.
- Mutually-exclusive toggle groups (frequency type, theme mode, Grid/Bars, Month/Year, time-range pickers, sort toggles) use `accessibilityRole="radio"` + `accessibilityState={{ checked }}`; independently-toggleable sets (day-of-week picker, Dashboard's task filter bubbles) use `accessibilityRole="checkbox"` + `accessibilityState={{ checked }}` instead, since more than one can be active at once. Screen/section tab bars (`TaskHeader`, `DashboardScreen`'s Calendar/Stats/Streaks) use `accessibilityRole="tab"` + `accessibilityState={{ selected }}`.
- Color/icon swatches (`ColorPicker`, `IconPicker`) have no visible text at all — `ColorPicker` labels each swatch by its hex value (`Color #EF4444`; no color-naming data exists at runtime, only as source comments on `DEFAULT_COLORS`/`EXTENDED_COLOR_BATCHES`, so this was the honest option short of a larger data-model change), `IconPicker` labels each by its icon name with hyphens turned to spaces (`run-fast` → "run fast").
- Tappable list/card rows that navigate elsewhere (Dashboard's Stats bar-chart rows, Streaks history rows, the per-task mini calendar cards, Archived Tasks rows) get a combined `accessibilityLabel` (task name + the relevant stat) plus an `accessibilityHint` naming the destination ("Opens this task's stats").
- `TaskCard`'s main `Pressable` is the hardest case: the same physical control tap-flips between task/calendar/stats faces *and* long-press-completes or long-press-opens-the-full-screen, and a physical long-press isn't a standard screen-reader gesture (VoiceOver/TalkBack's own double-tap already maps to `onPress`/flip). Solved with a custom `accessibilityActions` entry (labeled dynamically per visible face — "Mark complete" / "Open full calendar" / "Open full stats") routed through the *same* `handleLongPress` the physical gesture calls via `onAccessibilityAction`, so the two stay in sync automatically without duplicating logic.
- `ToastBanner` announces itself via `accessibilityLiveRegion="polite"` + `accessibilityRole="alert"` so a screen reader picks up a new toast without the user needing to manually navigate to it; its Undo button gets `accessibilityRole="button"` + a label matching its visible text.
- `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Month-view day cells get a full spoken state (`"Monday, January 5, Completed"` / `"...2 of 3 completed"` / `"...Missed"`) plus `accessibilityState={{ disabled: isFuture }}`, since the visual encoding (solid fill / pie wedge / X mark / dimmed) carries no equivalent information otherwise.
- Native `Switch` components (`SettingsScreen`) already announce their own on/off state as a platform control, but needed an explicit `accessibilityLabel` matching their row's text — a `Switch` and its neighboring `Text` aren't programmatically associated otherwise, so without one a screen reader would only announce "on"/"off" with no idea *what* was toggled.

Touch targets: `AddTaskScreen`'s day-of-week buttons and the three +/-/stepper pairs (days/week, days/month, times/day) were 36×36 and 32×32 respectively — both below iOS HIG's 44×44pt minimum. Bumped to 44×44 (radius adjusted to stay circular); the day-of-week row still fits 7 buttons across a standard phone width with `justifyContent: 'space-between'`, just with less slack than before.

`app/utils/streaks.ts` — frequency-aware streak engine, extracted from `TaskContext`:
- Filters completions to "qualifying" ones first (`timesCompleted >= task.timesPerDay`) — a partially-logged day doesn't count.
- `daily` / `specific_days_of_week`: a streak only *breaks* on a missed **due** day, but every day completed — due or not — adds to the running count (2026-08-07, per explicit user direction: previously non-due days were just skipped/ignored entirely, worth neither credit nor blame). Internally this walks every calendar day (not just due days) into segments that each end at a due-day "gate": hitting the gate links the whole segment's day-count into the streak, missing it resets the chain going forward — but the segment that closes on a miss still keeps its own bonus days in `bestStreak`/`lastStreak` up to (not including) the actual miss, rather than losing them entirely; only the *next* segment starts the new `currentStreak` from zero. A due day that's today and not yet completed isn't a miss yet — its trailing days are held as a "pending" segment that's still provisionally counted (e.g. completing Sat/Sun ahead of Monday's gate counts immediately rather than waiting for Monday to resolve). For `daily`, every day is its own due day, so this degenerates to the simple one-day-per-segment case with no non-due days to add.
- `days_per_week` / `days_per_month`: the streak unit is the period (week/month) — a period counts as "met" once enough distinct qualifying days land in it, including mid-period for the current one (so hitting the weekly quota early still keeps the streak alive without waiting for the week to end). A period that fails its quota still contributes its own days to the run that's ending (same "tail stays counted up to the miss" idea as due days above) — it just doesn't link forward, so the next period starts fresh. Status only flips to `'expiring'` once every remaining day of the still-open current period — including today, if today's own chance hasn't been used yet — would be *required* to still reach quota (2026-08-07, per explicit user direction: e.g. needing 1 more day only turns urgent on the period's last day; needing 3 more turns urgent once exactly 3 days remain). Comfortable slack short of quota still reads as `'up_to_date'`, not `'expiring'`.

  **Bug fix (2026-08-12), per direct user report ("if all remaining days are required to meet the quota, but the current day is completed, it shows expiring -- it should show streak").** `calculateQuotaStats`'s own `'expiring'` condition (`isTight = remainingOpportunities <= stillNeeded`) never actually checked whether *today itself* had already been used before flagging urgency -- it already correctly *excluded* today from the `remainingOpportunities` count once used (`todayAlreadyCounted`), but that only affected how tight the *future* days were, not whether today's own already-banked action should suppress "expiring" altogether. Confirmed as a genuine inconsistency, not a deliberate quota-specific design choice, by checking `calculateDueDayStats` (the daily/specific_days_of_week sibling of this function, just above): that branch already gates its own `'expiring'` on `isPending && todayIsDue && !todayCompleted` -- the due-day engine has always required today to be *not yet done* before calling it urgent; the quota engine simply never had the equivalent guard. `'expiring'` is meant to be an actionable "do something today or this breaks" signal -- once today's own action is already taken, there's nothing left to act on *today* regardless of how tight the days *after* today are (that becomes tomorrow's own problem to flag, if it's still tight once tomorrow arrives still undone). Fixed with one added clause: `isTight = !todayAlreadyCounted && remainingOpportunities <= stillNeeded`. Also resolves a real inconsistency this exposed: `DashboardCalendarView.tsx`/`TaskCalendarScreen.tsx`'s own "expiring today" clock-icon checks (`isExpiringToday`) were already independently guarding with their own redundant `!isCompleted` condition alongside `streakStatus === 'expiring'` -- this fix makes the underlying status itself correct instead of relying on every downstream consumer to compensate for it a second time. Two new regression tests added directly beside the existing "only flags expiring once..." tests in `__tests__/utils/streaks.test.ts` (for both `days_per_week` and `days_per_month`), confirming the fix in both directions: already-done-today at zero remaining slack reads `'up_to_date'`, while the identical tightness with today still undone correctly stays `'expiring'`. Verified via a throwaway diagnostic test run directly against the real reported scenario before writing the permanent test coverage, per this project's own established practice for exactly this class of streak-engine investigation. `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (207/207 at the time, unaffected elsewhere).

  **A second, much bigger bug surfaced immediately by the same user, on-device, testing the fix above (2026-08-13): "I'm now looking at a day that has daily tasks which aren't complete but show a streak status of up to date."** Diagnosed directly against the code, not guessed: a genuine `daily`-frequency task routes through `calculateDueDayStats` (untouched by the fix above) and, tested standalone with an open streak and today not completed, correctly returns `'expiring'` -- so the streak-math itself wasn't the culprit this time. The real cause was one level up, in `taskStore.ts`: every task's `.stats` (the field every badge/screen actually reads -- `currentStreak`, `streakStatus`, etc.) is a **cached snapshot**, only ever recomputed by `withUpdatedStats` at the exact moment a mutation happens (`completeTask`, `undoCompleteTask`, `archiveTask`, ...), each call defaulting `calculateTaskStats`'s own `asOfDate` to real "now" *as of that mutation*. Nothing re-triggers that computation just because real time keeps passing with no further mutation -- so a 94-day streak locked in by completing it *yesterday* correctly computed `'up_to_date'` as of yesterday's own mutation, and then simply never got recomputed again: opening the app *today*, having done nothing yet, still shows yesterday's stale cached verdict, since nothing told the store "today has now arrived, and today itself hasn't been touched." This is a pre-existing architectural gap (not introduced by the fix above, and not specific to any one frequency type -- `daily`/`specific_days_of_week`/quota tasks are all equally affected), just newly surfaced by the user specifically re-testing streak-status behavior right after the entry above landed.

  Deliberately **not** fixed by making every consumer (`getStreakBadgeStyle`, `TaskCard`, `DashboardStatsView`'s aggregate calc, six-plus other call sites) recompute `calculateTaskStats` live on every render instead of trusting the cached field -- `.stats` being a stable, mutation-time snapshot is exactly what `achievements.ts`'s whole detection engine is built around (before/after diffing a specific completion event, see that file's own extensive "chronological replay" history above), and that architecture needs to stay snapshot-based, not become live-recomputed. Instead, kept the cached-field model but closed the actual staleness window: a new `refreshStats()` action on `taskStore.ts` recomputes every task's `.stats` fresh against real "now," *without* bumping `updatedAt` (this isn't a real edit, just refreshing a derived field -- bumping it would misrepresent "when was this task actually last changed" to export/sync consumers). Called from a new `useEffect` in `_layout.tsx`'s `AppGate`, on two triggers: once immediately after hydration completes (covers a cold launch on a fresh day after the app was fully closed overnight), and again on every `AppState` transition to `'active'` (covers the far more common real case -- backgrounding overnight without a full relaunch; this is the first use of `AppState` anywhere in this codebase). Deliberately **not** a running timer while the app stays open and actively foregrounded -- crossing midnight during one continuous session without ever backgrounding is a rare edge case, not worth a live interval for. `refreshStats()` never calls `recordCompletionAchievements` -- achievements still only ever fire from `completeTask` itself (a real user action), so this passive background refresh can't retroactively "earn" anything just because time passed.

  No new unit test coverage -- the underlying `calculateTaskStats`/`calculateDueDayStats`/`calculateQuotaStats` logic itself was already correct and already covered (this bug was purely about *when* that already-correct function gets called, an app-lifecycle/store-wiring concern, not a pure-function one), matching this project's own stated testing scope (pure `utils/` functions only, no component/store/lifecycle tests). Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (210/210, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is the one that actually matters here, given the bug itself was only ever visible on-device: leave a long-streak task uncompleted overnight, background the app (don't force-quit), reopen it the next day, and confirm the badge now correctly reads `'expiring'` (or `'expired'`, if enough time has passed) immediately on reopen rather than only after the task itself is next touched.

  **Follow-up (2026-08-13), a real efficiency concern raised directly by the user after asking how the fix worked: "why not just CHECK to see if the cached stats are from a prior day and refresh them if so" instead of unconditionally recomputing every task on every foreground.** A fair catch -- the original `refreshStats()` rebuilt *every* task's object identity via `.map()` on every single foreground event, regardless of whether the calendar day had actually changed since the last check. That directly works against a pattern this codebase invests real effort in elsewhere (see "State management" above): every other mutator here deliberately preserves the exact object reference for any task that didn't change, specifically so a per-task selector can skip re-rendering components that read an unaffected task. An unconditional full-array rebuild defeats that for *every* task, *every* time the app comes back to the foreground -- multiple unnecessary re-renders, plus (since Zustand's `persist` middleware writes to AsyncStorage after every `set()`) an unnecessary disk write, even on a day where nothing was ever actually stale.

  Fixed by adding a day-check guard rather than a real per-task invalidation scheme (which would need tracking, per task, exactly when its own stats were last computed -- overkill, since staleness here is purely a function of "has the calendar day changed," identical for every task at once, not something that varies task to task). `taskStore.ts` gained `statsRefreshedOn: string | null` -- a plain `'yyyy-MM-dd'` string, deliberately **not persisted** (excluded via the store's existing `partialize`, same as `hasHydrated`) since it only needs to guard against redundant checks *within one running process*; a fresh cold launch always starts with it `null`, so the very first check after any real app restart still genuinely recomputes regardless of how long the app was actually closed, which is exactly the case that needs it to. `refreshStats` was renamed `maybeRefreshStats` and now short-circuits (`if (get().statsRefreshedOn === todayStr) return;`) before touching `tasks` at all whenever it's already checked today -- only performing the real recompute (and re-stamping `statsRefreshedOn`) on a genuine day change. In practice this means: one real recompute the first time the app opens on any given day, and a cheap no-op string comparison on every foreground after that for the rest of the day, restoring the same reference-preservation guarantee this store's other mutators already provide.

  No new mutator needed to also stamp `statsRefreshedOn` after a real completion/edit -- by the time any user interaction is even possible, `maybeRefreshStats` has already run at least once for that process (at hydration), so every task is already known-fresh for the current day; a later single-task mutation that same day doesn't reintroduce any gap for the *other*, untouched tasks. Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (210/210, unaffected -- this remains an app-lifecycle/store-wiring change with no pure-function logic to unit test). Not yet confirmed on-device -- same manual check as the entry above, plus confirming repeated backgrounding/foregrounding *within* the same day doesn't cause visible re-render churn or repeated AsyncStorage writes (e.g. via a quick `console.log` in `maybeRefreshStats` while testing, not left in the shipped code).

Data model (`app/types/index.ts`):
- `Task`: id, name, icon, color, `frequency` (`daily | specific_days_of_week | days_per_week | days_per_month`), `daysOfWeek`, `daysPerWeek`, `daysPerMonth`, `timesPerDay`, timestamps, optional `archived`, `stats`, `completions`
- `TaskCompletion`: id, taskId, `date` (yyyy-MM-dd, the day it counts for), `completedAt` (actual timestamp), `timesCompleted` (increments per press up to `timesPerDay`, rather than one record per press)

`app/utils/data.ts` holds pure chart/date-range helpers (time-frame bucketing, day-of-week/hour-of-day histograms, aggregate stats) used by Dashboard and per-task Stats screens.

Three smaller pure-logic modules were pulled out of component files specifically so they're unit-testable (components themselves aren't unit-tested — see Testing above): `app/utils/periodStats.ts` (`getExpectedPeriodTotal` — the frequency-aware "x/N" quota math behind TaskCard's "This week"/"Past 30 days" boxes), `app/utils/pieWedge.ts` (`buildPieWedgePath` — the SVG path geometry behind `PartialDayPie`'s clockwise wedge), and `app/utils/calendarGrid.ts` (`getTrailingBlankCount` — the leading/trailing blank-cell padding math shared by the calendar grids in `TaskCard`'s flipped face and `TaskCalendarScreen`).

**Partial-day fill now carries "heatmap" strength (2026-08-07):** every calendar's partial-completion fill used to be one flat opacity regardless of how close to done a >1x/day task's day actually was (1-of-3 reps read identically to 2-of-3). `PartialDayPie` (the wedge used by `TaskCard`'s flipped calendar face, `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Month view, and `DashboardCalendarView`'s main grid) now sets its fill `opacity` to `Math.max(0.35, fraction)` instead of a flat `0.35` — the wedge angle already encoded the fraction, opacity now reinforces it instead of staying constant. The two flat-opacity dot grids that don't use `PartialDayPie` (`TaskCalendarScreen`'s Year view `yearDot`, and `DashboardCalendarView`'s per-task mini month grid `monthGridDot`) got the same treatment directly on their own partial-state style (`opacity: Math.max(0.35, fraction)`), each needing a `fraction` field added to their per-day cell type since neither previously computed one (only the boolean `isPartial`). The 0.35 floor is kept from the original flat constant, so a bare sliver of progress still reads clearly rather than fading to near-invisible. `TaskCard`'s own front-face today's-progress ring is deliberately untouched — it's a separate `AnimatedPath`, not `PartialDayPie`, and isn't a calendar.

**Soft-skip vs. hard-miss calendar indicators (2026-08-09), per explicit user direction.** Every calendar previously rendered any empty past day the same way (`MissedDayMark`'s hand-drawn X, in `colors.textTertiary`), regardless of whether that day actually broke the streak. Two real gaps: `TaskCard`'s `CardCalendar` and `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Month view computed `isMissed` as plain `isPast && !isCompleted && !isPartial` with **no due-day check at all** — so even a `specific_days_of_week` task's genuinely non-due days showed a hard-miss X (only `DashboardCalendarView`'s main grid was due-aware, via `isDueOnDate`, and even that one still showed an X for every empty day on `days_per_week`/`days_per_month` quota tasks, since `isDueOnDate` returns `true` unconditionally for those frequencies — it only special-cases `specific_days_of_week`). The deeper issue: quota-based frequencies evaluate success at the *period* level (see `calculateQuotaStats`), so no single empty day on those is ever really "the" miss the way a missed due day is for `daily`/`specific_days_of_week`.

`canDayBreakStreak` went through **two** wrong designs before landing on the right one — both corrections came from the same user, each pointing at a real conceptual gap rather than a bug in the arithmetic:

1. **First pass**: `false` unconditionally for both quota frequencies ("quota is decided at the period level, so no single day is ever *the* miss"). Corrected — a quota period absolutely can have real hard misses, just not on a fixed per-day schedule the way due-day gating does.
2. **Second pass**: custom per-day "capacity" math — walk the day's own period, sum what's already banked before it plus every day still available after it, and call it a hard miss once that total can't reach quota anymore. This was internally consistent and heavily tested, but per explicit user redirect it was the wrong *shape* of solution: *"We already had all the logic to calculate streaks, we shouldn't need all this additional logic, just fill the connection spaces with a dash rather than an X."* Concretely, it also produced the wrong real-world result — a currently-losing week/month mostly showed dashes (capacity math only turns to X once quota becomes mathematically unreachable, which for a generous quota can be very late in the period), when the user expected a genuinely failed period to read as mostly/entirely X.

**Final design**, entirely reusing the existing streak-history machinery instead of inventing new logic: `canDayBreakStreak(task: Task, date: Date, chains: StreakChain[]): boolean` now lives in `app/utils/reports.ts` (moved from `streaks.ts` — it needs `getTaskStreakChains`, defined in `reports.ts`, which itself imports from `streaks.ts`; keeping `canDayBreakStreak` in `streaks.ts` would have made that a circular import). For `daily`/`specific_days_of_week` it's unchanged — still just `isDueOnDate` (a missed due day always breaks, a non-due day never can, regardless of streak history). For `days_per_week`/`days_per_month`:
- A day is a **soft skip (dash)** if it falls inside the span (`chain.startDate`–`chain.endDate`, inclusive) of any chain `getTaskStreakChains` already reconstructs — the dash literally connects two real days that are part of the same run, exactly matching the user's framing.
- Or if it falls inside the **current, not-yet-elapsed period** of a streak that's still alive right now (`task.stats.currentStreak > 0`) — `getTaskStreakChains` only reflects periods that are already *decided*, so a current period that hasn't reached quota yet (but hasn't run out of time either) won't appear as a chain yet even though it isn't broken; `task.stats.currentStreak` already carries the correct "don't judge a period before it's over" logic from `calculateQuotaStats`, so it's the source of truth for this "pending tail" case (per the user: *"we will have a tail on pending streaks... the current week/month is not yet over"*).
- Otherwise it's a **hard miss (X)** — before the task's first-ever completion, or in the dead space after a streak has actually broken, matching *"we shouldn't have dashes before at least 1 completed day."*

Verified against the real demo dataset (`demo-data/streakaholic-demo-data.json`, via a throwaway `tsx` script printing a full mark-by-mark day string per task) before considering this done: an `expired`/`never_started` task now shows a solid run of X's for the entire dead stretch since its last real chain ended, a currently-`up_to_date` task's ongoing streak dashes cleanly through to yesterday, and a day inside an old closed chain still reads as connected regardless of that chain's own period having ultimately failed or succeeded.

**Performance note**: `getTaskStreakChains` walks a task's *entire* completion history, so calendar grids memoize it once per task (`useMemo(() => getTaskStreakChains(task), [task])` in `TaskCard`/`TaskCalendarScreen`; a `Map<taskId, StreakChain[]>` in `DashboardCalendarView`, mirroring its existing `completionCountsByTask` pattern) and pass the result into `canDayBreakStreak` per cell, rather than letting the function recompute chains itself on every single empty-day evaluation across a grid.

**Follow-up (2026-08-09): the skip line now visually connects across consecutive days, and shows the day number faded behind it where numbers are shown, per explicit user direction.** `SkippedDayMark.tsx` was rewritten from a small hand-drawn SVG `Line` (fixed `size`, inset within its own little box) to a plain `View` bar that stretches to `width: '100%'` of whatever cell it's given — a straight rectangle has no icon-font baseline-offset concerns to fight (unlike `MissedDayMark`'s X, which is still SVG for that reason), so there was no reason to keep it SVG-based once the goal was "fill the available width" rather than "draw a precise small glyph."

The real fix wasn't the component, though — it was *where* each screen renders it. All three calendar surfaces had been nesting the mark inside a smaller inner circle (`calendarDayInner`'s `calendarDot`-adjacent slot in `TaskCard`, `gridDot` in `DashboardCalendarView`, `dayContent` in `TaskCalendarScreen`) that's noticeably narrower than the actual day cell and, in two of the three, has `overflow: 'hidden'` on top of that — a `width: 100%` line in there would still get clipped short of the cell's true edges, so consecutive skipped days' lines would never actually touch. Fixed by moving the line out to render full-bleed over the *outer* cell instead:
- `TaskCard.tsx`'s `CardCalendar`: no restructuring needed — `calendarDayInner` isn't clipped and already sizes to (almost) the full cell, so just dropping the old `size` prop was enough for the line to stretch correctly.
- `DashboardCalendarView.tsx`: the skip mark moved from a child of `gridDot` (a small clipped circle) to a new sibling `skippedLineOverlay` — `position: 'absolute'`, filling the full `gridCell` — rendered after `gridDot` so it paints on top. `gridDot` itself still switches to `isSkipped`'s shared `gridDotEmpty` background, same as before, just no longer draws the mark itself.
- `TaskCalendarScreen.tsx`'s Month view: same `skippedLineOverlay` pattern, filling the outer `day` `TouchableOpacity` (not the inner circular, `overflow: 'hidden'` `dayContent`), with `pointerEvents="none"` so the decorative overlay never intercepts the cell's own tap handling. This is also the screen with day numbers, so `isSkipped`'s branch inside `dayContent` changed from rendering `SkippedDayMark` directly to rendering the day number itself at reduced opacity (`skippedDayNumber: { opacity: 0.4 }`, matching the existing `futureDay` treatment) — the line overlay then paints on top of that faded number afterward in render order, so the number shows through faintly *behind* the connecting line rather than being replaced by it. `TaskCard`/`DashboardCalendarView` don't show day numbers at all, so this half of the change only applies to `TaskCalendarScreen`.

None of the three screens had a gap between adjacent day cells in their row layout to begin with (`calendarWeekRow`/`weekRow`/the Timeline grid's column `FlatList` all lay cells edge-to-edge already), so once each line fills its own cell's true full width, neighboring cells' lines meet with no visible seam — reading as one continuous connector strung across a whole streak run rather than a series of isolated dashes.

**Follow-up (2026-08-09): completed days now grow connector stubs too, per explicit user direction ("add a connector line on the filled days... on the first day of a streak there's no left line, on the last day of a streak there's no right line except to connect a gap tail in quota based streaks").** Previously only skipped (dash) days drew a line — a completed day's dot just sat there, so a completed day next to a skipped day left a visible gap between the dot's own (smaller-than-the-cell) edge and the skip line's far end, even though the skip line itself already reached the shared cell boundary.

New `isConnectedDay(task, date, chains, completionCounts)` export in `reports.ts`: true if `date` is completed, or is a soft skip per `canDayBreakStreak`, or is *today* specifically with a live streak reaching into it (`task.stats.currentStreak > 0` — today is never itself marked completed/skipped by any of the three screens, since all three gate on `isPast`, so it needs its own case here). A day gets a **left connector stub exactly when its immediate predecessor is also connected**, and a **right connector stub exactly when its immediate successor is also connected** — deliberately not computed from chain boundaries directly, since checking the two neighboring days' own connected-ness already reproduces every case the user asked for with no special-casing: a streak's first day has a non-connected predecessor (nothing before it, or a hard miss) so it naturally gets no left stub; a streak's last day gets no right stub *unless* the day after it is also connected — which is exactly the quota "pending tail" case (the day after a quota chain's last real completion can still be a soft skip if the current period is still open and alive), so the exception the user called out falls out of the same rule for free rather than needing to be coded separately. This also means a completed day immediately before *today* grows a right stub reaching toward it whenever today is part of a live streak, which extends naturally to due-day-gated frequencies too, not just quota ones.

Rendering-wise, each connector stub is a `SkippedDayMark` (already a `width: '100%'` bar) wrapped in a `left: 0`/`right: 0`, `width: '50%'` absolutely-positioned half-cell container — reusing the mark component rather than adding a new one. Same clipping consideration as the skip-line fix above: `TaskCard`'s `calendarDayInner` isn't clipped, so the stubs render as ordinary siblings there; `TaskCalendarScreen`'s `dayContent` has `overflow: 'hidden'` and is smaller than its outer cell, so its stubs render as siblings of that inner circle instead, spanning the full outer cell like the skip-line overlay does. **Paint order matters here** (unlike the plain skip line, which has nothing on top of it): each screen renders its connector stub(s) *before* the completed-day dot/circle in JSX, so the solid dot paints over the stub's inner half and only the outer half — reaching toward the shared cell edge — stays visible; getting this backwards would tint part of the dot itself with the stub's lower-opacity color. `isConnectedDay` is covered in `__tests__/utils/reports.test.ts`.

**Follow-up (2026-08-09): reverted from `DashboardCalendarView`'s Timeline grid entirely, per explicit user direction ("This looks great on the Task Details Calendar view. Revert it from the Timeline view -- I want the line to be solid across connected lines, but dots don't need connector stubs").** `TaskCard`'s flipped calendar face and `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Month view (the "Task Details Calendar view") keep the connector stubs described above. `DashboardCalendarView.tsx` dropped `hasLeftConnector`/`hasRightConnector`, the `isConnectedDay` import, the now-unneeded `addDays` import, and the `leftConnectorOverlay`/`rightConnectorOverlay` styles entirely — back to only `isSkipped` rendering its own full-width `SkippedDayMark` line, with completed days as plain dots and no connector stubs. `isConnectedDay` itself is untouched (still used by the two screens that kept this feature) and its test coverage stays as-is.

**Follow-up (2026-08-09): the soft-skip dash on `TaskCard`'s own flipped calendar face (the Home screen task grid) now looks deliberately different from the other two calendars' — thicker, shorter, and semi-transparent, per explicit user direction.** This is the densest, smallest calendar grid in the app, and the full-width edge-to-edge connecting line (right for the larger `TaskCalendarScreen`/Dashboard grids) read as too thin/busy at this scale. `day.isSkipped`'s `SkippedDayMark` is now wrapped in a `calendarSkippedLineWrap` (`width: '55%', alignSelf: 'center', opacity: 0.5`) instead of rendering directly at the full cell width, and passes `thickness={4}` (up from the component's own default of `2.5`) — a short, bold, semi-transparent tick instead of a thin full-width connector. This is a deliberate per-screen visual choice, not a component default change: `SkippedDayMark` itself is untouched (still defaults to a full-width `2.5`px bar), so `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Month view and `DashboardCalendarView`'s Timeline grid are unaffected — only this one call site overrides the width (via its wrapper) and thickness (via the prop).

**Third calendar-day state added (2026-08-09), per explicit user direction: "a soft miss on a day that is already NOT counting towards a streak is neither a hard miss (X) or a streak connecting pass (-), its just an empty day."** Until now every empty day was binary — a hard miss or a soft skip (connecting dash) — via `canDayBreakStreak`. That meant a genuinely dead task (no live streak, hasn't been touched in weeks/months) still painted an unbroken wall of X's for every single missed day since its last real activity, which reads as far more alarming than warranted: only the *moment* a streak actually broke is meaningful; the empty time since then isn't threatening anything anymore.

`canDayBreakStreak` (boolean) was replaced with `getDayStreakState(task, date, chains): 'connected' | 'hardMiss' | 'softMiss'` in `app/utils/reports.ts`:
- `daily`/`specific_days_of_week`: a non-due day is still always `'connected'`, unchanged. A *due* day is `'hardMiss'` only if it's the very first due day strictly after the most recent chain's `endDate` (a new `isFirstDueDayAfter` helper walks forward from that chain's end and returns `false` the moment it finds an earlier due day, so at most one due day per broken chain ever qualifies) — every due-day miss after that first one, or any due-day miss when there's no prior chain at all (a task that's never had a real streak to break), is `'softMiss'` instead.
- `days_per_week`/`days_per_month`: unchanged for the `'connected'` case (inside a chain's span, or the current period's live pending tail). For everything else, a new `lastChainBefore` helper finds the most recent already-closed chain preceding this day; if this day still falls within *that same period* (`getPeriodBounds(lastChain.endDate, unit).end`) — i.e., it's still part of the specific period whose failure actually closed the chain — it's `'hardMiss'`; once past that period, or if there was no prior chain to anchor to at all, it's `'softMiss'`.

Both helpers are file-local (not exported) since nothing outside `getDayStreakState` needs them. `isConnectedDay` (used for the connector-stub feature) was updated to compare against `getDayStreakState(...) === 'connected'` directly rather than negating the old boolean — softMiss and hardMiss both correctly count as "not connected" for stub purposes, but the old `!canDayBreakStreak` would have wrongly folded softMiss into "connected."

Verified against the real demo dataset the same way as the chain-based redesign before it: a truly `expired`/`never_started` task's long-dead stretch now fades to soft-miss almost immediately after its one real hard-miss day, rather than staying a solid wall of X's; a `daily` task with a single genuine break shows exactly one X, a short soft-miss gap, then resumes as a fresh live streak.

**Rendering, per screen** (only the two the user specified got explicit new styling; `TaskCard`'s flipped face reuses an existing style since the result is visually identical):
- `TaskCalendarScreen.tsx`'s Month view ("Task Details Calendar"): soft-miss renders "just a faded day number" — the `isSkipped` branch's `Text` style (renamed `skippedDayNumber` → `fadedDayNumber`, since it's now shared) is reused for `isSoftMissed` too, with no mark or connecting line drawn at all.
- `DashboardCalendarView.tsx`'s Timeline grid: soft-miss renders "a faded empty gray circle" — a new `gridDotSoftMiss` (`opacity: 0.3`) applies *instead of* `gridDotEmpty`, so the dot keeps `gridDot`'s own default grey fill (unlike hard-miss/skip, which go transparent to show their mark) just dimmed, with nothing drawn inside it.
- `TaskCard.tsx`'s flipped calendar face (Home screen task grid): no new style needed — `isMissed`/`isSkipped` are the only two branches with dedicated rendering; a soft-missed day, having neither, already falls through to the existing catch-all `calendarDotFuture` branch (a plain faded dot), which happens to be exactly the right look. The catch-all's comment was updated to note it now deliberately covers both actual future days and soft-missed ones.

**Real bug found and fixed the same day**, reported directly against the demo data ("Read Before Bed", specific_days_of_week: Jul 5/6 completed, Jul 8 missed, but Jul 9 rendered as a connecting dash — "there's no streak at that point"). Root cause: `getDayStreakState`'s `daily`/`specific_days_of_week` branch checked `isDueOnDate` *first* and returned `'connected'` for *any* non-due day unconditionally, before ever checking whether a real chain actually reached it — a leftover from the original (pre-tri-state) rule where "non-due = always safe" was a fine simplification, since back then the only alternative was `'hardMiss'` and a non-due day genuinely never breaks anything either way. Once `'softMiss'` existed as a third option, that same unconditional shortcut became actively wrong: a non-due day sitting right after a real hard-miss (chain already closed, nothing currently connected) still drew a connecting line, implying an active streak that no longer existed.

Fixed by checking chain membership *first*, matching the quota branch's own structure: `if (chains.some(chain => day >= chain.startDate && day <= chain.endDate)) return 'connected'` runs before the due/non-due check now, and only a day that *isn't* covered by any real chain falls through to `!isDueOnDate(...) → 'softMiss'` (rather than the old `'connected'`). This is safe without adding a due-day equivalent of the quota branch's "pending tail" check: due-day chains settle *continuously* (`getDueDayStreakChains` finalizes a segment at every single due-day gate, not once per period), so a currently-live run is already fully reflected in `chains` up through yesterday — there's no "period isn't over yet" ambiguity the way `days_per_week`/`days_per_month` has. Added a direct regression test reproducing the exact reported scenario (`__tests__/utils/reports.test.ts`), plus one confirming a non-due day genuinely *inside* a real chain still correctly connects. Re-verified against the actual "Read Before Bed" demo task via the same throwaway-script method used throughout this feature's development — Jul 9 now reads `softMiss` as expected.

**Follow-up (2026-08-09), a real redesign, not just a tweak, per explicit user direction: "an X should be shown on any non-completed day where it would be REQUIRED for the streak to continue, even if the streak is already expired... days outside the completed days all fail if the quota isn't met because at least SOME of them would be REQUIRED to meet quota."** This directly reverses the "fade to soft miss over time" mechanism from the very first `getDayStreakState` design (the previous two entries above) — that mechanism turned out to be an overcorrection: it was solving for "don't show an endless wall of X's for a dead task," but the user's actual bar is narrower and different — a day that was genuinely *required* stays a hard miss forever, full stop, no softening based on how long ago it happened. `softMiss` still exists, but only for days that were never actually required in the first place, not as a general "time heals all X's" fallback.

**daily/specific_days_of_week**: dropped the `isFirstDueDayAfter`/"only the first miss after a chain" restriction entirely. A missed due day is now unconditionally `'hardMiss'` as long as the task has ever had *any* real chain before it (`lastChainBefore(chains, day)` — still an existence check, not a proximity one); with no prior chain at all (task never actually engaged with), it's still `'softMiss'`, since there's nothing for that day to have been "continuing." A non-due day's connect-vs-soft-miss logic (the chain-membership check from the bug fix above) is unchanged.

**days_per_week/days_per_month**: replaced the whole chain-span-based approach with direct **per-period pass/fail**, since the old approach had its own related bug the user's example exposed: a day chronologically *before* a failing period's own last real completion (but still within that period, and within the *overall* chain span thanks to the "IncludingClose" bonus-day credit that chain length calculations use) was reading as `'connected'` even though its own period had failed — conflating "contributes to this chain's *length* number" with "this specific period actually succeeded." `getDayStreakState` now walks the day's own period (`getPeriodBounds`) and directly counts qualifying days within it via the caller's `completionCounts` map (bounded to 7 or 31 day-lookups, not a full `completions` array re-scan — kept cheap on purpore, matching the map-based pattern used everywhere else in this codebase): if that count already meets quota, `'connected'` (regardless of whether the day itself is before or after the period's own actual completions); if the period is still the current, not-yet-elapsed one, also `'connected'` (the pending-tail case, now decided purely by "is this period over yet," not gated on `task.stats.currentStreak`/chain history at all — an elapsed-vs-not-elapsed check alone is sufficient and simpler); otherwise (period elapsed, quota not met) `'hardMiss'` for **every** empty day in it, not just a capacity-derived subset — per the user's own reasoning, since any of them individually could have been "the" required day, there's no principled way to single out only some.

One real **asymmetry, kept deliberately**: due-day types still get a "never had any real chain → `softMiss`" exception; quota types do not (an isolated failed first-ever period's own empty days are `hardMiss`, full stop) — the user's quota-side wording had no equivalent carve-out, so none was added. `getDayStreakState`'s signature changed to `(task, date, chains, completionCounts)` (the `completionCounts` param is new, needed for the per-period counting; every call site already had this map built for other purposes, so no new per-screen computation was required, just threading the existing value through) — updated all three calendar screens and `isConnectedDay` (which forwards to `getDayStreakState` for its own "is this empty day connected" check) accordingly. Rewrote the entire `getDayStreakState` test suite in `__tests__/utils/reports.test.ts` around the new behavior (all 99 tests passing) and re-verified against the real demo dataset via the same throwaway-script method used throughout this feature: failed periods/expired streaks now show solid, permanent runs of X, and `softMiss` is correctly confined to non-due days outside any chain and never-engaged-with due days.

New component `app/components/SkippedDayMark.tsx` — a single horizontal dash, hand-drawn via `Line` (same reasoning as `MissedDayMark`: an icon-font glyph carries baseline offset that fights a perfectly centered dot), colored with the task's own `task.color` rather than a neutral tone, so it visually reads as "this didn't touch your streak" rather than "you missed something." All three calendar surfaces now derive `isMissed`/`isSkipped` from `canDayBreakStreak` (an empty day is one or the other, never both) and render `SkippedDayMark` wherever they previously either showed nothing distinct or (bug) showed a hard-miss X:
- `TaskCard.tsx`'s `CardCalendar`: `day.isSkipped` gets its own branch (previously fell through to the same dimmed "future" dot style as an actual future day — now visually distinct from both).
- `TaskCalendarScreen.tsx`'s Month view: same pattern as its existing `isMissed` branch, plus `stateLabel` gained a `'Skipped'` case for the day-cell's spoken accessibility state.
- `DashboardCalendarView.tsx`'s main grid: replaced the old `gridDotNotDue` (a dimmed-but-otherwise-plain grey circle for non-due days) — `gridDotMissed` was renamed `gridDotEmpty` and is now shared by both `isMissed` and `isSkipped` (both want the same transparent-background treatment, showing only their respective mark), with a new `skippedMark` style matching `missedMark`'s `opacity: 0.3` so neither empty-day state visually outranks the other.

**Deliberately unaffected**: `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Year view (`yearDot`) and `DashboardCalendarView`'s per-task mini month grid (`monthGridDot`) — per their own existing documentation, neither ever distinguished a missed day from a future one in the first place (completed/partial/future only), so there's no hard-miss X to demote to a soft-skip there.

**The thin connector line/dash became a thick, full-cell, semi-transparent capsule wrapping the whole streak run, plus a streak-count badge (2026-08-10), per explicit user direction — scoped to `TaskCalendarScreen`'s Month view ("Task Details → Calendar") and `DashboardCalendarView`'s main Timeline grid ("Dashboard → Timeline") only.** `TaskCard`'s own flipped calendar face (Home screen) deliberately keeps its existing thick-short-faded `SkippedDayMark` treatment (see its own 2026-08-09 entry above) — not part of this ask, not touched.

New export in `app/utils/reports.ts`, `buildDayConnectionInfo(task, dates, chains, completionCounts): Map<dateString, DayConnectionInfo>` (`{ isConnectedSelf, isRunStart, isRunEnd, showStreakBadge, badgeValue }`), deliberately separate from `getDayStreakState`/`isConnectedDay` above rather than folded into either — it only adds run-boundary detection and a completed-day count on top of what those already compute; callers still call `getDayStreakState` directly for their own hard-miss/soft-miss cell rendering, unchanged.

- `isRunStart`/`isRunEnd` are computed by checking `isConnectedDay` on the actual neighboring *calendar date* (via `subDays`/`addDays`), not by looking at whether a neighbor happens to be in the caller's own rendered date list — so a run that continues into an adjacent, off-screen month or unloaded Timeline page correctly does *not* get a rounded cap where it's merely clipped by the current view. This is what lets the capsule only round at a streak's true beginning/end, staying flat/square everywhere it continues into a connected neighbor, so adjacent cells' bands visually merge into one continuous shape.
- The streak-count badge lands on the chronologically **last completed day** of a run, which isn't always the run's own rightmost cell — a `days_per_week`/`days_per_month` task's still-open, not-yet-decided current period can extend the connected run past the last real completion (the "pending tail" case `getDayStreakState` already handles). A single forward pass tracks the most recent completed index and a running completed-day count within whatever run is currently open, flushing (placing the badge) exactly when `isRunEnd` fires — this works identically for an already-closed historical run and the still-live one reaching up to today, with no special-casing needed for either. Verified directly against this exact "live streak, badge stays on the last real completion, not on today" scenario in both the unit tests and the demo-data check below.
- No badge on a **1-day run** (`badgeValue`/`showStreakBadge` require `runCompletedCount > 1`) — mirrors `getRecentStreaks`' own existing "a single completed day isn't really a streak" filter, and avoids a "1" badge on every isolated completion, which would've been noisy rather than notable. A day that's individually completed but stranded between two hard-misses (e.g. the one day of a `days_per_week` period that otherwise failed its own quota) is still `isConnectedSelf` (a completed day is always self-connected) but reads as its own unbadged 1-day island — both `isRunStart` and `isRunEnd` true, no badge.
- The badge's `value` is a **direct count of completed days within the run**, not a lookup against a matching `StreakChain`'s own `.length` — deliberate, not an oversight: `getQuotaStreakChains`'s `chainMetSegments` walk treats a not-yet-elapsed current period that hasn't met quota *yet* as a "failing" segment that closes the chain early (since it only knows `met: completedInPeriod.length >= quota` per segment, with no separate "still pending" state) — which would under-report or mis-place the badge for a currently-live, not-yet-decided quota streak. Counting completed days directly within the same connected run that's already being used for the capsule's own boundaries sidesteps this entirely — the number always matches exactly what's visually connected on screen, and never depends on `chains`' own "already decided" semantics being exactly in sync with the live pending-period logic.

New shared component `app/components/StreakCountBadge.tsx` (`{ value, color, size?, style? }`) — a small, fully opaque, white-bordered pill, deliberately opaque (unlike the semi-transparent capsule it sits on top of) so the number stays legible regardless of how many translucent layers or solid dots are behind it.

Per-screen wiring, both via a new `useMemo`'d `buildDayConnectionInfo` call keyed on the same `task`/`streakChains`/`completionCounts` the screen already computes for other purposes (no new per-cell cost, matching the existing memoization pattern throughout this file):
- **`TaskCalendarScreen.tsx`'s Month view**: `hasLeftConnector`/`hasRightConnector`/`leftConnectorOverlay`/`rightConnectorOverlay` (half-cell stub) and the `isSkipped` full-cell `skippedLineOverlay` were all replaced by one full-`day`-cell `connectorBand` (opacity 0.22, `task.color`), rounded via `borderRadius: 999` (large enough to always resolve to a true half-circle regardless of the cell's actual screen-width-dependent size) on whichever side(s) `isRunStart`/`isRunEnd` mark true. The `SkippedDayMark` import and its two overlay styles were removed from this file entirely (component itself is untouched — `TaskCard.tsx`'s `CardCalendar` still uses it for its own distinct treatment).
- **`DashboardCalendarView.tsx`'s Timeline grid**: same `connectorBand` pattern, but with left/right **reversed** from the Month view's mapping — this grid renders newest-first left-to-right (today at index 0, `subDays(today, i)` increasing rightward), the opposite of normal calendar chronology, so a run's chronological start (`isRunStart`, the *older* edge) renders on the right and its end (`isRunEnd`, the *more recent* edge) renders on the left.
