Reads `pendingCelebrations[0]` + `achievementCelebrationsEnabled`; when disabled, an effect calls `dismissCurrentCelebration()` immediately whenever a pending item appears, without ever rendering — drains the queue silently rather than backlogging popups for if the setting gets re-enabled later. When enabled: a **top-anchored** card (`top: insets.top + 8`, its own host at the same `zIndex: 1000` `ToastBanner` uses but positioned opposite it) reusing `ToastBanner`'s exact interaction shape for consistency — the identical `Gesture.Pan()` swipe-to-dismiss thresholds (`SWIPE_DISMISS_DISTANCE: 80`, `SWIPE_DISMISS_VELOCITY: 800`), the same drag-follows-finger + fade `useAnimatedStyle`, `accessibilityLiveRegion="polite"` + `accessibilityRole="alert"`. Two deliberate departures from `ToastBanner`, both confirmed by directly reading it first rather than assuming: entrance uses `Easing.out(Easing.back(1.6))` (the same slight-overshoot "pop" `OnboardingHint`'s own bubble uses) instead of Toast's plain ease-out, for a touch more "look what you earned"; and the entrance `translateY` starts *negative* (dropping in from above) rather than Toast's positive (rising from below), matching the flipped anchor. Auto-dismisses after 3.5s (Toast's own default is 8s — there's no action to read or tap here, just a moment to notice). **Deliberately doesn't reuse `ParticleSystem`** — that component is bespoke-tuned for one specific context (a small 64×28 badge, a fire-only red/orange palette, semantically wired to "streak is on fire" via `useFireCelebration`); reusing it here would need re-tuning for a bigger surface and would misleadingly imply every achievement (e.g. `perfect-day`) is a streak/fire event. A plain Reanimated pop-in is the more minimal, better fit.

**Trophy Case, `app/screens/TrophiesScreen.tsx` + thin `app/trophies.tsx` route re-export** (matching `about.tsx`'s `export default AboutScreen` pattern) — mirrors `ArchivedTasksScreen.tsx`'s visual language closely (same header/row/card shape): a `FlatList` of `achievements` sorted newest-first, each row icon-in-a-circle (task color if present, gold for the global `perfect-day`) + title + description + earned date, empty state (`trophy-outline` + "No trophies yet — keep completing your habits to start earning them"). Read-only, no per-row navigation.

**Two deliberate scope decisions, stated explicitly rather than left to be discovered as bugs later:**
- **No revocation on Undo.** `undoCompleteTask`/`uncompleteTask` won't retract an already-earned achievement — the trophy stays in history even if the triggering completion is later undone. Matches this codebase's general bias toward simplicity for this class of edge case (see e.g. the free-tier task cap's own "considered and not pursued" notes above), and matches how collectibles conventionally work.
- **No historical backfill.** Existing tasks with an already-long streak/completion history don't retroactively earn achievements the moment this shipped — a mutation-time before/after comparison has nothing to diff against for history that predates the feature. Only future threshold-crossings fire.

**Tests**: `__tests__/utils/achievements.test.ts`, 21 cases following `streaks.test.ts`'s exact house style (local fixture builders, plain `describe`/`it`) — every kind's threshold-crossing and dedup behavior, the `new-best-streak` spam regression, `perfect-day`'s due/archived/dedup/backfill-date handling, and a multi-achievement-in-one-call batch case.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (148/148, up from 127 with this feature's 21 new tests) across every new/touched file. `AchievementStinger.tsx` has one accepted `react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` warning on its entrance-animation effect — confirmed `ToastBanner.tsx` has the identical warning on the identical pattern (an intentionally-empty dependency array paired with `key`-based remounting per new item), so this isn't a new inconsistency. Not yet confirmed on-device, per the project's standing convention — worth prioritizing: the full detection matrix end-to-end (new task climbing to day 2/3, breaking and beating a real record, crossing streak/completion tiers, a genuinely perfect day, a lapsed-then-revived streak), the Stinger and the completion toast appearing together without collision, the celebrations toggle actually gating the popup while the Trophy Case stays complete regardless, and a cold-launch-then-quick-background/foreground check for the hydration-race fix.

**Follow-up (2026-08-10): the compact toast-style Stinger became a full-screen cinematic celebration; the Trophy Case became a badge grid with locked/progress cards; a test dataset was built to exercise every kind, per explicit user direction ("full screen cinematic animation, with confetti/particles... more dressed up emblem and sequential reveal animation, along with prominent animated text counter... more badge like rendering... in a card layout... show locked/unachieved trophies with progress... create a test data set").**

**`AchievementStinger.tsx` rewritten as a full-screen takeover**, no longer a `ToastBanner`-style banner — since it now covers the whole screen, it naturally supersedes the bottom completion toast while showing (superseding the earlier "top vs. bottom anchoring" fix, which existed specifically to let the two coexist). A sequential JS-`setTimeout`-driven reveal (not Reanimated's own animation-finished callbacks, per this codebase's established distrust of those for real control flow — see the `TaskCard` completion-pop history above) plays out in beats: a "dressed up" emblem (a `task.color`/gold circular badge with a soft glow behind it and six slowly, continuously rotating translucent-gold spokes through its center, `ZoomIn.springify()` pop-in) at 150ms, title at 750ms, description at 950ms, then — "where applicable" — an `AnimatedCounter` at 1150ms: a plain `requestAnimationFrame` + `setState` count-up from 0 to `achievement.value` over 900ms (deliberately not a Reanimated-worklet-to-JS bridge — there's no cheaper way to animate a *text* value than re-rendering it, so there's nothing to gain from the UI thread here). The counter is skipped only for `comeback`, whose `value` is trivially 1 (the moment of revival, not a number worth counting up to) — every other kind shows it. A "Tap anywhere to continue" hint + explicit "Awesome!" pill both appear last; the whole screen is one `Pressable` (tap anywhere dismisses), backed by `expo-blur`'s `BlurView` (a genuinely new dependency-in-use — `expo-blur` was already installed but unused anywhere in the app until now) for a frosted-glass backdrop over whatever screen was open underneath, plus a dark tint on top for text contrast. Auto-dismisses ~3.2s after the last element reveals (~4.65–5.55s total depending on whether the counter played). New sibling component `app/components/Confetti.tsx` — a full-screen falling/tumbling/rotating burst (60 pieces, a lively multi-color palette deliberately distinct from `ParticleSystem`'s fire-only red/orange/yellow language, since confetti is a different celebratory register), built fresh rather than reused/repurposed from `ParticleSystem` (rise-and-swirl embers vs. fall-and-tumble confetti are different enough motion models that bending one config shape to cover both wasn't worth it) but following the same per-piece Reanimated component pattern (a `useMemo`'d config array, `useAnimatedStyle` per piece, mount-only `useEffect`).

**Trophy Case became a responsive grid of badge-style cards, one per achievement *kind*** (not per task+kind — a per-task grid would mean up to 12 × active-task-count cards, most locked/irrelevant to any given task; one card per kind, unlocked the moment *any* task has earned it, keeps the screen a fixed, scannable size regardless of task count). Same 2/3/4-column responsive breakpoints `HomeScreen`'s own task grid already uses. Unlocked cards show a full-opacity `task.color`/gold badge circle + the most recent earn's date (or "Earned N×" if more than one instance exists — genuinely possible for per-task kinds, since two *different* tasks can each independently earn their own copy of e.g. `streak-10`). Locked cards show a dimmed badge with a small lock-icon overlay, plus — where a meaningful metric exists — a live progress bar.

New pure/testable exports in `achievements.ts` power this: `getAchievementCardStatus(kind, achievements, activeTasks, today?)` and `getAllAchievementCardStatuses(...)`, plus `ACHIEVEMENT_KIND_ORDER` (the fixed catalog-order the grid renders in). Progress is computed differently per kind family, all live-derived from the current task list rather than stored:
- **Fixed-threshold kinds** (`first-streak`, every `streak-N`/`milestone-N` tier): among tasks that haven't already earned *this specific* kind (a task that's already earned `streak-10` isn't "in progress" toward it anymore), the one with the highest current value (`currentStreak` for streak/first, `totalCompletions` for milestone) wins the card's progress bar, capped at the target — e.g. "7/10 · Morning Workout".
- **`new-best-streak`**: no fixed target — picks whichever task has the highest `currentStreak / bestStreak` ratio among tasks that haven't already tied or passed their own record, showing progress against *that task's own* `bestStreak` as the target.
- **`comeback`**: no clean numeric fraction fits "will you ever complete it again" — shows a simple `opportunityAvailable` readiness flag instead (true whenever any active task currently has `streakStatus === 'expired'`), rendered as "Ready — revive a lapsed streak" rather than a bar.
- **`perfect-day`**: since it's repeatable across different dates (not truly "one-time" the way its `ONE_TIME_KINDS` membership might suggest — that only dedupes *within* one calendar date), its progress is always live against *today* specifically (`X of Y tasks done today`), independent of whether it's ever been earned on some other day before.

Covered by 12 new tests in `achievements.test.ts` (33 total in that file now) — one per progress-family behavior plus the catalog-order/count invariant.

**A testing-only delete button, added per explicit user mid-task request ("add a delete option to the trophies, this way I can delete and re-trigger to test").** A new `deleteAchievementsByKind(kind)` action on `achievementsStore.ts` wipes every earned instance of one kind (across every task, for per-task kinds) from both `achievements` and `pendingCelebrations`, putting that kind's card straight back to locked so its underlying threshold can be crossed again. Rendered as a small trash-can button in the top-right corner of unlocked cards only (nothing to delete on a locked one) — immediate on tap, no confirm dialog, since this exists purely to speed up iterating on the achievement feature itself, not as a real user-facing destructive flow (unlike Archive/Delete Task, which deliberately kept its `Alert.alert` confirm — see the Toasts policy above). Still gets the same `notificationAsync(Warning)` haptic the app's other destructive taps use.

**Achievements test dataset, `demo-data/generate-achievements-test-data.py` → `streakaholic-achievements-test-data.json`** — a second, purpose-built sibling to the main `generate-demo-data.py`/`streakaholic-demo-data.json`, aimed specifically at exercising all 12 achievement kinds rather than at general realism. Since achievements only ever fire off a real `completeTask` press taken *after* import (never retroactively from imported history — see "No historical backfill" above), every one of its 11 tasks is engineered to sit exactly one action away from crossing one specific kind's threshold: 9 fire on a plain "complete today" tap, one (`New Best Streak Demo`) requires backfilling a single deliberately-left-empty day on the Calendar tab (the *only* way that achievement can ever fire at all — see achievements.ts's own comment on why `bestStreak` tracking `currentStreak` so tightly makes a same-day completion structurally incapable of crossing a prior record, only a backfill-bridged jump can), and `perfect-day` fires automatically as a bonus on whichever task's action happens to complete the set last. Verified — not just hand-derived — via a throwaway `tsx` script driving the real `calculateTaskStats`/`detectCompletionAchievements` against the generated JSON before finalizing (same practice the main demo dataset's own README describes), which caught two real modeling mistakes before they shipped: `New Best Streak Demo`'s first draft also collaterally crossed `streak-10` (fixed by shrinking its numbers so the post-backfill streak lands well clear of the next tier), and `Comeback Demo`'s first draft (a realistic multi-week silent gap) left the task's `streakStatus` decayed all the way to `'never_started'` instead of `'expired'` by the time "today" arrived — the same "more than one trailing missed day buries the real run" mechanic the main demo dataset's own Meditate task works around by using `5x/week` instead of `daily`, documented in that file's own comments; fixed here by narrowing the gap to *exactly* one missed day. `demo-data/README.md` documents the full per-task action checklist, including which taps incidentally also earn a second kind (not bugs — dedup is scoped per-task, so these don't block the dedicated task for that second kind from independently earning its own copy later) and a note that, more than the main dataset, this one should be regenerated close to actual test time since its two trickiest tasks depend on exactly-one-day gaps that drift with time.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, up from 148 with the new progress-status tests) across every new/touched file. Not yet confirmed on-device — worth prioritizing given how much of this is genuinely new (full-screen takeover, `expo-blur` in actual use for the first time, a second falling-particle system, a grid layout with live-computed progress bars): the cinematic sequence's pacing/legibility on a real device, confetti performance, the blur backdrop's look against both themes, and running through the new test dataset's full checklist end-to-end.

**Follow-up (2026-08-10): tapping an unlocked Trophy Case card replays its celebration, per explicit user direction.** A new `queueCelebration(achievement)` action on `achievementsStore.ts` re-pushes an already-earned `Achievement` record straight onto `pendingCelebrations` — no new id/`earnedAt` assigned and `achievements` history left untouched, since nothing new was earned, only re-shown; `AchievementStinger` picks it up exactly like a fresh one (it doesn't distinguish "genuinely new" from "replayed"). `AchievementBadgeCard`'s outer `View` became a `TouchableOpacity` (`disabled` when locked, since there's nothing earned yet to play), calling `onPlay(latest)` with a light `impactAsync` haptic. The existing per-kind delete button still nests inside it without conflict — RN's touch responder system resolves nested `TouchableOpacity`s by letting whichever one is actually touched claim the gesture, so tapping delete doesn't also trigger a replay.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the emblem became a fully-dressed, continuously-animated medallion; the Stinger's own pacing and layout were both fixed, per explicit user direction ("multiple concentric circles... get rid of the starburst lines... overlay lighting sheen effect... thin circles pulsing outwards... the entire badge should gently bob... sparkly particles... the timing... should be much more spaced out... currently the layout shifts every time a new element appears, it should not... the confetti should be colorized based on the achievement color").**

**New `app/components/TrophyBadge.tsx`** replaces the earlier rotating-ray emblem entirely — a fully self-contained animated unit (everything starts on mount, loops for as long as it stays mounted, no props control timing beyond the `icon`/`color` it's given):
- **Concentric frame**: three static, non-animated rings around the badge (`FRAME_RINGS`, increasing size/decreasing opacity) — purely decorative, giving the badge a "medallion" feel rather than floating alone. The starburst rays (six rotating translucent-gold spokes, from the previous design) are gone entirely.
- **Pulsing sonar rings**: a `PulseRing` sub-component, three instances (`PULSE_POOL`) staggered a second apart (`PULSE_STAGGER`) via `withDelay`, each looping independently (`withRepeat(withTiming(1, {duration: PULSE_DURATION, easing: Easing.out(Easing.cubic)}), -1, false)`). A single `progress` value per ring drives `width`/`height`/`borderRadius` (growing), `borderWidth` (shrinking, `PULSE_MAX_BORDER` → `PULSE_MIN_BORDER`), and `opacity` (fading) all together — deliberately *not* a `transform: scale` on a bordered box, since scaling a box scales its border proportionally too (thicker as it grows), the opposite of the "starts thick, thins out as it expands" the user asked for. The shared `Easing.out(Easing.cubic)` curve on the single driving `progress` value is what gives the whole ring both its fast/big initial growth *and* its slow/subtle finish "for free," without separately tuning size and speed.
- **Lighting sheen**: a translucent white diagonal bar (`sheen`) inside the badge's own circle, clipped via `overflow: 'hidden'` and swept across via `translateX` on a `withRepeat(withSequence(sweep, pause), -1)` loop. The badge itself was split into two nested views (`badgeShadowWrap`, carrying the shadow/elevation; `badgeClip`, carrying `overflow: 'hidden'` + the icon + the sheen) specifically because applying `overflow: 'hidden'` and a shadow to the *same* view clips the shadow away on Android (a known RN quirk this codebase has hit and worked around before — see `TaskProgressIcon`'s own history).
- **Bob**: the *entire* stack (frame + glow + pulse rings + badge) rides on one outer `Reanimated.View` whose `translateY` oscillates via `withRepeat(withTiming(1, {duration: BOB_DURATION, easing: Easing.inOut(Easing.sin)}), -1, true)` (the `reverse: true` argument is what makes a single one-way `withTiming` call read as a smooth back-and-forth cycle, without a manual `withSequence`).
- **Periodic sparkle particles**: reuses `ParticleSystem` (not a new particle implementation) with a sparse, twinkly override (`SPARKLE_CONFIG` — small size, white/gold `colorPairs`, no swirl, short life, `spawnWindow: 500`) and the component's *default* radial spawn (no `spawnArea` override) with `distance` set to just outside the badge's own radius, so sparkles scatter in a loose ring around it rather than on top of it. "Periodically" (not just once) reuses the same `onComplete`-driven remount pattern `TaskCard`'s own fire-celebration particles use elsewhere in this codebase: `handleSparkleComplete` bumps a `sparkleKey` state after a `SPARKLE_PAUSE` delay, and `<ParticleSystem key={sparkleKey} .../>` remounting on that key change is what triggers a fresh burst — no separate `setInterval` needed.

**Stinger timing widened substantially** — `EMBLEM_DELAY`/`TITLE_DELAY`/`DESCRIPTION_DELAY`/`COUNTER_DELAY` all pushed out (200 → 1600 → 2500 → 3400ms, up from 150/750/950/1150), `COUNTER_DURATION` 900 → 1200ms, `HINT_PAUSE_AFTER_CONTENT` 300 → 800ms, `AUTO_DISMISS_PAUSE` unchanged at 3200 → 3500ms — total sequence now runs ~7.7–8.9s (up from ~4.65–5.55s) depending on whether the counter plays, giving each beat real room to land rather than crowding into the first ~2 seconds.

**The layout-shift bug — a real, reported bug, not a tuning issue.** Every element (`title`, `description`, counter, hint) used to be *conditionally mounted* (`{showTitle && <Reanimated.View entering={...}>...}`) inside a `justifyContent: 'center'` container — each new element mounting added a new flex child, growing the stack's total height, which a centered container re-centers as a whole, visibly shifting every *already-revealed* element up (or down) each time a later one appeared. Fixed with two changes together (neither alone was sufficient): the outer `content` container dropped `justifyContent: 'center'` in favor of top-anchoring (`paddingTop: insets.top + 48`, badge appears near the top, everything else stacks below it, matching the explicit ask); and a new `RevealSlot` component wraps every piece with a **fixed `minHeight`, always mounted** regardless of its own reveal state — only the *content inside* the slot fades/drifts in (`opacity`/`translateY` on a per-slot `useSharedValue`, animated via a `useEffect` keyed on that slot's own `revealed` boolean, replacing the earlier per-element `entering` prop approach, which only fires on genuine mount and therefore couldn't apply here now that everything mounts immediately). Since every slot's layout footprint is reserved from the very first frame, the container's total height — and therefore every earlier slot's position — never changes as later slots reveal their content. `AnimatedCounter` gained an `active` prop for the same reason: it's now mounted immediately (inside its always-present slot) rather than only once `showCounter` flips true, so its own count-up `requestAnimationFrame` loop needed an explicit gate to stop it from silently finishing in the background long before its slot ever becomes visible.

**Confetti colorized by achievement**, per explicit direction. `Confetti.tsx` gained a `baseColor` prop (defaults to gold); a new `buildPalette(baseColor)` derives a 5-color set via `tinycolor` (the base color itself, +15/-12/+30 lightness variants, plus a flat white fleck for sparkle/contrast) instead of the previous fixed 7-color rainbow — so the burst now visually matches whichever task (or gold, for the task-less `perfect-day`) is being celebrated. `AchievementStinger` passes `baseColor={accentColor}` (the same `task.color ?? GOLD` already driving the badge/rings/sheen) through to `<Confetti>`.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure new component + rendering/timing changes, no tested `utils/` logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth prioritizing given the amount of continuous/looping animation now running simultaneously (bob + 3 pulse rings + sheen sweep + periodic sparkle bursts, all on top of the confetti and the reveal sequence itself): actual frame performance on a real Android device, whether the widened ~8s total duration feels right in practice, and whether `RevealSlot`'s fixed `minHeight` values (76/56/110/90) are generous enough for every achievement's actual title/description text without clipping or, conversely, leaving an awkwardly large gap for short ones.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the badge now drops in with a bounce, gained an accent-colored icon + ribbon banner, and the Stinger's title/counter duplication was fixed by merging them, per explicit user direction ("drop in like it's falling from the camera... scale ease out bounce effect... the icon larger, use an accent color rather than just white, add a ribbon like text banner... the title and number counter are kind of duplicative, combine them").**

**Drop-in bounce (`TrophyBadge.tsx`)**: the badge+ribbon group now starts at `DROP_START_SCALE` (2.4x) and `DROP_START_ROTATION` (28°) and animates to scale 1/rotation 0 via a single `drop` shared value driven by `withTiming(1, {duration: DROP_DURATION, easing: Easing.out(Easing.bounce)})` — one continuous curve for the whole "falls from the camera, tumbling, then bounces to rest" motion (not a snap-then-settle two-phase like `TaskProgressIcon`'s own completion pop) since `Easing.out(Easing.bounce)` already produces exactly the decaying-oscillation approach-with-bounces shape asked for. Scale and rotation are interpolated off the *same* progress value so they bottom out in sync, rather than two independently-timed animations that could drift apart.

**Badge face redesigned (`TrophyBadge.tsx`)**: previously the badge circle itself was filled with the accent color and the icon was plain white on top of it — dressing it up with an *accent-colored* icon on that same accent-colored circle would have been invisible regardless of which color the achievement happened to use. Fixed by flipping which part carries the accent: `badgeClip`'s fill is now plain white with an accent-colored border (tying it back to the accent-colored frame/pulse rings around it), and the icon (`size` 56 → 66) is colored with the accent directly — always legible against white regardless of the accent's own hue. A new **ribbon banner** (`ribbonRow`/`ribbonBar`/`ribbonTailLeft`/`ribbonTailRight`) drapes across the badge's lower third like a medal's engraved tail: a short all-caps label (`getRibbonText` in `AchievementStinger.tsx` — "10 DAYS", "100 DONE", "7 TASKS", absent for `comeback`) in a colored bar with two small triangular tails, built via the classic border-triangle trick (three of a zero-size box's four borders transparent, the fourth colored) rather than an SVG path. The ribbon is a static, permanent label (always shows the final value immediately) — only the Stinger's own inline title number (below) animates; a medal's engraving doesn't count up.

**Title/counter duplication fixed by merging them (`AchievementStinger.tsx`)**: previously the title text already spelled out the number in plain static words (e.g. `streak-10`'s title, `"10-Day Streak!"`) *and* a separate giant counter block below animated the same number a second time — genuinely redundant. Fixed by introducing `TITLE_PARTS: Partial<Record<AchievementKind, {prefix, suffix}>>` (kept local to this file, deliberately not added to `achievements.ts`'s shared `ACHIEVEMENT_META`, since `title` there is still needed elsewhere as a plain string — `TrophiesScreen`'s cards, this same file's own accessibility label) — for every kind with a `TITLE_PARTS` entry, the title now renders as `{prefix}<AnimatedNumber>{suffix}`, with the number itself styled bigger/bolder/gold (`titleNumber`, fontSize 42 vs. the surrounding text's 26) via RN's nested-`Text` rich-text support, and the count-up (`useCountUp`, an extracted hook replacing the old standalone `AnimatedCounter` component) drives just that inline segment. `new-best-streak` and `perfect-day` gained numbers in their titles for the first time in this pass too ("New Best: 24 Days!", "Perfect Day: 7 Tasks!") — previously their titles carried no number at all while the (now-removed) separate counter block still showed one, which was a milder version of the same duplication-adjacent inconsistency. `comeback` (no `TITLE_PARTS` entry, no counter) falls back to plain `meta.title` unchanged. The now-single-phase title (no more separate counter reveal phase) also let the reveal timeline simplify: `COUNTER_DELAY` and the `showsCounter`-branching hint-delay computation are gone, replaced by `DESCRIPTION_DELAY` (2900ms, positioned so the ~1200ms count-up starting at `TITLE_DELAY` finishes before it) feeding directly into the hint delay.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth prioritizing: the bounce's actual feel/timing (`DROP_DURATION` 1000ms was picked by judgment, not measured against a reference), whether the ribbon's border-triangle tails render cleanly across Android's various renderers, and whether the inline mixed-font-size title (26/42) reads well rather than looking visually unbalanced for longer prefix/suffix combinations.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): `AchievementStinger` renamed to `AchievementCelebration`, and auto-dismiss removed entirely, per explicit user direction ("don't make the stinger automatically close. I know that's how stingers usually work, maybe a different name is appropriate").** A "stinger" — by convention a short sting that plays and vanishes on its own — was no longer an accurate name for a full-screen moment meant to be sat with on the user's own schedule, not timed out from under them. The new name matches vocabulary the store already used before this rename (`pendingCelebrations`, `dismissCurrentCelebration`, `queueCelebration` — "celebration," not "stinger," was already this feature's own internal word for the concept).

`app/components/AchievementStinger.tsx` → `app/components/AchievementCelebration.tsx` (component `AchievementStinger` → `AchievementCelebration`, internal `StingerContent` → `CelebrationContent`); `app/_layout.tsx`'s import/mount updated to match. `AUTO_DISMISS_PAUSE` and its driving `setTimeout` were deleted outright (not just lengthened) — the screen now stays up indefinitely until the user dismisses it themselves via the existing tap-anywhere `Pressable` (the "Awesome!" button was already just a visual affordance inside that same Pressable, not a separate handler, so no new dismiss path was needed). Every other in-code comment referencing the old name (`settingsStore.ts`, `achievementsStore.ts`, `achievements.ts`, `TrophiesScreen.tsx`, `TrophyBadge.tsx`, `Confetti.tsx`) was updated to the new one so a future reader grepping for the current component name finds every reference. `TODO.md`'s original completion-checklist entry and `demo-data/README.md`'s casual "congratulation stinger" phrasing were deliberately left as-is (consistent with how this file's own historical prose is never retroactively rewritten when something later changes — see the many "Follow-up" entries throughout this doc instead of edited originals).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected) and a repo-wide grep confirming zero remaining code-level `Stinger` references outside one deliberate explanatory comment in the new file itself. Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the badge drop-in bounce was actually broken, not just untuned — fixed (`TrophyBadge.tsx`).** Reported directly by the user as "wonky" against the intended effect ("falling from towards the camera to the surface, bouncing against the surface until it comes to rest"). Two real bugs, both in the original implementation from the "dress up the badge" pass:

1. **The bounce curve was driving `scale`, not position.** A bounce is fundamentally a *vertical motion* (an object approaching a floor, rebounding, approaching again with less energy) — applying that same easing curve to *size* instead just wobbles the badge bigger and smaller with no floor for it to be bouncing against, which is exactly why it read as wonky rather than as a bounce. Fixed by driving `translateY` from the bounce-eased `drop` value (falling from `DROP_START_OFFSET_Y` down to its resting position at 0), with `scale` and `rotation` now *derived from that same progress* rather than animated independently — both settle toward their resting value in lockstep with position, so a rebound (position ticking back up) also makes the badge a touch bigger and more rotated at the same moment, reading as one coherent physical motion (further from the "surface" = momentarily closer to camera again, which is also a nice reinforcement of the falling-toward-camera framing) instead of three separately-timed animations that happened to overlap.
2. **The easing direction was backwards.** `Easing.bounce` (bare) is already Penner's "bounceOut" shape — a smooth initial fall, then decreasing-amplitude bounces clustered near `t=1` as it settles, exactly what "bounces against the surface until it comes to rest" describes. The original code wrapped it in `Easing.out(Easing.bounce)`, and `Easing.out`'s definition (`(t) => 1 - easing(1-t)`, confirmed directly from Reanimated's own source rather than assumed) *flips* an already-out-shaped curve, moving the bounce texture to the *start* and smoothing out the landing — the opposite of the intended effect. Fixed by using `Easing.bounce` directly, unwrapped.

`DROP_DURATION` 1000 → 1200ms (a little more room for the added positional motion to read clearly); new `DROP_START_OFFSET_Y = -120` constant for how far above its resting spot the badge starts.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure animation-math change). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the frame rings/glow/pulse rings now burst outward at the moment of impact instead of appearing on mount, per explicit user direction ("I want the background circles to burst out after the emblem 'hits' the surface").** Previously all of these were inert relative to the fall — the frame rings rendered at their static opacity immediately, the glow was a flat `opacity: 0.3` from frame one, and the pulse rings' own staggered loop started counting from mount, all overlapping the badge's ~1200ms fall with no connection to it. New `IMPACT_DELAY` constant (`Math.round(DROP_DURATION / 2.75)`) marks the moment the badge actually first touches down — derived directly from Penner's bounceOut shape, whose first segment (a smooth, bounce-free rise) spans exactly `1/2.75` of the total duration before the first rebound.

New `BurstRing` component (mirrors `PulseRing`'s per-instance pattern) replaces the frame rings' previous static `View`s: each starts at scale 0/opacity 0 and pops outward via `withDelay(delay, withTiming(1, {duration: BURST_DURATION, easing: Easing.out(Easing.back(1.4))}))` — the `back` easing's slight overshoot is what makes it read as a "burst" rather than a plain fade-in. The three rings are staggered `BURST_RING_STAGGER` (70ms) apart, innermost first, so they read as a ripple expanding outward from the impact point rather than all three popping in simultaneously. The glow gained its own `glow` shared value fading in the same way (opacity only, no scale — a soft ambient glow "popping" would look odd) over the same `BURST_DURATION` starting at `IMPACT_DELAY`; its previous static `opacity: 0.3` was removed since `glowStyle` now owns that value entirely. The pulse rings' own per-instance stagger delay is now `IMPACT_DELAY + i * PULSE_STAGGER` instead of just `i * PULSE_STAGGER`, so the first pulse also begins right at touchdown rather than overlapping the fall.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth checking that `IMPACT_DELAY`'s derived timing actually lines up visually with the badge's own perceived touchdown moment, since it's computed from the easing formula rather than measured against the rendered animation directly.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the burst rings' overshoot became elastic (not `back`) and stopped fading in, and the pulse rings grew considerably, per explicit user direction ("dramatic elastic overshoot, creating a ripple like effect... start at 100% opacity, not fade in... pulsing ring to have a much larger ending radius, and a bit slower of an interval, and start a bit thicker (but keep fade to 1px)").**

**`BurstRing`'s easing switched from `Easing.out(Easing.back(1.4))` to bare `Easing.elastic(1.8)`.** Traced the actual formula rather than guessing (the same discipline the drop-bounce fix above established, after getting `bounce`'s direction wrong once already): `elastic(t) = 1 - cos(t·π/2)³·cos(t·p)` (where `p = bounciness·π`) evaluates to exactly 0 at t=0 and exactly 1 at t=1, with the decaying-amplitude term `cos(t·π/2)³` multiplying an oscillating `cos(t·p)` — meaning it overshoots partway through and settles precisely at 1 by the end, with no `Easing.out(...)` wrapper needed (confirmed by hand-computing sample points: peaks ~1.06 around t≈0.6 before settling). `back`, by contrast, needed that wrapper because its own bare shape pulls *below* zero near t=0 (an anticipation dip) and never overshoots on its own — a different curve family with a different native orientation, which is exactly why checking each easing function's own formula matters rather than assuming a shared "bare = out-shaped" convention across all of them. Raising `bounciness` from elastic's default of 1 to `BURST_ELASTIC_BOUNCINESS = 1.8` both deepens the first overshoot and adds a couple of extra decaying wobbles after it (bounciness only scales the oscillation's *frequency* term, but a higher frequency means the first overshoot peak lands earlier, while the decaying-amplitude envelope has decayed less by then — so the earlier peak is also a taller one), which is what gives the "dramatic... ripple like" quality asked for beyond the default's more modest single bounce. `BURST_DURATION` 500 → 650ms to give the extra oscillation room to actually read rather than feeling crammed. `BURST_RING_STAGGER` 70 → 90ms for a slightly more pronounced ripple between the three rings.

**Opacity no longer fades in.** `BurstRing`'s `opacity` moved out of the animated style entirely, back to a plain static value (`ring.opacity`, always at its full designed strength) set directly in the style array — only `scale` (0 → 1, elastic) is still animated. Before the burst starts, `scale: 0` already makes the ring fully invisible regardless of what opacity is doing, so there was never a *visual* need for the opacity fade in the first place; removing it is what makes the ring read as popping into existence already fully saturated, rather than materializing gradually as it grows.

**Pulse rings enlarged and slowed.** `PULSE_MAX_SIZE` `BADGE_SIZE * 1.7` → `BADGE_SIZE * 3` (now extends well past the outermost frame ring and the `TROPHY_BADGE_STACK_SIZE` bounds into the surrounding blurred backdrop — nothing clips it, so this is intentional, not a bug). `PULSE_STAGGER` 1000 → 1500ms; `PULSE_DURATION` is now expressed as `PULSE_STAGGER * 3` (was a separately-set `3000` that merely happened to equal the same 3x ratio) so the two constants can't drift out of that ratio independently in a future edit. `PULSE_MAX_BORDER` 5 → 8 (thicker at spawn); `PULSE_MIN_BORDER` (1, "fade to 1px") deliberately left unchanged.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the halo got the most dramatic overshoot, the frame rings a gentler one, both gained an ongoing subtle idle pulsation after their burst settles, and a real bug (a ghost pulse ring visible before the badge even lands) got fixed — per explicit user direction ("the big background filled circle, what I am calling the halo circle, [should have] the most elastic and overshoot. The small rings should be more gentle... after the initial pop-in animation the background circles should pulsate... I see a single ring circle behind where the emblem drops... I shouldn't see that").**

**New shared hook, `useBurstThenPulsate(delay, bounciness)`**, factors out a single shared value's full lifecycle -- invisible (scale 0) until `delay`, elastic burst to scale 1, then an infinite, slow, subtle breathing loop (`PULSATE_AMPLITUDE` ±4.5%, `PULSATE_HALF_CYCLE_DURATION` 3000ms each direction, `Easing.inOut(Easing.sin)`) -- used by both `BurstRing` (frame rings, now taking `RING_BOUNCINESS = 1.15`) and a new equivalent treatment for the halo (`HALO_BOUNCINESS = 2.6`, deliberately the highest bounciness in the file). The previous `BURST_ELASTIC_BOUNCINESS` constant (one shared value for everything) was split into these two so the halo could be dialed up independently of the rings being dialed down, per the explicit "halo most dramatic, rings more gentle" direction. Extracting the shared hook (rather than duplicating the sequence three times) is also what makes the "burst then pulsate" *lifecycle* itself consistent across every element that uses it, not just the easing curve.

**The halo (the big filled background circle behind the badge, previously just a flat `opacity` fade-in with no scale motion at all) now bursts and pulsates too.** It's now driven by `useBurstThenPulsate(IMPACT_DELAY, HALO_BOUNCINESS)` exactly like a frame ring — scale-only animation (no separate opacity fade, same reasoning as `BurstRing`: scale 0 already hides it, so a fade would be redundant), with `styles.glow`'s `opacity: 0.3` restored as a plain static value instead of being computed from an animated shared value. The old `glow`/`glowStyle` shared value and its bare `withTiming` fade were removed outright.

**The reported ghost ring was a real bug, not a request for something new.** `PulseRing`'s own `progress` shared value rests at `0` before its delayed `withRepeat` animation starts — but `progress = 0` is that ring's own *most visible* frame by design (full `PULSE_MIN_SIZE`, full `PULSE_MAX_OPACITY`, full `PULSE_MAX_BORDER` — the state a pulse starts from before expanding and fading out). Every pulse ring was therefore sitting there fully rendered and static for its entire pre-first-activation `delay` window, which is exactly the "single ring circle behind where the emblem drops" the user was seeing before the badge had even landed. `BurstRing`/the halo never had this problem since their own resting state (scale 0) is inherently invisible — this was specific to `PulseRing`'s different resting-state design. Fixed with a second shared value, `active` (`0` until `delay`, then an instant `withTiming(1, {duration: 0})` flip), multiplied into `opacity` alongside the existing `PULSE_MAX_OPACITY * (1 - progress.value)` term — gates visibility independently of whatever `progress` itself happens to be doing, so nothing renders at all until each ring's own delay has actually elapsed.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth specifically checking that the surface reads as genuinely clean before the badge lands now (the reported bug's actual fix), and that the halo's higher bounciness doesn't overshoot so far it clips against the frame rings visually.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the halo's overshoot was reduced but the wobble stretched out, since those turned out to be two separate knobs, not one — reported directly ("the halo overshoot is way too much, yet the elasticity is missing. It should wobble for a lot longer").**

Worked out (by tracing `Easing.elastic`'s actual formula, `elastic(t) = 1 - cos(t·π/2)³ · cos(t·p)` where `p = bounciness·π`) that `bounciness` was the wrong knob to have reached for on its own the first time. The first (biggest) overshoot peak lands where `cos(t·p)` first hits -1, i.e. at `t = 1/bounciness` — a *higher* bounciness moves that peak *earlier* in the curve, where the decaying amplitude envelope hasn't decayed as much yet, making the peak both earlier *and* dramatically taller. Concretely: bounciness 1 → roughly a 6% overshoot; the previous `HALO_BOUNCINESS = 2.6` → roughly 56% (hand-computed both, not just estimated) — which is exactly the "way too much" that was reported. But bounciness *also* sets how many wobbles fit into the animation's fixed duration, so simply dialing it back down would have shrunk the overshoot *and* compressed the tail wobbles into an even shorter, less perceptible span — which is what "the elasticity is missing" was actually describing: not too few wobbles existing, but the wobbles that do exist happening too fast to read as anything other than a single snap.

The fix needed both knobs moving in coordination, not one: `HALO_BOUNCINESS` 2.6 → 1.5 (tames the first peak back down to a modest ~12.5%, hand-computed the same way), paired with a new `HALO_BURST_DURATION = 1700` (vs. the rings' shared `BURST_DURATION = 650`) — stretching the now-smaller wobbles out over nearly 3x the real time is what actually makes them read as a long, clearly-perceptible elastic wobble rather than a quick blur. `useBurstThenPulsate` gained a third, optional `duration` parameter (defaulting to `BURST_DURATION`, so the frame rings' own call site didn't need to change) to let the halo use its own value instead of the shared constant. The frame rings' own `RING_BOUNCINESS`/`BURST_DURATION` were left untouched — this report was specifically about the halo.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the halo's burst switched from elastic easing to genuine spring physics for a real multi-swing "rubber band" wobble, and the idle pulsation across the halo/rings was staggered into a ripple, per two rounds of explicit user direction the same day ("it's not bouncy enough... I want it to continue to bounce back and forth... like a rubber band you release and it vibrates back and forth (but not quite that much)"; "the halo/rings pulsation is all in sync... I want them to be staggered, creating more of a ripple effect").**

**Why elastic easing couldn't give a real multi-swing vibration.** Asked directly how the burst was implemented (single elastic animation vs. several sequenced ones) — answered single `withTiming(1, {easing: Easing.elastic(bounciness)})`, with the wobble shape coming entirely from that one easing function. The follow-up request ("continue to bounce back and forth... but not quite that much") exposed a real limit of that approach, not just a tuning gap: `bounciness` is elastic's *only* knob, and it controls overshoot size and oscillation count *together* (per the trace above) — there's no way to get several *visible, moderate* swings out of it, since pushing bounciness high enough to get multiple swings also blows the first peak out to an unusable size. `withSpring`, by contrast, has `damping` and `stiffness` as two more genuinely independent knobs: `stiffness` sets how fast each oscillation cycle runs, `damping` (relative to `stiffness`) sets how many cycles stay visible before the motion dies out — exactly the "several moderate swings, not one wild spike" shape being asked for, and a technique this codebase had already tuned once before for a different feel (`TaskProgressIcon`'s completion pop, `damping: 23, stiffness: 1000` — fast, punchy, quick to die out). New `HALO_SPRING_CONFIG = { damping: 7, stiffness: 90, mass: 1, energyThreshold: 0.001 }` deliberately goes the opposite direction on both knobs from that prior tuning — much lower stiffness (slower, more lingering swings) and a lower damping-to-stiffness ratio (more visible swings before settling) — for a langorous multi-swing wobble instead of a quick punch. Confirmed the actual config field names directly against this Reanimated version's own source (`springUtils.ts`) rather than assuming, the same discipline that caught the `energyThreshold`-not-`restDisplacementThreshold` naming difference documented elsewhere in this file.

**`useBurstThenPulsate` restructured to accept a burst-animation factory** (`() => number`, confirmed via `tsc` that both `withTiming(1, {...})` and `withSpring(1, {...})` satisfy this — Reanimated types a `SharedValue<number>`-targeting animation call as returning a plain `number` on the public surface even though the runtime value is an animation descriptor) instead of a hardcoded `bounciness`/`duration` pair — decouples "what kind of burst" from the shared "then pulsate" lifecycle, so the halo (`withSpring`) and the frame rings (still plain `Easing.elastic(RING_BOUNCINESS)`, unchanged — no complaint was raised about the rings' own gentler pop) can use genuinely different animation primitives through the same hook. `HALO_BOUNCINESS`/`HALO_BURST_DURATION` (the previous elastic-tuning constants) were removed outright, replaced by `HALO_SPRING_CONFIG`.

**Pulsation staggered into a ripple.** The idle breathing loop previously started right after each element's own burst settled, with no *extra* offset — since the burst-phase stagger between elements is only tens of milliseconds (negligible against the pulsate loop's 6000ms full cycle), every element's breathing was effectively in lockstep, reported directly as "all in sync." `useBurstThenPulsate` gained a `pulsateDelay` parameter — an *additional* `withDelay` wrapping just the `withRepeat` pulsate step, applied after the burst animation completes rather than folded into the overall `delay` (which still only gates the burst's own start). New `PULSATE_STAGGER = 500`ms constant, applied in the same halo-then-ring-0/1/2 order the burst ripple itself already uses (halo `pulsateDelay: 0`, leading; each frame ring `(i + 1) * PULSATE_STAGGER`) — continues the same ripple motif into the resting state instead of the ripple stopping the moment everything's landed.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth prioritizing given this is the component's most novel piece yet (first use of `withSpring` anywhere in this file, and the first animation-factory-as-parameter pattern): whether the halo's spring config actually produces a "several visible swings, moderate amplitude" feel rather than either dying out too fast (too close to the old single-overshoot problem) or oscillating too wildly/too long, and whether the 500ms pulsate stagger reads clearly as a ripple at a normal glance rather than just looking like slightly-off-sync elements.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the pulsate loop's "sudden snap" turned out to be a real bug in how it was built, not a tuning issue — reported directly ("the elements suddenly snap. I want them to all be a smooth continuous expand and contract like breathing").**

Root-caused by reading Reanimated's own `withRepeat` source (`repeat.ts`) directly rather than guessing from behavior. With `reverse: false` (what the pulsate loop used), each repeat does **not** continue from wherever the wrapped animation currently sits — `repeat()`'s own code explicitly resets to `animation.startValue`, the value captured once when the *first* iteration began, before replaying the wrapped animation from there. The pulsate loop wrapped a two-leg `withSequence` (up to `1 + AMPLITUDE`, then down to `1 - AMPLITUDE`) — each cycle legitimately *ended* at `1 - AMPLITUDE`, but the next repeat's reset-to-original-start snapped it back up to `1` (where the very first cycle began) before climbing again. That reset-then-climb, repeating every `PULSATE_HALF_CYCLE_DURATION * 2`, was the "sudden snap." `bob` (the badge's existing gentle vertical bob, elsewhere in this same file) never had this problem because it wraps a single `withTiming` with `reverse: true` — genuinely different logic in `repeat.ts` where each repeat *does* continue from the current value, just swapping the wrapped animation's own start/end for the next leg.

Fixed by rebuilding pulsate as two fully independent shared values instead of one value with burst-then-pulsate chained via `withSequence`: `burst` (just the pop-in, settles at 1 and sits still forever after — never re-enters an animation of its own) and a new `pulse` value using `bob`'s exact proven pattern (`withRepeat(withTiming(1, {...}), -1, true)`, oscillating smoothly 0↔1 forever with no reset-to-start behavior in play). The two are combined via a new `useDerivedValue(() => burst.value * (1 + (pulse.value - 0.5) * 2 * PULSATE_AMPLITUDE))` — `pulse`'s tiny oscillation only becomes visually meaningful once `burst` has actually reached ~1 (multiplying by a still-near-zero `burst` during the fall/pop-in keeps the perturbation negligible), so the two never needed to be causally sequenced through one value at all, which is what made the snap-prone `withSequence` chaining unnecessary in the first place. `useBurstThenPulsate`'s signature grew a `burstDuration` parameter — since `pulse`'s own start is no longer chained *after* `burst` finishes (they're independent), it needs a wall-clock estimate of when the burst is "done" to know when to start pulsating: exact for the frame rings (`BURST_DURATION`, a fixed elastic-timing duration), an explicit estimate for the halo (`HALO_BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE = 1700`, since a spring has no fixed duration — it runs until its own physics settle, so this is scheduling-only, doesn't drive the spring itself, and doesn't need to be exact: being off by a few hundred ms only shifts *when* pulsation visibly begins, not whether anything glitches).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — this was a genuine architecture fix (not just constant-tuning), so worth confirming carefully: no snap anywhere in the pulsate cycle now, the breathing reads as continuous expand/contract, and the ripple stagger between elements is still visible with the new independent-value structure.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): pulsate retimed, per explicit user direction once the snap fix confirmed smooth ("the overall cycle a little faster, and the stagger/cycle difference of the elements a little more spaced apart").** `PULSATE_HALF_CYCLE_DURATION` 3000 → 2300ms (full breathe cycle ~4600ms, down from 6000ms); `PULSATE_STAGGER` 500 → 800ms (the ripple gap between halo/ring-0/ring-1/ring-2). Pure constant tuning on top of the now-fixed independent-value architecture — no structural change. Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected).

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the halo's spring re-tuned for several visible wobbles instead of one, per explicit user direction ("about 1 overshoot, 1 undershoot, then it's resting. I want there to be at least 3 or 4... very wobbly. The whole thing probably needs to take longer").** The original `HALO_SPRING_CONFIG` (`damping: 7, stiffness: 90`) has a damping ratio `ζ = damping/(2·√(stiffness·mass)) ≈ 0.37` — coincidentally close to `TaskProgressIcon`'s own already-documented completion-pop ratio (`damping: 23, stiffness: 1000`, ζ≈0.38), which is tuned for a quick, punchy *couple* of bounces, not a long wobble — explaining exactly the "1 overshoot, 1 undershoot" that was reported.

Worked the physics out explicitly rather than just nudging numbers: the number of visible oscillation cycles before a spring's amplitude decays to some fraction is `ln(1/fraction) / (2π·ζ)` — depends only on `ζ`, not on `stiffness` (stiffness only sets how *fast* each cycle plays out, i.e. the period, not how many of them survive the decay). So getting several visible cycles genuinely requires a lower `ζ`, not just a slower `stiffness`. But percent overshoot for a step response is `exp(-ζ·π/√(1-ζ²)) × 100%` — a `ζ` low enough to survive 3+ cycles (`ζ≈0.15`) necessarily produces a large first-peak overshoot too (well past 50%), since the same "how underdamped is this" quantity governs both. These aren't independently tunable via a linear spring model — a spring physically can't wobble for a long time without also swinging far on its very first cycle (the same reason a real rubber band's biggest excursion is its first snap-back, not a later one). Documented this directly in the constant's own comment so it doesn't get "fixed" again as if it were a bug, and accepted it here since a bigger first swing is also just an honest match for "like a rubber band you release."

New config: `{ damping: 2.1, stiffness: 50, mass: 1, energyThreshold: 0.0004 }` (ζ≈0.15, down from ≈0.37; `stiffness` also lowered 90→50 for a slower ~0.89s period per cycle, since "a little more of a lingering wobble" was part of the ask too, not just more cycles). `energyThreshold` tightened 0.001 → 0.0004 so more of the longer decay tail actually renders before Reanimated calls it "at rest" (same lesson as `TaskProgressIcon`'s own `energyThreshold` history elsewhere in this file). `HALO_BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE` (the pulsate-scheduling estimate, not physics-driving) bumped 1700 → 3000ms to match the new, considerably longer real settle time (~3 cycles to decay to 5% at the new period ≈ 2.7-3s).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — the overshoot-vs-wobble-count tradeoff above is a real prediction from the physics, not a guess, but worth explicitly confirming the first peak doesn't read as *too* dramatic now that it's meaningfully bigger than before — if so, the fix is a further request, not evidence these numbers are wrong.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): confirmed the predicted tradeoff was real and too extreme, fixed by shrinking the spring's travel distance instead of its physics, per explicit user direction ("it goes to like 200% then shrinks down to so small you can even see it behind the emblem. I want it to be more of a wiggle near the size it will rest at... Note that you do NOT need to have the halo circle start small if that helps you control the elasticity the way I want -- perhaps it starts near final size").**

The previous entry's prediction landed exactly: percent overshoot is a fraction of the spring's own *travel distance* (its step size), not an absolute cap, and the halo was stepping the full `0 -> 1` range — so the same ζ≈0.15 that gives several nice wobbles on a *short* step reads as "ridiculous... balloony" stretched across a full 0-to-1 journey (overshoot to ~1.65+ predicted, ~2x reported — the discrepancy is plausibly the multi-cycle decay pattern compounding beyond the single-peak estimate, not a wrong formula). The user's own proposed fix was exactly right and is what shipped: stop animating the halo's *scale* from 0 at all. `HALO_START_SCALE = 0.8` is the new starting point — the spring now only has to travel `0.8 -> 1`, a step 5x shorter, so the *same* wobble count and speed (damping/stiffness left untouched) now swings roughly ±10% of final size instead of ±65%+.

Since scale-0 was also how the halo stayed invisible before its burst, and 0.8 is far too large a scale to read as "hidden," visibility had to move to a different mechanism: the halo was pulled out into its own new `Halo` component, only **mounted** (not just scale-hidden) once `TrophyBadge`'s new `showHalo` state flips true via a plain `setTimeout(IMPACT_DELAY)` — the same "mount = start" JS-timer pattern already used throughout this codebase (`BurstRing`'s own mount-time effects, `AchievementCelebration`'s reveal sequence) rather than an in-component animated delay. Mounting fresh at IMPACT_DELAY means the spring genuinely starts *at* 0.8 the instant it appears, with no earlier animation to have been running invisibly in the background. `useBurstThenPulsate` gained a fifth parameter, `initialScale` (default `0`, so `BurstRing`'s own call site needed no changes), making `useSharedValue(initialScale)` instead of a hardcoded `0` — a small, generically-useful change now that "the burst doesn't have to start from nothing" is a real, reusable idea, not halo-specific.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — the math here predicts a tight, fast wiggle (~±10% of rest) with the same visible wobble count as before; worth confirming that reads as "like a rubber band" rather than either too subtle now or still too wide.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the frame rings switched from `Easing.elastic` to genuine spring physics too, matching the halo's mechanism, per explicit user direction ("the pop-in splash rings should have the same basic animation profile (elastic, etc) as you did with the halo").**

`BurstRing`'s burst animation changed from `withTiming(1, {duration: BURST_DURATION, easing: Easing.elastic(RING_BOUNCINESS)})` to `withSpring(1, RING_SPRING_CONFIG)` — the same technique now used for the halo, rather than the older easing-curve approach. `RING_SPRING_CONFIG = { damping: 7, stiffness: 90, mass: 1, energyThreshold: 0.001 }` deliberately reuses the *exact* damping/stiffness the halo itself started with (ζ≈0.37) before being asked to get wobblier — not a fresh guess, but a config already validated by direct user feedback as reading like "about 1 overshoot, 1 undershoot, then resting," which is exactly the gentle character rings still need (the "small rings should be more gentle" direction from earlier in this same session stands unchanged; only the *mechanism* — elastic easing vs. spring physics — was asked to match the halo, not the wobble count/amplitude).

Rings deliberately were **not** given the halo's "start near final size" treatment (`HALO_START_SCALE`) — that fix specifically existed to tame the *large* overshoot a *low* damping ratio produces when stepping the full `0 -> 1` range. At ζ≈0.37, the rings' own overshoot is already modest (~29%, not the halo's earlier ~65%+), so there's no equivalent problem to fix, and rings visually "bursting outward from nothing" (a real, established design element — see `FRAME_RINGS`' own comment on reading as a shockwave rippling from the impact point) is a look worth keeping, unlike the halo, which had already been given explicit permission to give up that "grows from invisible" quality. `RING_BOUNCINESS` and the old `BURST_DURATION` (an exact elastic duration) were removed outright, replaced by `RING_BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE = 850` (scheduling-only, same role as `HALO_BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE` — a spring has no fixed duration to hand `useBurstThenPulsate` directly).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the frame rings and halo unified onto one shared spring config, so both wobble for the same length of time, per explicit user direction ("the rings don't seem to wobble as long as the halo circle does, it should wobble just as long").**

The previous fix (rings keeping the halo's *original*, gentler ζ≈0.37 config while the halo moved to a wobblier ζ≈0.15 one) genuinely satisfied "same mechanism, gentler rings" — but a gentler damping ratio also means fewer visible cycles before settling (`ln(1/fraction) / (2π·ζ)`, independent of stiffness), so the rings were correctly gentler *and* shorter-lived, which reads as "doesn't wobble as long" even though nothing was actually broken. Stretching the rings' *stiffness* down instead (to slow their existing ~1.3 cycles into the same real-time span the halo's ~3 cycles fill) was considered and rejected: 1.3 cycles spread across ~3 seconds reads as a slow, sluggish single sway, not a wobble — getting a comparable multi-cycle wobble *duration* genuinely requires a comparable *cycle count*, which needs the same low ζ the halo already uses, not just a slower one.

Given that, the two configs no longer have a reason to differ: `RING_SPRING_CONFIG`/`RING_BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE` and `HALO_SPRING_CONFIG`/`HALO_START_SCALE`/`HALO_BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE` were merged into single shared constants (`BURST_SPRING_CONFIG`, `BURST_START_SCALE`, `BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE`) used by both `BurstRing` and `Halo`. Since the shared config is the halo's own low-ζ one, the rings also picked up its `BURST_START_SCALE = 0.8` "start near final size" treatment (the fix that keeps a low-ζ spring's overshoot tame — see that constant's own comment) — meaning rings switched from "grows outward from scale 0" to "mounts already near-final-size," the same visibility model the halo already uses. This did give up the rings' distinct "bursts from nothing" shockwave read (a real, deliberate change, not an oversight), but that tradeoff followed directly from matching the halo's actual wobble character, not from a separate decision to abandon it.

Mechanically: `BurstRing` lost its `delay` prop (mounting is now the trigger, same as `Halo`, so there's nothing left for an internal delay to do) and gained a fresh `pulsateDelay`-only signature. `TrophyBadge` gained `visibleRingCount` state (0→3, one `setTimeout` per ring at its own `IMPACT_DELAY + i·BURST_RING_STAGGER` moment, mirroring `showHalo`'s own single-timer pattern) driving conditional mounting (`{i < visibleRingCount && <BurstRing .../>}`) in place of the old always-mounted-with-internal-delay approach.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth specifically checking that losing the rings' "grows from nothing" burst doesn't read as a regression now that they pop in already near-full-size like the halo; if the shockwave-from-impact look is missed, that's a real tradeoff to revisit, not evidence this fix is wrong.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): a real content/copy pass on `AchievementCelebration.tsx`, per six explicit points of user direction in one request (task-colored number, ease-in-out count-up, an "unlocked on" date, the task rendered as a badge instead of quoted text, a redesigned title layout that doesn't cram everything into one hyphenated line, and some actual personality in the copy).**

1. **Number color**: `titleNumber`'s hardcoded `GOLD` → the achievement's own `accentColor` (`task.color ?? GOLD`), so the hero number visually ties back to the badge/rings/confetti above it instead of always reading gold regardless of which task it's celebrating.
2. **Count-up easing**: `useCountUp`'s eased curve went from ease-out-cubic (`1 - (1-t)³`, full speed from frame one, decelerating) to a proper ease-in-out-cubic (`t<0.5 ? 4t³ : 1-(-2t+2)³/2`) — starts slow, speeds up through the middle, slows back down into the final value, per direct request ("right now it starts fast and slows down").
3. **Unlocked date**: a new small, deliberately muted (`rgba(255,255,255,0.45)`, 12px) caption — `Unlocked {format(parseISO(achievement.earnedAt), 'MMM d, yyyy')}` — added below the flavor text/task chip. Achievement records already carry `earnedAt` (used elsewhere for Trophy Case sorting/display), so this needed no new data, just surfacing it here too.
4. **Task-as-badge, not quoted text**: new `TaskChip` component — a small pill (task-colored background, the task's own icon + name, white text) — replaces what used to be `"${taskName}"` woven as a literal quoted string inside `ACHIEVEMENT_META`'s shared `describe()` text. Deliberately *not* implemented as genuinely inline text-with-an-icon (RN's inline-non-text-content-within-`Text` support is inconsistent enough across platforms that it wasn't worth the risk) — instead the flavor line (below) stays plain, task-name-free prose, and the chip stands on its own beneath it as clear attribution. Renders nothing at all for `perfect-day` (`achievement.taskName` is undefined there, since it has no single task of its own).
5. **Title layout redesigned into a vertical hero, not one sentence**: replaced the previous `TITLE_PARTS` (prefix/number/suffix inline, e.g. "10-Day Streak!") with `NUMBER_BLOCK_TEXT` — a small uppercase `eyebrow` (celebratory context, e.g. "Streak Milestone", "New Personal Best"), the huge animated number on its own line, and a small uppercase `unit` caption underneath (e.g. "DAY STREAK", "TOTAL COMPLETIONS"). Explicitly *not* trying to reuse the Trophy Case tiles' compact single-line format here — per direct user framing, that format is right for a tile but this screen has the room (and the mandate, "we want to emphasize the number") to let the number stand alone as the clear hero rather than being read as part of a sentence. `comeback` (no meaningful number) still falls back to `meta.title` alone, now styled as `plainTitle` (bumped to 30px, since it's no longer sharing a slot with a number block).
6. **Actual personality in the copy**: new `FLAVOR_TEXT`, one hand-written, lighthearted line per kind (e.g. "Triple digits! Welcome to legendary territory." for `streak-100`, "One thousand days?! You're basically a superhero." for `streak-1000`) — deliberately kept **separate** from `ACHIEVEMENT_META`'s own `describe()` in `achievements.ts`, which stays plain/factual since it's still used by `TrophiesScreen`'s compact card captions and this same screen's own `accessibilityLabel` (screen readers get the factual summary, not the whimsical one — a deliberate split, not an oversight).

`RevealSlot` minHeights adjusted for the new, taller content shapes (title slot 100→150 for the three-line eyebrow/number/unit stack; description slot 56→130 for the flavor line + chip + date). Otherwise the phase timeline (`EMBLEM_DELAY`/`TITLE_DELAY`/`DESCRIPTION_DELAY`/hint) is unchanged — this was a content and layout pass, not a re-timing.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth checking in particular: the new taller title/description slots don't push the hint/button off-screen on a shorter device, the flavor copy's tone actually lands as "witty" rather than corny, and the task chip's `maxWidth: 260` truncation (`numberOfLines={1}`) handles a genuinely long task name gracefully.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the "Awesome!" button removed and the lower half of the screen decluttered, per direct user feedback that the emblem/counter/title landed well but "the rest... it's just all mashed together" ("rethink the presentation and layout of the rest").**

Two changes, both about giving each idea its own room rather than stacking everything at the same tight spacing:

1. **The date moved out of the flavor-text/task-chip cluster and into its own footer, merged with the dismiss hint.** That cluster previously held three visually distinct textures (bold white prose, a colored pill, a tiny muted caption) 10px apart — cramped regardless of what any individual piece looked like. Now `descriptionBlock` holds just two things (the flavor line, then the task chip) at a wider 16px gap, and the date joins the now-buttonless dismissal hint as one small, quiet, `rgba(255,255,255,0.4)` footer line: `"Unlocked {date} · Tap anywhere to continue"`. Both date and dismiss-hint are genuinely tertiary information (when this happened, how to leave) — combining them into one muted footer line matches their actual weight, rather than the date competing for attention inside the more prominent middle block.
2. **The "Awesome!" button removed outright** (`continueButton`/`continueButtonText` styles deleted, not just hidden) — tap-anywhere-on-screen (the same `Pressable` that already wrapped the whole layout) was already fully sufficient to dismiss; the button was a redundant, visually heavy affordance competing with the content above it for attention it didn't need.

Also widened `content`'s own inter-section `gap` (16 → 28) so the major blocks (emblem, hero number, flavor+chip, footer) read as clearly separate ideas rather than one continuous stack — the description block's own *internal* gap (16, between its two remaining children) stays deliberately narrower than this, since elements within one idea should sit closer together than the ideas themselves. `taskChip` picked up a small drop shadow to read as a slightly more polished, standalone badge now that it's one of only two elements in its block instead of competing with a third.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the unlock date made genuinely visible (not just less-tiny), and the lower half's hierarchy actually redesigned rather than re-spaced, per direct follow-up feedback ("I want the unlocked date not quite so subtle. The layout of the other elements still sucks -- thing about spacing, alignment, visual hierarchy").**

The previous pass had already separated the date from the flavor-text/task-chip cluster, but still buried it — merged into one 12px/`rgba(255,255,255,0.4)` sentence with the dismiss hint (`"Unlocked {date} · Tap anywhere to continue"`), two genuinely different kinds of information (a fact worth noticing vs. a disposable UI instruction) flattened into equal visual weight. Split apart and re-leveled:

- **`unlockedLabel`** now reuses the *same small-caps label language* the number block's `eyebrow`/`unitCaption` already established (uppercase, `letterSpacing: 1.2`, `fontWeight: 700`) at `rgba(255,255,255,0.7)` — a deliberate, designed detail rather than a throwaway caption. The caps + letter-spacing carry real legibility weight beyond what the font size alone would suggest, which is what actually answers "not quite so subtle" (the fix wasn't just "make it bigger," it was "give it the same typographic treatment as the other things on this screen that already read as intentional").
- **`dismissHint`** (`"Tap anywhere to continue"`) drops further still — 11px, `rgba(255,255,255,0.35)` — now clearly the single most disposable line on the screen, exactly matching its actual importance (how to leave, vs. the date's "a fact worth noticing").
- A new **`metaDivider`** (a short 40px, 1px, low-opacity rule — not spanning the block's own text width, just a small deliberate mark) sits above both, separating "the celebratory moment" (flavor text + task chip, renamed `momentBlock`) from "the record" (unlock date + dismiss hint, `metaBlock`) as two genuinely distinct ideas rather than one continuous list — the actual "visual hierarchy" fix requested, not just more padding between existing elements.

Reusing an established typographic pattern (rather than inventing a fourth text style) was a deliberate choice — it's also what ties the top-of-screen number block and the bottom-of-screen metadata together as clearly related, rather than every section developing its own unrelated caption style.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — this is the second round of layout feedback on this same section, so worth a genuinely careful on-device look this time rather than assuming another pass will be needed regardless.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): a plain-fact sentence added alongside the whimsical flavor line, stating what condition actually triggered the achievement, with the task rendered genuinely inline (not a separate badge) — per explicit user direction ("I also want some text that clearly describes what the achievement is for... include task name with nice colored/icon rendering, but inline with the text").**

New `TriggerText` component replaces the earlier `TaskChip` badge entirely (deleted, not just hidden) — the task reference moved *into* this new factual sentence as its subject (e.g. "🏃 Morning Workout reached a 10-day streak.") rather than existing as a separate standalone element, since stating the trigger condition already needed to name the task anyway; keeping a redundant badge alongside it would have reintroduced the "mashed together" clutter from two follow-ups ago. `momentBlock` now holds `FLAVOR_TEXT` (the reaction, unchanged) directly above `TriggerText` (the fact) — "reaction, then fact," a deliberate order, with `triggerText` styled smaller/dimmer (`rgba(255,255,255,0.8)`, 15px) than `flavorText` (17px, full white) so the two don't compete for the same visual weight.

**Genuine inline icon-in-text, not a workaround.** The earlier `TaskChip` design deliberately avoided embedding an icon inside running `Text`, on the assumption that RN's inline-non-text-content support is inconsistent. That assumption doesn't actually apply to `MaterialCommunityIcons` specifically: `@expo/vector-icons` implements its icon components as `Text` rendering a single glyph from a custom icon font, not an `Image` or arbitrary `View` — so nesting one inside a parent `Text` is exactly as well-supported as nesting a styled `Text` span, which is already used throughout this file (e.g. the hero number's own inline styling elsewhere). This is why the redesign could deliver literally what was asked (inline icon + colored name within the sentence) rather than another approximation.

New `TRIGGER_SUFFIX` map (local to this file, `Partial<Record<AchievementKind, (value: number) => string>>`) supplies the sentence's tail for every per-task kind with a numeric value (e.g. `'streak-10': v => \` reached a ${v}-day streak.\`` — the task name is always the subject, so only a suffix is needed, no prefix); `'perfect-day'` (no single task) gets its own complete, task-less sentence directly in `TriggerText`; `'comeback'`'s suffix function ignores its `value` argument (a lapsed-streak revival has no meaningful count to restate). Kept deliberately separate from `ACHIEVEMENT_META`'s own shared `describe()` in `achievements.ts` for the same reason `FLAVOR_TEXT` already is — that text is still used by `TrophiesScreen`'s card captions and this screen's own `accessibilityLabel`, neither of which should suddenly start requiring inline-icon rendering support.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device — the inline-icon-in-Text technique, while sound in principle (confirmed via how `@expo/vector-icons` actually implements its components, not assumed), is genuinely new to this codebase and worth a careful first look: baseline alignment between the icon glyph and the surrounding text at these specific font sizes, and whether `momentBlock`'s new `minHeight={130}` comfortably fits both sentences without clipping on a real device.

**TEMPORARY (2026-08-11), testing-only, flagged for removal once the user confirms testing is done — not part of the shipped feature.** Every card in `TrophiesScreen.tsx`'s Trophy Case grid is now tappable, not just unlocked ones, so a *locked* achievement's celebration can be previewed without actually earning it. `buildPreviewAchievement(status)` builds a throwaway `Achievement` from whatever the card's own live progress calculation already found (`status.progress?.taskName`/`taskColor`/`target`), with a `preview-` id and `dedupScope: 'preview'`; `AchievementBadgeCard`'s `handlePlay` passes that (instead of a no-op) to the same `onPlay`/`queueCelebration` path an unlocked card's replay already uses. This is safe to spam — `queueCelebration` only appends to the ephemeral `pendingCelebrations` queue, never to the persisted `achievements` array, so previewing a locked card doesn't fabricate real history. One accepted gap: the preview has no `taskIcon` (`AchievementCardStatus.progress` doesn't carry one), so `AchievementCelebration`'s `TriggerText` renders the task name without its icon for these preview-only plays — real earned achievements are unaffected. Both the `buildPreviewAchievement` block and the `handlePlay`/`TouchableOpacity` changes are marked in-code with a "TESTING ONLY -- REMOVE WHEN TOLD" comment, and there's a standing session task tracking the revert (restore the `unlocked`-only gating on `onPress`/`disabled`/`accessibilityRole`/`accessibilityLabel`, delete `buildPreviewAchievement`) for whenever the user says testing is complete. Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected).

**Content review (2026-08-11), per explicit user direction spelling out the exact expected structure for the celebration screen: "emblem, counter+labels OR title (if no counter is applicable), witty encouraging non-technical comment, technical description that includes inline themed task name if applicable, then unlocked date label, then subtle tap to continue label."** Cross-checked `AchievementCelebration.tsx`'s actual JSX order against this spec — it already matched exactly (`emblemSlot` → `RevealSlot(showTitle)` holding `numberBlock`/`plainTitle` → `RevealSlot(showDescription)`'s `momentBlock` holding `FLAVOR_TEXT` then `TriggerText` → `RevealSlot(showHint)`'s `metaBlock` holding the divider/`unlockedLabel`/`dismissHint`), with consistent spacing (`content`'s 28px inter-section gap vs. `momentBlock`'s tighter 16px within-idea gap) and a deliberately shared small-caps typographic language between `eyebrow`/`unitCaption`/`unlockedLabel`. No structural or layout changes were needed.

Reading the actual generated text for all twelve kinds against the spec did surface two real content bugs, both fixed:
1. **Number formatting inconsistency.** The hero counter (`bigNumber`, via `count.toLocaleString()`) and `TrophyBadge`'s ribbon banner (`getRibbonText`, via `n.toLocaleString()`) both comma-format large values, but `TRIGGER_SUFFIX`'s sentence fragments interpolated `${v}` raw — so `streak-1000`/`milestone-1000` read "reached a 1000-day streak"/"logged its 1000th completion" right after the same screen had just shown "1,000" moments earlier in the hero number. Fixed by switching every `TRIGGER_SUFFIX` function to `v.toLocaleString()`.
2. **A real grammar bug in `perfect-day`'s trigger sentence.** `` `Every task due that day${value ? ` — all ${value} of them` : ''} was completed.` `` mismatches subject/verb once the plural interjection is inserted ("all 5 of them **was** completed"). Rewritten as a single, always-grammatical sentence: `` `All ${value.toLocaleString()} tasks due that day were completed.` `` (falling back to `'Every task due that day was completed.'` on the — currently unreachable in practice, since real detections always set this — case `value` is undefined).
