Every other kind's content was verified individually against the four-part text spec (numberBlock's eyebrow/unit, `FLAVOR_TEXT`'s whimsical non-technical line, `TRIGGER_SUFFIX`'s factual inline-task sentence) and found correct as-is: `comeback` correctly has no `NUMBER_BLOCK_TEXT` entry (falls back to `plainTitle`, matching "title if no counter is applicable" exactly, since a lapsed-streak revival has no natural number to lead with), and `perfect-day`'s `TriggerText` correctly omits any task-colored inline name (it's a global achievement with no single task to attribute).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure copy/formatting fix, no layout or logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth specifically checking the `milestone-1000`/`streak-1000` sentences and the reworded `perfect-day` sentence render as expected with real data.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): a real layout bug in `RevealSlot` fixed, plus a spacing/positioning pass, per four specific gripes ("not enough space between the encouragement text and the technical text... too much space in general between the text elements top to bottom... the unlocked date and tap to continue and divider are left aligned, but everything else is center aligned... not enough space between the unlocked date and the tap to continue text -- tap to continue could be at the very bottom").**

Gripes 1, 3, and 4 all traced back to a single structural bug in `RevealSlot`: its `style` prop (carrying `alignItems`/`gap` for a multi-child slot) was applied to the *outer* wrapper `View`, but the actual flex container holding the revealed children as siblings is the *inner* `Reanimated.View` — which never received it. Two consequences, both invisible for single-child slots (the number block, the plain title) but real for the two multi-child slots:
- **`gap` never applied at all.** The outer View only ever wraps one child (the inner `Reanimated.View`), so a `gap` set on it has no second sibling to apply between; the real siblings (flavorText/TriggerText, and the divider/date/hint row) sat inside the inner view, which never got the gap. `momentBlock`'s "16" and `metaBlock`'s "8" had never actually been rendering — explaining gripe 1 ("they run together") directly.
- **Left-alignment instead of centering**, but only for children without their own explicit width or `textAlign`. The inner `Reanimated.View` defaults to `alignItems: 'stretch'` (never overridden, since `style` landed one level too high) — `flavorText`/`triggerText` happened to look fine anyway since both already set `textAlign: 'center'` on themselves, self-correcting regardless of the container bug. But `metaDivider` (fixed `width: 40`, can't stretch) and `dismissHint`/`unlockedLabel` (no `textAlign` of their own) had nothing to fall back on: a fixed-width child that can't stretch defaults to flex-start under `alignItems: 'stretch'`, and a stretched `Text` still left-aligns its own glyphs internally by default — exactly the left-alignment gripe 3 described, while `unlockedLabel` (coincidentally the widest of the three, so stretching it to itself was a no-op) looked correctly centered by chance, making the other two look wrong by comparison rather than everything looking uniformly broken.

Fixed by moving `style` onto the inner `Reanimated.View` (`style={[style, animatedStyle]}`) and leaving only `minHeight`/centering-the-whole-block on the outer `View` — the real fix all three symptoms needed, not a one-off patch to `metaBlock` alone (`momentBlock` had the identical bug, it just didn't visibly manifest).

With `gap` now genuinely rendering for the first time, `momentBlock`'s value needed real tuning rather than trusting the old (never-actually-shown) "16": bumped to **22**, deliberately wider than `metaBlock`'s **10** (up from 8), since flavor/trigger are two distinct ideas (reaction, then fact) that need clearer separation than the divider/date pairing does.

**Gripe 2 ("too much space in general")** was addressed on two fronts: `content`'s own inter-section gap (emblem → title-or-number → moment → meta) went **28 → 18**, and each `RevealSlot`'s `minHeight` (previously sized generously to guard against layout shift, before this same bug meant actual content wasn't reliably centered within it) was tightened to match real content more closely: title slot 150→130, moment slot 130→115, meta slot 80→40 (the meta slot now holds only the divider + date, see below, so its real content is much shorter than before).

**Gripe 4** ("not enough space between date and hint... could be at the very bottom") was solved by relocating, not just re-spacing: `dismissHint` came out of `metaBlock` entirely and became its own final `RevealSlot` at the bottom of `content`, separated from the divider/date block above it by a new `spacer` style (`{flex: 1}`) — since `content` is already `flex: 1`, this spacer absorbs whatever vertical space is left after every other section renders, pushing the dismiss hint down to the screen's true bottom edge (above `paddingBottom`) regardless of device height, rather than needing an absolute position or an untested `margin: 'auto'`. `unlockedLabel`/`dismissHint` also picked up an explicit `textAlign: 'center'` each, as a second, redundant line of defense against the exact centering bug just fixed (harmless either way, since the real fix already covers it).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure layout fix, no logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device — this is the second layout pass on this same screen's lower section, so worth a genuinely careful look this time: the divider/date now centering correctly, real visible gaps at both the flavor/trigger boundary and around the divider, and the dismiss hint actually landing near the bottom edge rather than looking oddly isolated.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the "too much/not enough" vertical spacing turned out to be one shared value applied to two very differently-sized variants, and the flavor/trigger pair merged into one sentence, per explicit user direction ("the reason I thought the vertical spacing was too much was because on non-counter achievements there's more [space]. Now there's not enough for counter-based ones. Just try your best to make the vertical spacing comfortable... combine the encouragement text and technical text into a single description element. Put the encouragement text sentence after the technical achievement description, all a single inline wrapping center aligned text element").**

**Root cause of the spacing complaint**: the title `RevealSlot`'s `minHeight` was one fixed value shared by both of its variants — the tall `numberBlock` (eyebrow + huge number + unit caption, ~120px of real content) and the much shorter `plainTitle` (a single 30px line, used only by `comeback`). Tuning that one shared value for either variant necessarily broke the other: sized for `numberBlock` (the earlier 150→130), `comeback`'s celebration sat in a lot of unused reserved space, reading as "too much space" — but shrinking it to fix that (last round) then left every counter-based achievement genuinely cramped instead. Since the variant is a constant for an achievement's whole playback (only the reveal *opacity* animates, never the variant itself), there's no anti-layout-shift reason it needs to be one shared number — split into `titleMinHeight = showsNumber ? 140 : 64`, sized to each variant's own real content instead of splitting the difference between two very different heights.

**The merge**: `TriggerText` was renamed `DescriptionText` and now renders one `<Text>` containing both halves in order — the technical trigger sentence (with the task's icon + task-colored bold name still woven inline, unchanged from before) followed by a space and the whimsical `FLAVOR_TEXT` line, e.g. "🔥 Morning Run reached a 10-day streak. Ten days strong. Keep that fire burning!" — rather than two separately-styled, separately-gapped elements. Since it's now one paragraph instead of two visually distinct tiers, the two previous styles (`flavorText` at 17px/600-weight/full-white, `triggerText` at 15px/500-weight/80%-white) collapsed into one `descriptionText` style (16px/600-weight/92%-white — splitting the difference rather than favoring either old tier) so the merged sentence reads as one consistent voice. `momentBlock` (which existed specifically to `gap` the two elements apart) is gone, replaced by a `descriptionBlock` with no `gap` at all, since there's only one child now. The description `RevealSlot`'s own `minHeight` dropped 115→100 to match the single (if sometimes longer-wrapping) paragraph's real footprint.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure copy/layout change, no logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth checking the merged sentence's actual wrap behavior for a long task name (the icon + bold name + full trigger + flavor line combination is meaningfully longer than either half was alone) against the new 100px minHeight, and that both `titleMinHeight` variants (140/64) read as comfortable rather than either still cramped or still loose.

**Same turn, a mid-turn request: the full-screen overlay needed to read as more opaque, and blue if it was a simple change ("reduce see through visual clutter... if you can make it blue the background that'd be amazing, but only if that's a simple thing like a css property").** `styles.tint` (the plain color layer sitting on top of the existing `BlurView`) went from `rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.45)` to `rgba(8, 14, 36, 0.62)` — a one-line `backgroundColor` change, no new layer or technique: higher opacity for less see-through, and a deep navy instead of pure black for the requested blue cast. `BlurView`'s own `intensity`/`tint="dark"` props were left untouched.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): `FLAVOR_TEXT` rewritten across all twelve kinds, per direct user correction that it read as "too technical... redundant to the title/condition" and should instead be "a witty, encouraging, quippy comment, NOT with any explicit technical/condition reference."** The original lines leaned heavily on restating the exact detail the *technical* half of the same merged sentence (see the prior "combine the encouragement text and technical text" follow-up above) already states seconds earlier — "Ten days strong," "25 days in a row," "Half a hundred," "Triple digits," "One thousand days," "Ten check-ins," "A full century of check-ins," "One thousand check-ins," "Every single task, done" all either spelled out the number/unit a second time or paraphrased the exact condition (`new-best-streak`'s "You just beat your own record" restating the achievement's own name; `comeback`'s "Welcome back" echoing its own title verbatim). Once the two halves became one continuous sentence in the prior change, that redundancy became much more obvious/audible than when they were two visually separate elements.

Rewrote every entry to be purely reactive/celebratory with zero reference to the specific mechanic, number, or unit (no "day(s)," "streak," "completion(s)," "check-in(s)," "record," or digits anywhere in the map) — e.g. `streak-100`: "Triple digits! Welcome to legendary territory." → "This is basically a personality trait now."; `milestone-1000`: "One thousand check-ins. Absolutely incredible." → "Truly, deeply, impressively dedicated."; `comeback`: "Welcome back — you never really left." → "Look who's back in action!" (no longer echoing its own title). The file-level comment above the map was updated to state this as an explicit, deliberate constraint for future edits, not just describe what the map currently contains — so a later addition doesn't quietly reintroduce the same redundancy this correction removed. Also fixed a stale comment reference to the old `TriggerText` component name (renamed `DescriptionText` in the prior merge) that this pass happened to sit next to.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure copy change, no logic or layout touched). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): three of the rewritten lines got a second pass for more genuine creativity, and `comeback`'s "you never really left" sentiment was explicitly restored, per direct user feedback ("come up with something more creative for streak-1000, milestone-100 and milestone-1000... I liked the 'you never left' sentiment of comeback, I don't want to lose that").** The prior pass had already removed every technical/numeric reference, but `streak-1000`/`milestone-100`/`milestone-1000` had landed on fairly generic praise ("At this point you're basically unstoppable," "You've clearly got this figured out," "Truly, deeply, impressively dedicated") — not wrong, just not especially memorable for the app's three biggest tiers. Replaced with more imagistic/specific lines: `streak-1000` → "Certified legend. No notes." (a modern, hyperbolic phrase rather than a plain adjective); `milestone-100` → "This is starting to look like a lifestyle."; `milestone-1000` → "Somewhere, a trophy case just got a lot fuller." — a deliberate small wink at the app's own real "Trophy Case" screen (`TrophiesScreen`) rather than a generic trophy metaphor.

Separately, `comeback`'s line had been rewritten last pass to "Look who's back in action!" specifically to stop it echoing its own title ("Welcome Back!") verbatim — but that swap also lost the "you never really left" sentiment the user had liked from the very original copy. Landed on "Like you never left." — keeps that exact sentiment while still not repeating the title's own "Welcome back" wording, satisfying both the earlier no-redundancy correction and this one.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure copy change). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): `BlurView` removed entirely over performance concerns, and the navy tint reverted to plain black, per direct user feedback ("I am concerned about performance so just remove the blur. I also don't know why you made it blue, I just wanted the overlay to be strong enough to properly back drop the congratulations content").** Asked directly beforehand how the blur was actually implemented -- tracing `expo-blur`'s own source (v15.0.8) turned up a real gap worth recording: the package's README states plainly it "only supports iOS... On Android, a plain View with a translucent background will be rendered," confirmed in the native Android code (`ExpoBlurView.kt`) -- a `blurMethod` enum defaults to `NONE` (a flat computed tint color, `TintStyle.kt`'s `toBlurEffect`) unless the caller explicitly opts into `experimentalBlurMethod="dimezisBlurView"`, which enables genuine `RenderEffect`/`RenderScript` blur via a bundled third-party library but is flagged by Expo itself as experimental and "may cause performance and graphical issues." Since this app's real target platform is Android (not iOS), the `<BlurView>` as configured was already only producing a flat tinted layer functionally identical to the plain `tint` View sitting on top of it -- i.e. it was paying for an extra native view + composited layer without actually blurring anything on the platform that matters, before performance was even raised as a concern.

Removed `<BlurView intensity={70} tint="dark" .../>` and its `expo-blur` import outright (not just disabled) -- the `tint` View is now the screen's sole backdrop. Its `backgroundColor` also reverted from the earlier `rgba(8, 14, 36, 0.62)` navy (a color the user never actually asked for -- the prior request was "if you can make it blue... but only if simple," which was read as permission, not a request) to a plain near-opaque black, `rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.92)` -- bumped well past the earlier 0.62 specifically because, with no blur underneath it at all now, this one flat layer is doing the entire job of backdropping the content. `expo-blur` itself was left in `package.json` rather than uninstalled -- this codebase already has a documented precedent of leaving genuinely-unused dependencies installed rather than churning `package.json` for every one (see `about.tsx`'s credits-filtering logic and its own note on `react-native-circular-progress`/`react-native-confetti-cannon`/etc.) -- so this achievement feature's own earlier note about `expo-blur` being "in genuine use for the first time" is now stale; it's back to unused, alongside that same list.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure removal + a color value change). Not yet confirmed on-device — this one's actually a performance-motivated change, so worth specifically checking the celebration screen still feels responsive (one fewer native view + composited layer per mount) and that the plain black backdrop reads as intended without the blur it used to sit on top of.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): asked directly whether black was actually the right call, then a small tweak once the answer landed.** Recommended sticking with a neutral near-black over reintroducing the navy, reasoning that `accentColor` (driving the badge/rings/hero number/confetti) is task-color-driven and therefore arbitrary — a more saturated backdrop risks going muddy against a task whose own color happens to be similar, where pure black sidesteps that entirely. Offered a middle ground the user then asked for: `styles.tint`'s `backgroundColor` went from `rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.92)` to `rgba(5, 8, 16, 0.92)` — barely distinguishable from true black at this opacity, but with a faint cool undertone, splitting the difference between "safe neutral" and "some personality" rather than committing fully to either.

**Same turn, a mid-turn note: "I like the little divider above the date, put it below a well."** The single `metaDivider` above `unlockedLabel` was mirrored with a second one below it, so the date now sits bracketed between two thin rules instead of only introduced by one — `RevealSlot`'s own `minHeight` for this slot bumped 40→48 to fit the added divider.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure styling change). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the hero counter's count-up duration now scales with the achieved value, per explicit user direction ("the shortest should be 1 second, the highest should be 5").** The fixed `NUMBER_COUNT_DURATION = 1200` constant was replaced with `getCountUpDuration(value)` -- a *log*-scaled map, not linear: achievement values span three orders of magnitude in this app (a `first-streak`'s minimum of 2 up to `streak-1000`/`milestone-1000`'s 1,000), and a linear map across that same range would put every value below ~100 within a few hundred ms of the 1s floor, making the scaling essentially invisible for most kinds. Log-scaling instead means the animation's *pace* roughly tracks the number's own order of magnitude: `t = min(1, log10(max(1, value)) / log10(1000))`, then `duration = 1000 + t * 4000`. Concretely: `first-streak` (value 2) ≈1.4s, `streak-10`/`milestone-10` ≈2.3s, `streak-100`/`milestone-100` ≈3.7s, `streak-1000`/`milestone-1000` lands exactly on the 5s ceiling (any value at or above 1,000 -- e.g. an unusually large `new-best-streak` jump -- clamps there too, never exceeding it).

This had one real knock-on effect worth fixing rather than leaving as a latent bug: the reveal timeline's `DESCRIPTION_DELAY` was a fixed constant tuned against the *old* fixed 1200ms duration (`TITLE_DELAY` 1600 + 1200 + a 100ms buffer ≈ 2900) -- with duration now varying up to 5000ms, a high-tier achievement's description would have started revealing while its own hero number was still visibly climbing underneath it. Fixed by making the description's reveal delay conditional: `showsNumber` kinds now compute it as `TITLE_DELAY + countDuration + DESCRIPTION_PAUSE_AFTER_COUNT` (a 300ms settle beat after the count-up's own actual duration, whatever that is for this achievement), while `comeback` (the one kind with no counter, via `plainTitle`) keeps the original fixed `DESCRIPTION_DELAY_NO_COUNTER = 2900` unchanged, since there's no count-up to wait on there.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure timing/math change, no other logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth specifically checking a `streak-1000`/`milestone-1000` celebration (longest count, ~5s) to confirm the description genuinely waits for the number to finish rather than just approximately lining up, and that a small-value celebration (`first-streak`, ~1.4s) doesn't feel unnaturally rushed now that it's the fast end of a real range rather than a flat 1.2s for everything.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the ceiling brought down 5s → 3s, per direct user direction.** `COUNT_DURATION_MAX` 5000 → 3000, the only value changed -- `getCountUpDuration`'s log-scale formula, `COUNT_DURATION_MIN`/`COUNT_DURATION_REF_VALUE`, and the dynamic `descriptionDelay` wiring from the entry above are all untouched and automatically pick up the new ceiling (`streak-1000`/`milestone-1000` now lands at 3s instead of 5s; every smaller tier's duration shifts down proportionally along the same curve). Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): `TrophyBadge`'s coin face, flat white/neutral since it was first built, is now a pale tint of the achievement's own accent color, per direct user feedback ("I don't like the white fill... use color adjustments to come up with thematic colors if necessary").** New `tinycolor.mix(color, '#ffffff', FACE_TINT_WHITE_MIX)` (62% white) computed once as `faceColor` and applied as `badgeClip`'s inline `backgroundColor`, replacing the static `backgroundColor: '#fff'`. Chose `tinycolor`'s RGB `mix` over HSL `.lighten()` deliberately: lightening behaves inconsistently across this app's actual task-color range (an already-light color, or gold's own naturally high lightness, barely changes or clips straight to white under `.lighten()`), while mixing toward white by a fixed percentage blends consistently regardless of the base color's own lightness -- a warm red reads as a pale rose, gold as a pale cream, a blue as a pale sky, all via the same one formula. 62% keeps the hue clearly legible while staying light enough that the full-saturation `color` icon drawn on top still reads with plenty of contrast (unaffected by this change -- the icon and border both stay at full `color`, only the face itself changed). The sheen sweep's own color was also brightened (`rgba(160,160,160,0.35)` muted grey → `rgba(255,255,255,0.5)` clean white) since a grey streak, tuned back when the face behind it was flat white, read as a dull smudge against the new colored tint rather than a glint of light.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected — pure color/styling change, no logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device — worth checking across a spread of real task colors (including an already-light/pastel one, the likeliest case for reduced icon-to-face contrast) and the gold-accented `perfect-day` case specifically, since gold's own high natural lightness is the color most likely to read as barely-tinted at this mix percentage.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the whole contrast scheme flipped, per direct user request ("maybe try inverting the contrast scheme").** The pale-tint-face + full-saturation-icon pairing from the entry above lasted one round -- `badgeClip`'s `backgroundColor` is now the raw, full-saturation `color` itself (no `tinycolor` mix at all), with the icon switched from `color` to plain `'#fff'`, and the border switched from an inline accent color to a static white rim. `tinycolor` and `FACE_TINT_WHITE_MIX` were removed from the file entirely -- nothing else in it used `tinycolor`, so this dropped back to zero color-math dependencies for the face.

This inversion isn't arbitrary -- it actually resolves a real inconsistency the tinted version had: the ribbon banner just below the coin (`ribbonBar`) was *already* a solid `color` fill with white text, unrelated to and untouched by the prior tint change, so the medallion previously mixed two different light/dark pairings (pale face + accent icon, solid ribbon + white text) within the same badge. Now both pieces share the identical scheme (solid `color`, white content), reading as one coherent design instead of two. The border also had to move off accent-color -- with the face now the same full color as the frame/pulse rings surrounding it, a same-color border would have made the coin's own edge disappear against them; white keeps it legible.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected). Not yet confirmed on-device.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the shimmer sheen reworked into a delayed, two-stage effect, per three explicit user requests in one turn: don't start until the drop-in bounce has settled; a more dramatic ease; and a second, reverse-direction stage clipped to a ring around the badge's edge.**

**1. Delayed start.** The shimmer's driving animation is now wrapped in `withDelay(DROP_DURATION, ...)` -- previously it kicked off in the same mount-time `useEffect` as everything else, meaning it could start sweeping while the badge was still literally still falling/bouncing on screen. `DROP_DURATION` (1200ms, the `drop` shared value's own `withTiming` duration) is exactly when that bounce animation's value settles at 1, so this is the same "wait for the thing to actually finish" reasoning already used elsewhere in this file (`IMPACT_DELAY`, `BURST_SETTLE_ESTIMATE`), just applied to the shimmer for the first time.

**2. Dramatic easing.** Both stages (see below) switched from `Easing.inOut(Easing.quad)` to a new shared `SHEEN_EASING = Easing.inOut(Easing.cubic)` constant -- a steeper S-curve than quad (slower start/end, a more pronounced acceleration through the middle), read as more "dramatic" per Penner's typical quad-cubic-quart-quint escalation.

**3. A second, reverse, ring-clipped stage.** Rather than two independently-timed animations kept in sync by hand, both stages are derived from one shared `sheenPhase` value (0 -> 1 -> 2 -> pause -> reset) -- the same "one control value, several derived properties/stages" pattern `dropStyle` elsewhere in this file already established. Phase 0->1 (`sheenStage1Style`) is the original sweep, unchanged in shape (`translateX` -TRAVEL -> +TRAVEL). Phase 1->2 (`sheenStage2Style`) sweeps the *reverse* direction, +TRAVEL -> -TRAVEL, picking up exactly where stage 1 left off rather than jumping -- reading as one continuous motion that swings across and then back, not two disconnected effects. Both stages are clamped ([0,1]) so once their own phase window passes, they simply sit parked at whichever end of `SHEEN_TRAVEL` (± `BADGE_SIZE * 1.6`) they finished at -- already well outside `badgeClip`'s clipped circle, so (same as the original single-stage version) no separate opacity toggle is needed to hide either bar between cycles.

Stage 2's "clipped by a donut shape... around the edges" requirement doesn't use any real clip-path/mask API -- React Native has no built-in support for one here, and this codebase already has a documented precedent (`ParticleSystem`'s glow circles) of preferring a cheap layered-Views approximation over pulling in a real graphics library (e.g. `react-native-skia`) for this class of effect. Instead: a new `sheenMask` View -- a plain opaque disc the exact color of the coin face, sized `DONUT_MASK_SIZE = BADGE_SIZE - DONUT_RING_WIDTH * 2` (an 18px rim showing around it) -- is inserted into the paint order *between* stage 2 and the icon/stage 1: `[stage 2 sweep] -> [mask disc] -> [icon] -> [stage 1 sweep]`. Since the mask disc paints after stage 2, it simply covers/hides stage 2's sweep everywhere the two overlap except the exposed rim band; since the icon and stage 1 both paint *after* the mask disc, neither is affected by it at all -- stage 1 still sweeps freely across the icon and the whole face exactly as it always did. This ordering (not a real mask) is the entire trick -- three plain views layered in the right sequence.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected -- pure animation/layout addition, no other logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is a materially new technique for this file (a layered-paint-order fake mask, and a single value driving two sequential animation stages instead of one), so worth a careful first look: that the delay genuinely lines up with the bounce settling (not too early/late), that the reverse stage 2 sweep is actually visible and reads as a distinct rim highlight rather than being lost against the mask, and that `DONUT_RING_WIDTH`'s 18px is a good width for that rim band at the badge's actual on-device size.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), same day: the two stages made simultaneous instead of sequential, and the sweep considerably slower, per direct user direction ("combined into a single simultaneous effect, so both going in opposite direction... overall duration of the shimmer animation should also be longer").** The prior version's `sheenPhase` ran 0->1 (stage 1) then 1->2 (stage 2) in sequence, so the rim highlight only appeared *after* the full-face sweep finished crossing. Simplified back to a single 0->1 sweep (the `withSequence`'s second `withTiming(2, ...)` leg was removed entirely, along with the manual clamping/offset math each style needed to isolate its own phase window) -- both `sheenStage1Style` and `sheenStage2Style` now read the *exact same* `sheenPhase.value` at every frame, differing only in which direction they map it to (`t*2-1` vs. `1-t*2`), so they move in perfect opposite-direction lockstep rather than one waiting for the other. `SHEEN_SWEEP_DURATION` 750ms → 1800ms (more than double the original single leg, and longer than the two sequential legs summed together at 1500ms) for the requested slower overall duration -- `SHEEN_PAUSE` (the idle gap between cycles) was left unchanged.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected -- pure animation-math simplification, no other logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth checking that the two simultaneous sweeps read clearly as two distinct motions (one across the whole face, one on the rim, moving opposite ways) rather than blending into visual noise now that they overlap in time, and that 1800ms doesn't feel sluggish at the badge's actual on-device size.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), same day: the donut mask dropped entirely, and the second step retargeted from the badge's own rim to the ribbon banner, per direct user direction ("get rid of the secondary sheen effect (the donut). Instead lets go back to a 2 step shimmer, the first one as it is, the second one is reversed but uses the banner/ribbon as the mask, just the middle rectangle part (not the tips)").** Reverts the *simultaneous* change from the entry immediately above back to *sequential* (`sheenPhase` runs 0->1, then 1->2, exactly like the original two-step version before that round) -- but step 2 no longer targets a ring around the badge at all.

`DONUT_RING_WIDTH`/`DONUT_MASK_SIZE` and the `sheenMask` disc were deleted outright, along with `sheenStage2Style`'s badge-ring math. The badge itself is back to a single sheen (`badgeSheenStyle`, renamed from `sheenStage1Style` now that it's the only one targeting the badge) -- step 1, "as it is," unclipped, full-face, unchanged in shape/easing/duration from the entry two above.

**Step 2 now targets the ribbon banner instead**, clipped to just `ribbonBarClip` (the banner's own rectangle) and explicitly not the two triangular tail tips (`ribbonTailLeft`/`ribbonTailRight`) flanking it -- those were never wrapped in anything clippable and still aren't. This needed the ribbon's own render structure split the same way the badge's already is, and for the same reason: `ribbonBar` used to be one `View` carrying both the drop shadow and the padding/background together, but adding `overflow: 'hidden'` (needed to clip the new sheen sweep to the rectangle) to a view that also has Android `elevation` clips the shadow away -- the exact Android quirk `badgeShadowWrap`/`badgeClip`'s own split already exists to avoid, elsewhere in this same file. `ribbonBar` is now `ribbonBarShadowWrap` (shadow + zIndex only, deliberately no width/height so it auto-sizes around its child's own intrinsic content width, unlike `badgeShadowWrap` which can hardcode `BADGE_SIZE` since the ribbon's text length varies) wrapping a new `ribbonBarClip` (the padding/`overflow:hidden`, with `backgroundColor` now applied here instead).

A new `ribbonSheen` bar reuses the exact same visual technique as the badge's (`rgba(255,255,255,0.5)`, `rotate: '20deg'`) but at a much smaller scale (`RIBBON_SHEEN_WIDTH`/`HEIGHT`/`TRAVEL` -- 16/70/80, vs. the badge's 28/`BADGE_SIZE*2.2`/`SHEEN_TRAVEL`), sized deliberately generously (taller/wider than any realistic ribbon text) since `ribbonBarClip`'s own `overflow:hidden` clips away whatever doesn't fit -- there's no cost to over-covering, only to under-covering and leaving a gap. It's centered horizontally via `left: '50%'` + `marginLeft: -RIBBON_SHEEN_WIDTH/2` (safe here, unlike this codebase's own documented percentage-positioning centering bug elsewhere -- that one was specifically about a parent whose height came from `aspectRatio`; `ribbonBarClip`'s size is plain intrinsic content sizing, not derived that way) and vertically via a fixed pixel offset computed from a rough content-height estimate (`RIBBON_ESTIMATED_HEIGHT = 24`, close enough since the bar is already far taller than the ribbon itself). `ribbonSheenStyle`'s own math is the same reverse-continuation shape step 2 already had against the badge (`t = clamp(phase - 1, 0, 1)`, `1 - t*2`), just scaled by `RIBBON_SHEEN_TRAVEL` instead of `SHEEN_TRAVEL`.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected -- pure animation/layout change, no other logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth checking in particular: the ribbon sheen's vertical centering (an estimate, not a measurement) looks right against the ribbon's actual rendered height, the sheen doesn't visibly poke out past the ribbon's rounded/tail edges, and step 2 firing right as step 1 finishes reads as one continuous two-part glint rather than two disconnected effects.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), same day: an even more dramatic ease, and the ribbon sweep decoupled from the badge sweep's own completion so the two overlap, per two direct requests ("I want the easing even more dramatic -- you should see a long slow start and tail and quick pass over... the shummer over the banner is delayed too long, it should start sooner even if that means there's overlap with the stage 1 shimmer").**

**Easing.** `SHEEN_EASING` moved from `Easing.inOut(Easing.cubic)` to `Easing.inOut(Easing.poly(5))` -- Reanimated has no built-in named `quart`/`quint` the way `quad`/`cubic` are named constants, but `Easing.poly(n)` is its generic power-easing function (`t^n`), and its own doc comment in `Easing.ts` confirms `poly(5)` is exactly `easeInQuint`. A higher power spends proportionally more of the duration near both endpoints and less near the midpoint than a lower one like cubic -- exactly the "long slow start and tail, quick pass over" shape asked for, taken one step further up the same quad -> cubic -> quart -> quint progression this file was already climbing.

**Overlap.** The two sweeps used to be driven by one shared `sheenPhase` value running 0->1 (badge) then 1->2 (ribbon) in strict sequence -- so the ribbon sweep could only ever start once the badge sweep had fully finished, which read as "delayed too long." Replaced with two fully independent shared values, `badgePhase` and `ribbonPhase`, each its own `withRepeat` loop built from a shared `sheenLoop()` factory (same `SHEEN_SWEEP_DURATION` sweep + `SHEEN_PAUSE` pause shape for both, so they stay offset by a fixed amount forever rather than drifting apart cycle to cycle) -- only their *starting* `withDelay` differs: `badgePhase` starts at `DROP_DURATION`, `ribbonPhase` at `DROP_DURATION + RIBBON_START_OFFSET` (a new constant, 500ms -- comfortably under the 1800ms sweep duration, so the ribbon sweep now genuinely starts while the badge sweep is still going, per explicit direction that overlap is fine). Each style now reads its own phase directly (`badgeSheenStyle` from `badgePhase`, `ribbonSheenStyle` from `ribbonPhase`) rather than one style clamping to `[0,1]` and the other to `[1,2]` off a shared value -- `ribbonSheenStyle`'s own direction mapping is unchanged (still the mirror of the badge's), it's just no longer mathematically continuous with the badge's endpoint the way a single shared phase enforced, since "reversed" was always about direction, not literal value continuity.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected -- pure animation-timing change, no other logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth checking that `RIBBON_START_OFFSET`'s 500ms reads as a natural stagger rather than either too-simultaneous or still-too-delayed, and that the new quintic ease's much more pronounced slow start/tail doesn't make the sweep feel like it stalls at either end before its 1800ms duration.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), same day: both sweep durations lengthened further, and the shimmer's own start pulled earlier into the drop-in sequence, per direct user direction ("Make both the shimmer light animation durations longer, and have the first shimmer (the start of the cycle) start sooner in the overall dropin animation sequence").**

`SHEEN_SWEEP_DURATION` 1800ms → 2600ms -- both loops (`badgePhase`/`ribbonPhase`) share this one constant already (see the prior entry), so lengthening it affects both without any other change; combined with the already-dramatic quintic ease from the entry above, a longer duration further amplifies that "lingering start/tail" quality rather than fighting it.

The shimmer's own start delay was decoupled from `DROP_DURATION` entirely: a new `SHEEN_START_DELAY` constant (900ms, a plain literal rather than a computed fraction of `DROP_DURATION` since that constant isn't declared until later in the file and referencing it here would hit the temporal dead zone -- roughly documented as "~3/4 of DROP_DURATION" in the comment instead) now drives `badgePhase`'s own initial `withDelay`, replacing the direct `DROP_DURATION` (1200ms) reference an earlier round had specifically added ("shouldn't start until the emblem has come to a rest"). That earlier full-settle requirement is relaxed here per this direct follow-up -- the shimmer now begins while the badge is still finishing its last small rebound, not waiting for perfect stillness. `ribbonPhase`'s own delay (`SHEEN_START_DELAY + RIBBON_START_OFFSET`) shifts earlier by the same amount automatically, keeping their relative overlap stagger unchanged. `DROP_DURATION` itself is untouched and still drives the actual bounce animation and `IMPACT_DELAY` as before -- only the shimmer's *own* dependency on it was removed.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected -- pure animation-timing change, no other logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth checking that starting the shimmer mid-bounce (rather than after full settle) doesn't read as visually distracting layered on top of the badge's own still-active rebound motion, and that 2600ms doesn't feel sluggish given the pause between cycles stayed at 2400ms.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), same day, mid-turn: pushed even earlier, per direct follow-up ("I want the shimmer effect to start sooner in the into animation").** Rather than tune `SHEEN_START_DELAY` down to yet another guessed number (this was already the second round of "sooner" after the entry immediately above), replaced it with `IMPACT_DELAY` directly -- the same trigger point the frame rings/halo burst already key off (the instant the badge first touches down, before its own decaying rebounds). This is both the earliest *meaningful* moment already available in the sequence (no reason to fire before the badge has even landed) and removes a separately-tuned constant in favor of reusing one that's already load-bearing elsewhere in this file, so the shimmer's start now stays synchronized with the rings/halo burst by construction rather than needing to be kept in sync by hand if either ever changes. `SHEEN_START_DELAY` was deleted outright; `badgePhase`/`ribbonPhase`'s own `withDelay` calls now reference `IMPACT_DELAY` (declared earlier in the file, so no temporal-dead-zone concern the way referencing it from the constants block above would have had) directly.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (160/160, unaffected -- pure animation-timing change). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is the third round of "start it sooner" in one sitting, so worth confirming this actually reads as early enough now rather than needing a fourth pass.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the Trophy Case's testing-only delete button removed, per direct user request ("Remove the delete achievement button in the trophy case").** `AchievementBadgeCard`'s trash-can `TouchableOpacity` (top-right corner of unlocked cards), its `handleDelete` handler, and the `deleteButton` style were all deleted outright from `TrophiesScreen.tsx`. The underlying `deleteAchievementsByKind` action on `achievementsStore.ts` had no other caller anywhere in the app (confirmed via a repo-wide grep), so it was removed too, along with its now-unused `AchievementKind` import in that file -- left in place it would have been genuine dead code, not a reusable API. `TrophiesScreen.tsx` also dropped the now-unused `colors` prop from `AchievementBadgeCard` (it was only ever passed through for the delete button's icon tint) and the now-unused `AchievementKind` import.

This is a separate, already-shipped testing feature from the still-pending "tap any locked achievement to preview" one (`buildPreviewAchievement`, tracked in session task #33) -- that one stays, since the user hasn't said that round of testing is done yet; only the delete button was asked to go.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the delete button added back, per explicit user direction ("Add the achievement delete button back (for testing purposes)").** Both halves the previous removal took out are restored close to their original shape: `deleteAchievementsByKind(kind)` back on `achievementsStore.ts` (wipes every earned instance of that kind from both `achievements` and `pendingCelebrations`, so its Trophy Case card returns to locked and its threshold can be crossed again), and `AchievementBadgeCard`'s trash-can `TouchableOpacity` (top-right corner, unlocked cards only -- nothing to delete on a locked one) back in `TrophiesScreen.tsx`, wired through a restored `colors`/`onDelete` prop pair. Still immediate on tap with no confirm dialog (a deliberate dev/testing shortcut, not the real Archive/Delete Task flow, which is the one place in the app that keeps a genuine `Alert.alert` confirm) and still fires the same `notificationAsync(Warning)` haptic the app's other destructive taps use. The icon swapped from a plain `trash-can` to `trash-can-outline` and shrank slightly (16 → 14) purely as a small incidental refresh while rebuilding it, not a deliberate design change.

Both this button and the still-pending "tap any locked card to preview" feature (`buildPreviewAchievement`, session task #33) are explicitly dev/testing conveniences, not shipped user-facing functionality -- neither is gated behind a flag or environment check, so removing them again later (whenever the user says testing is done) means deleting the code outright, the same way the delete button's first removal was handled.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- pure additive UI/store change, no logic touched).

**Achievements re-architected from task-colored to kind-colored, per explicit user direction, after a genuine design discussion (2026-08-11).** The user flagged a real UX problem: since multiple different tasks can each independently earn the same kind (per-task dedup scope, see the earlier per-task-treatment discussion), a Trophy Case card's badge color -- previously `latest.taskColor ?? GOLD`, i.e. whichever task most recently earned it -- silently implied single-task ownership that wasn't true, while the card itself never named *which* task that color belonged to. Three directions were discussed and weighed (a drill-down list UI, fanning the grid out to one card per task+kind, and committing achievements to a kind-level identity with task info confined to the celebration) before the user picked the third, which was also the one recommended as least disruptive to this feature's own established minimalism (single accent, no modals, no UI proliferation) and the one that fixes the actual root cause (an implied-ownership color) rather than building scaffolding to manage it.

**`AchievementMeta` gained a `color: string` field** (`achievements.ts`) -- every kind's own fixed visual identity (badge/rings/confetti/ribbon/hero-number color), grouped by family rather than 14 fully distinct hues: `STREAK_COLOR = '#FF6B6B'` (the exact red `TaskCard`'s own streak-badge "fire" state and `getStreakBadgeStyle` already use, reused rather than invented) covers the whole streak ladder (`first-streak` through `streak-1000`, plus `new-best-streak`, which was already grouped with the streak family in this file's own catalog order); `COMPLETION_COLOR = '#FFD700'` (the same gold already used app-wide for "best streak"/trophy language) covers the three `milestone-N` tiers; `PERFECT_DAY_COLOR = '#42A5F5'` and `COMEBACK_COLOR = '#66BB6A'` are fresh, since neither "a perfect day" nor "a revived streak" had an established color anywhere else in the app to borrow -- picked to sit clearly apart from the red/gold/gray vocabulary the app's existing streak-status colors already occupy. Every one of the 14 `ACHIEVEMENT_META` entries got a `color:` line (scripted, not hand-typed, to avoid a copy-paste mismatch across 14 near-identical entries).

**`TrophiesScreen.tsx`**: `AchievementBadgeCard`'s `badgeColor` (unlocked state) and `progressFillColor` (in-progress bar) both switched from a task-derived value to `ACHIEVEMENT_META[status.kind].color` directly -- unconditionally, not a fallback, since every kind now always has one. The local `GOLD` constant (previously the fallback for a taskless kind) was removed outright as now-unused. The progress caption's task-name text (e.g. "3/10 · Morning Run") was deliberately left as plain, uncolored text -- it's informational context about which task is closest, not a branding claim, so it didn't need to change.

**`AchievementCelebration.tsx`**: `CelebrationContent`'s `accentColor` (previously `achievement.taskColor ?? GOLD`) was renamed `kindColor` and now reads `meta.color` unconditionally -- driving `TrophyBadge`'s `color` prop, the hero number's color, and `Confetti`'s `baseColor`. The local `GOLD` constant was removed as now-unused (every kind has its own color, no fallback needed). `DescriptionText`'s `color` prop was renamed `taskColor` and now specifically receives `achievement.taskColor ?? kindColor` from the caller -- per the user's own framing ("only show the task name/brand inside the congratulation message"), a task's own color is now confined to exactly one place: the inline icon + bold name within the description's trigger sentence. Everywhere else on the screen is uniformly kind-colored regardless of which task triggered it.

**New `AlsoEarnedBy` component** answers "which tasks have this trophy" -- per the user's own proposed mechanism ("providing a simple list in the congratulation message... rather than a whole separate UI layer"), reusing the celebration screen that already exists for both a fresh unlock and a Trophy Case replay tap, instead of building new browsing UI. Reads the full `achievements` array (`useAchievementsStore`), dedupes to one entry per distinct *other* task that's earned this same kind (a repeatable kind's own task can appear more than once in history; only its most recent instance is kept), sorts newest-first, and renders nothing (`null`) when there's no one else to list -- so a single-earner achievement's celebration is visually unchanged. Renders as one line under the existing "Unlocked {date}" label: "Also earned by: Deep Clean, Morning Run" -- each task's name in *that task's own* color, the second and last place (alongside `DescriptionText`'s own inline span) a task's brand is allowed to surface. `metaBlock`'s `RevealSlot` `minHeight` grew 40 → 64 to comfortably fit the extra line when it's present, without affecting layout when it's absent.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- pure color-sourcing and one additive component, no `utils/` logic touched aside from the new static `color` field, which TypeScript's own `Record<AchievementKind, AchievementMeta>` typing already guarantees every kind has, so no new runtime test was needed for its presence). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a careful look at: two different tasks earning the same kind now show visually identical badges as intended, the "Also earned by" line reads cleanly under the unlock date without crowding the dismiss hint below it, and the celebration's overall color identity (badge/rings/number/confetti) now stays visually consistent regardless of which task's completion triggered it.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), a real redesign on top of the kind-color work above, per explicit user direction across two messages ("I don't love the colors you picked... since we are hand picking colors now we can come up with our own color scheme, incorporating base and accent color... For streaks I want flame-like color scheme with increasing hotness from 2-50, then make 100 and 1000 kind of magical cosmic fire. For revive use purple colors. For perfect day use vibrant gold. For milestones I want to use gemstone inspired colors" / "Put the task icons on the trophy tiles" / "on the congratulations screen I want a little scrollable list that shows the actual date and task of each unlock" / mid-turn: "Also dress up the trophy screen emblem a bit per the color scheme, halo, banner, and shadow effect we use on the congratulation screen").**

**1. `AchievementMeta.color` changed from a flat `string` to `{ base: string; accent: string }`**, and every one of the 14 kinds got a genuinely hand-picked pair (scripted substitution, not hand-typed, to avoid a copy-paste mismatch across 14 near-identical entries) instead of one flat color shared across a whole family:
- **Streak ladder** (`first-streak` through `streak-50`): a real flame-temperature progression, deep ember red (`#C62828`) at the 2-day tier through orange/amber/hot-yellow (`#FF6F00`/`#FFB300`/`#FFD600`) at 50 -- vernacular "red hot -> white hot" fire intuition, not literal blackbody physics (which inverts past a point).
- **`streak-100`/`streak-1000`**: break from the natural fire spectrum entirely into cosmic violet (`#7C4DFF`) and electric magenta (`#D500F9`) -- "magical cosmic fire," per the literal ask, escalating in saturation/otherness for the higher tier.
- **`new-best-streak`**: a blue-white flare (`#40C4FF`) -- not on the fixed length ladder (it's "beyond the ladder," re-earnable indefinitely), given the one color in the set that's a nod to real physics (blue is the actual hottest flame color), reading as "hotter than the whole red-to-yellow ladder."
- **`comeback`**: rich purple (`#8E24AA`), per "for revive use purple colors."
- **`perfect-day`**: vibrant gold (`#FFC400`), per "for perfect day use vibrant gold" -- freed up from the milestone family, which used to share this same gold before this pass.
- **Milestones** (`milestone-10`/`100`/`1000`): a gemstone progression -- peridot green (`#8BC34A`) -> sapphire blue (`#1565C0`) -> diamond cyan (`#00E5FF`) -- escalating toward the most "precious" stone at the highest tier, echoing the kind's own medal/medal-outline iconography with a per-tier rarity story rather than one flat gold for all three.

Every pair's `accent` is a lighter/brighter variant in the same family (e.g. `streak-10`'s `#FF6F00` base pairs with a warm `#FFD180` glow) -- the "aura" color described below, distinct from the coin itself.

**2. `TrophyBadge.tsx` (the celebration screen's medallion) gained a real base/accent split**, not just a data-model change with no visual effect: a new optional `accentColor` prop (falling back to `color` if omitted) now drives the frame rings, halo glow, and sonar pulse rings -- the medallion's "aura" -- while `color` (the base) stays driving the coin face, ring-shadow tint, ribbon banner, and icon exactly as before. `AchievementCelebration.tsx`'s `CelebrationContent` renamed its local `accentColor` variable to `kindColor` (now `meta.color.base`) and added `kindAccentColor` (`meta.color.accent`), passing both into `<TrophyBadge>`.

**3. New `earners: AchievementEarner[]` field on `AchievementCardStatus`** (`achievements.ts`) -- every distinct task that's earned a kind at least once, deduped to its most recent instance per task, sorted newest-first. Empty for a locked kind or a global kind (first-streak/perfect-day have no per-task earner to show). `TrophiesScreen.tsx`'s new `EarnerIconRow` renders up to `MAX_VISIBLE_EARNER_ICONS` (4) small task-colored icon chips under an unlocked card's title, plus a "+N" overflow chip -- per explicit direction ("put the task icons on the trophy tiles so at a glance you know which ones have them"), this is the Trophy Case's own at-a-glance answer to "which tasks have this," distinct from (and complementary to) the celebration screen's own richer history list below.

**4. `AchievementCelebration.tsx`'s single-line "Also earned by" text (from the earlier kind-color pass) was replaced entirely with a scrollable `UnlockHistoryList`**, per explicit direction ("a little scrollable list that shows the actual date and task of each unlock (most recent at top) with task color name and icon"). This fully subsumes the old single "Unlocked {date}" line too (which only ever named *this one* celebrated instance) -- the meta block now shows a small-caps "Unlock History" label over a fixed-height (`HISTORY_LIST_HEIGHT = 96`, so `RevealSlot`'s own layout-shift-avoidance contract holds regardless of whether a kind has 1 instance or 50) `ScrollView`, one row per instance of this kind across every task, each row: a small task-colored icon chip, the task's own name in its own color, and the date, most-recent-first. A row for a taskless global instance falls back to the kind's own icon/color. `RevealSlot`'s `minHeight` for this slot grew `64 -> 64 + HISTORY_LIST_HEIGHT` to fit it.

**5. `TrophiesScreen.tsx` gained a new static `TrophyEmblem` component** for unlocked cards, per the mid-turn addition ("dress up the trophy screen emblem... per the color scheme, halo, banner, and shadow effect we use on the congratulation screen") -- reusing `TrophyBadge.tsx`'s exact visual language (a halo glow behind a white ring, a colored face with the same "oversized rotated square, clipped by overflow:hidden" flat diagonal-shadow technique, a ribbon banner with the classic border-triangle tail trick) at a much smaller, fixed 56px size (`EMBLEM_SIZE`), with **none** of TrophyBadge's animation machinery -- deliberately a fresh, self-contained duplication of the (small, well-understood) shadow-overlay math rather than importing pieces from the heavily-tuned, Reanimated-driven celebration component, since up to ~14 of these render simultaneously in a static grid where TrophyBadge is built to be the sole centerpiece of its own full-screen moment. `getEmblemShadowOverlayStyle(containerSize)` generalizes `TrophyBadge`'s own fixed-size formula to an arbitrary size. Locked cards deliberately keep their original plain dimmed-circle-plus-lock look, not this treatment -- nothing to show off yet, and the "dressed up = earned, plain grey = not yet" contrast is itself useful information at a glance. `badgeWrap`'s own style dropped its fixed square dimensions (the dressed-up emblem's halo/ribbon extend past `EMBLEM_SIZE`'s own footprint) in favor of a `minHeight` + centering, so it can size to whichever of the two very different-shaped badges it's holding.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- no test coverage exists or is expected for this component-level color/visual work per this project's stated testing scope; the one genuine data-shape addition, `AchievementCardStatus.earners`, didn't break any existing test since none asserted the full status object shape via strict equality). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is the largest visual change to the achievement feature since its initial build, so worth a careful full pass: the flame-to-cosmic streak progression actually reads as an escalating "hotness" story at a glance, the gemstone milestone colors feel distinct from the streak family, the Trophy Case's new dressed-up emblem (halo/ring/ribbon at 56px) doesn't look cramped or clipped within its card, the earner icon row doesn't overflow a narrow 2-column card, and the celebration's new scrollable history list scrolls smoothly and doesn't visually fight with the dismiss-hint spacer below it.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11), a third color pass, per explicit user direction ("for each one I want 3 colors: base, glow, and accent. Use a thematic color scheme and make these really pop. Some themes include: gold, silver, bronze, platinum, diamond, amethyst, cobalt, ruby, saffire, fire, intense fire, raging fire, magic fire, cosmic fire, rebirth (for revive), etc").** `AchievementMeta.color` grew a third field, `{ base: string; glow: string; accent: string }`, and all 14 kinds were re-picked against real named themes from the list (not a flat family color, and not the prior round's plain base/accent pair) -- see the type's own long comment in `achievements.ts` for the full per-kind reasoning, summarized here:
- **Streak ladder, a genuine fire-intensity escalation**: Fire (`first-streak`/`streak-2`, deep ember red `#E64A19`) -> Intense Fire (`streak-5`/`streak-10`, vivid orange `#FF6D00`) -> Raging Fire (`streak-25`/`streak-50`, blazing amber-gold `#FFA000`) -> Magic Fire (`streak-100`, breaking from real flame colors into violet `#7B1FA2`) -> Cosmic Fire (`streak-1000`, electric magenta-violet `#AA00FF`, the ultimate tier).
- **`new-best-streak`**: Diamond (`#00B8D4`, brilliant icy cyan) -- not on the fixed ladder, so not part of the fire story; a personal record is about brilliance, not heat.
- **Milestones**: a classic Bronze (`#8D5524`) -> Silver (`#607D8B`) -> Gold (`#B8860B`) medal progression, echoing their own medal-outline/medal iconography with an escalating sense of rank.
- **`perfect-day`**: Radiant Gold (`#FFAB00`) -- deliberately brighter/more saturated ("sunburst") than milestone-1000's deeper, more antique "trophy" Gold, so the two don't read as the same color despite sharing a family name.
- **`comeback`**: Rebirth (`#6A1B9A` deep violet base -> `#AB47BC` magenta-violet glow -> `#FFD54F` gold-dawn accent) -- night breaking into light, not a flat single purple.

**What each of the three colors actually drives, threaded through every consumer:**
- **`base`** -- the coin/face, ring-shadow tint, and ribbon banner (kept vivid enough to stay legible under the ribbon's white text -- `accent`, usually the palest of the three, is never used as a text background anywhere for exactly this reason).
- **`glow`** -- the surrounding aura: `TrophyBadge`'s halo and outer two frame rings/pulse rings, and the Trophy Case's own static halo.
- **`accent`** -- reserved for small, sparkle-scale detail rather than any large fill: `TrophyBadge`'s innermost frame ring (now split from the outer two, which use `glow`) and its twinkling particle sparkles (`getSparkleConfig(accentColor)`, replacing the previous fixed white/gold sparkle palette with one tinted per-kind), plus a new thin `accentRing` on the Trophy Case's static `TrophyEmblem` (a small ring just outside the white ring, giving the small static badge a two-tone aura split too, without needing any animation to make it legible at 56px).

**`TrophyBadge.tsx`'s props**: `accentColor` (which meant "the whole aura" in the prior round) was renamed `glowColor` and a *new* `accentColor` was added with its own, narrower meaning (sparkle/innermost-ring highlight) -- both optional, both falling back to `color` if omitted. `AchievementCelebration.tsx` now computes and threads `kindColor`/`kindGlowColor`/`kindAccentColor` (`meta.color.base`/`.glow`/`.accent`) into `TrophyBadge`.

**`Confetti.tsx`** gained optional `glowColor`/`accentColor` props too -- `buildPalette` now draws from all three (base, base+10% lighten, glow, accent, white) when both are supplied, instead of deriving every swatch from one hue's own lighten/darken variants; falls back to the original single-hue-derived spread when only `baseColor` is given, so the component's public API stays backward compatible. `AchievementCelebration.tsx` passes all three kind colors through.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- pure color/prop-threading work, no logic touched, no new test coverage needed or expected for this component-level visual work). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a careful look at: whether the fire-family escalation still reads clearly now that each tier is a genuinely distinct named color rather than a lightened/darkened variant of one hue, whether the innermost accent-colored frame ring and the outer glow-colored ones read as a deliberate two-tone aura rather than a mismatch, and whether the confetti's richer multi-hue palette (for kinds with real glow/accent contrast, e.g. Rebirth's violet/magenta/gold) looks cohesive rather than busy.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): each kind's color now names its own theme, per direct user request ("include the 'theme' you are going for in the code, right now there's a comment that just repeats the achievement key").** `AchievementMeta.color` gained a fourth field, `theme: string` -- real data sitting right next to the three hex values it names (`{ theme: 'Fire', base: '#E64A19', glow: '#FF7043', accent: '#FFAB91' }`), not a trailing comment that could quietly drift out of sync with the hex values beside it if one were edited without the other. Replaces the previous trailing `// first-streak`-style comment, which was redundant anyway (the kind is already the object's own key one level up) and didn't actually say anything about *why* those particular hex values were chosen. All 14 entries updated (scripted, matching each kind to the theme name already established in the previous round's design: Fire/Intense Fire/Raging Fire/Magic Fire/Cosmic Fire across the streak ladder, Diamond, Bronze/Silver/Gold, Radiant Gold, Rebirth). Nothing currently reads `theme` at runtime -- it's documentation-as-data for now, but structured so a future feature (e.g. showing a theme name somewhere in the UI) could pick it up directly rather than needing to invent a new lookup.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- pure data-shape addition, no consumer destructures `color` in a way a new field could break).

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): `accent`'s role and its own values both redesigned, per explicit user direction ("try making the accent color a VERY vibrant color based on the theme, and use it for the icon and circle border").** `accent` had been the *palest* of the three colors up to this point (a sparkle-scale highlight, deliberately desaturated so it was never used as a text background) -- this reverses that: every one of the 14 kinds got a new, deliberately bold/saturated `accent` value picked to genuinely *pop* against its own `base`/`glow` (not a tint/shade of the same hue) -- e.g. Cosmic Fire's electric cyan `#00E5FF` against its violet base, Silver's vivid blue `#2979FF` against its muted steel base, Raging Fire's hot-ember red `#FF1744` breaking from the amber/gold base+glow it sits next to. `AchievementMeta.color`'s own doc comment in `achievements.ts` was rewritten to match -- `accent` is now explicitly "the boldest of the three, not the palest."

**Two concrete new uses, matching the literal request ("the icon and circle border"), in both places a medallion renders:**
- `TrophyBadge.tsx` (celebration): the ring (`badgeRing`, previously a fixed white fill) and the coin's icon glyph (previously a fixed white `#fff`) both now use `accent` instead. The ring's own flat-shadow tint (`ringShadowColor`) is now darkened from `accent` itself rather than from white, so the shading stays correct for whatever color the ring actually is.
- `TrophiesScreen.tsx`'s static `TrophyEmblem` got the identical treatment (ring + icon -> `accentColor`, ring shadow tint darkened from `accentColor`). Its separate, thinner `accentRing` (a small ring floating just outside the white one, added in the prior round specifically to give the static tile *some* accent presence) was removed outright -- now that the main ring itself carries `accent` directly, a second same-colored ring sitting right next to it would have doubled up rather than adding anything.

`color` (base) keeps its existing roles (the coin face, the ribbon banner) unchanged -- still the only one of the three ever sitting behind text, still chosen to stay legible under the ribbon's white label regardless of how vivid `accent` gets.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- pure color-value and prop-wiring changes, no logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a careful look at whether the now much-more-saturated accent ring reads as a deliberate two-tone coin (accent ring + base face) rather than clashing, and whether the accent-colored icon stays legible against the base-colored face for every theme, especially the ones where accent and base sit close in hue (e.g. Gold's `#FFD600` accent icon against its `#B8860B` base face).

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): Diamond's own accent swapped, per direct user pushback ("Diamond accent color is a strange choice").** The prior pick, `#FF4081` (a hot pink, meant as a "prismatic sparkle/refraction" flash), read as an unrelated warm color randomly dropped next to Diamond's cool cyan base/glow, with no real diamond association to justify it. Replaced with `#651FFF` (Material Deep Purple A400, a vivid electric blue-violet) -- a genuine diamond phenomenon (a blue-violet fluorescence/dispersion flash some diamonds actually show under certain light) rather than an arbitrary contrast pick, and it stays in the same cool-toned family as the cyan base/glow instead of clashing warm-against-cool the way the pink did. Confirmed no collision with any other kind's own hex values (`#651FFF` is otherwise unused in the palette).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- single hex value change).

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): every `accent` re-picked for real contrast against its own `base`, per explicit user direction ("the contrast with the base color is not great in a lot of cases. See what you can do to improve contrast, probably by making them lighter").** Measured objectively first, not just eyeballed: a throwaway script computed WCAG relative luminance / contrast ratio (the same formula browsers use for text-contrast checks) for every kind's `accent` against its own `base`, since the icon glyph is drawn directly on `base` and needs to actually read against it. Confirmed the complaint was real and severe in places -- Silver's vivid blue accent (`#2979FF`) against its own steel-blue base measured **~1.1:1**, i.e. barely distinguishable, and half the other pairs sat in the 1.9-2.5 range.

**The fix isn't uniformly "lighter."** Computing each base's own luminance first showed why: for a base that's already bright (Intense Fire `#FF6D00`, Raging Fire `#FFA000`, Radiant Gold `#FFAB00`, luminance 0.32-0.50), even pure white only reaches ~2-2.8:1 against it -- there's no room *above* to create separation, so a lighter accent can't fix these regardless of how light it goes. For those three (plus Gold, whose lighter-direction ceiling was only ~3.25:1), the fix is a **deep, rich accent instead** -- verified via the same script that going dark clears 4-7:1 where going light was capped low. Every other kind (dark/mid-luminance bases: Fire, Magic Fire, Cosmic Fire, Diamond, Bronze, Silver, Rebirth) got a genuinely *light* accent instead of the previous mid-tone "vivid" pick, since a mid-tone sits roughly equidistant from both black and white and gets the worst of both directions.

This split also became a real thematic beat rather than an arbitrary numeric fix: the fire ladder's two hottest fixed tiers (Intense Fire, Raging Fire) now get a deep indigo-to-violet "blue-hot core" accent (`#1A237E`/`#4A148C`) -- a nod to the real phenomenon that a flame's actual hottest point reads blue, so the ladder's accent hue goes from a pale golden spark at its coolest tier to revealing a blue-hot core once it's genuinely raging, continuing into Magic Fire's light pink and Cosmic Fire's icy pale cyan once it goes fully supernatural. `milestone-1000`/`perfect-day` (Gold/Radiant Gold, both bright bases) share the same deep-indigo family (`#1A237E`/`#283593`) for the same contrast reason. Every final pair was re-verified via the script to clear at least ~3.5:1, most comfortably above 4.5:1 -- see the updated table:

| kind | base | accent (before -> after) | contrast (before -> after) |
|---|---|---|---|
| first-streak/streak-2 (Fire) | `#E64A19` | `#FFC400` -> `#FFF8E1` | 2.45 -> 3.69 |
| streak-5/streak-10 (Intense Fire) | `#FF6D00` | `#FFEA00` -> `#1A237E` | 2.29 -> 4.69 |
| streak-25/streak-50 (Raging Fire) | `#FFA000` | `#FF1744` -> `#4A148C` | 1.88 -> 5.81 |
| streak-100 (Magic Fire) | `#7B1FA2` | `#F50057` -> `#FCE4EC` | 1.96 -> 6.82 |
| streak-1000 (Cosmic Fire) | `#AA00FF` | `#00E5FF` -> `#E0F7FA` | 3.29 -> 4.54 |
| new-best-streak (Diamond) | `#00B8D4` | `#651FFF` -> `#311B92` | 2.76 -> 5.17 |
| milestone-10 (Bronze) | `#8D5524` | `#FF6E40` -> `#FFE0B2` | 2.19 -> 4.79 |
| milestone-100 (Silver) | `#607D8B` | `#2979FF` -> `#E1F5FE` | **1.10** -> 3.89 |
| milestone-1000 (Gold) | `#B8860B` | `#FFD600` -> `#1A237E` | 2.30 -> 4.07 |
| perfect-day (Radiant Gold) | `#FFAB00` | `#FF3D00` -> `#283593` | 1.87 -> 5.48 |
| comeback (Rebirth) | `#6A1B9A` | `#FFD600` (unchanged) | 6.65 (already good) |

`AchievementMeta.color`'s own doc comment in `achievements.ts` was rewritten to explain this properly (why direction isn't uniform, why blue-toned accents on the two hottest fire tiers is a real thematic choice and not an inconsistency) so a future edit doesn't accidentally "fix" it back toward one direction.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the contrast-driven redesign above reverted for the fire ladder, per direct user pushback ("Not great. Most of the streaks looked better before. Take a step back... keeping in mind I really liked the streak colors, and the base colors of the gemstones and exotics").** A real preference call, not a bug: the previous entry's own contrast-maximizing pass had genuinely measured, real problems (Silver's ~1.1:1), but fixing it by re-picking *every* accent purely for luminance separation meant abandoning the fire ladder's own warm identity for a "blue-hot core" the user didn't want -- accurate by the numbers, worse by the only metric that actually matters here.

New accents: Bronze `#FFB74D` (warm copper-gold, 3.51:1), Silver `#E1F5FE` (icy pale blue, 3.89:1), Gold `#FFF9C4` (pale warm gold, 3.04:1), Radiant Gold `#B71C1C` (deep ember red -- stays in the warm family unlike the reverted indigo, 3.47:1). Diamond (`#311B92`) and Rebirth (`#FFD600`) were left exactly as they already stood, since neither was called out as part of "the streaks" and both already had reasonable contrast (5.17:1, 6.65:1).

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): starting a per-kind review pass, Diamond first -- accent changed again, per direct user feedback ("Diamond -- base color is great, but accent is weird").** `#311B92` (deep violet, meant as a "diamond fluorescence flash" reference two rounds back) evidently didn't land -- a dark violet sitting next to a bright cyan base/glow doesn't obviously read as "diamond" without that backstory, and reads as an arbitrary color choice without it. Replaced with plain `#FFFFFF` -- the simplest, most literal reading of the theme (a diamond is colorless; its "color" *is* white light), which also sidesteps the whole "which hue represents a diamond" question the two prior picks (pink, then violet) kept guessing at. Contrast against the cyan base is modest (2.38:1, since the base itself is bright enough that even pure white has limited headroom -- the same ceiling problem documented for Intense Fire/Raging Fire/Radiant Gold in the entry above), accepted for the same reason: this round is optimizing for "does this look right," not the WCAG number.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- single hex value change). Rest of the palette (streak ladder, Bronze/Silver/Gold/Radiant Gold/Rebirth) unchanged -- per the user's own "one at a time" framing, only Diamond was touched this round.

**Follow-up (2026-08-11): the fire ladder expanded from 3 shared tiers to 5 fully unique ones, and Raging Fire's real defect diagnosed and fixed, per direct user direction ("Fire and Intense Fire both look good. Raging Fire has some contrast issues. I want streak-2, 5, 10, 25, 50 to all have a unique fire theme, with growing intensity").** Previously `streak-5`/`streak-10` shared one "Intense Fire" color set and `streak-25`/`streak-50` shared one "Raging Fire" set (3 unique tiers covering 5 kinds); now each of the 5 gets its own theme and colors: **Fire** (2, unchanged -- explicitly liked) -> **Intense Fire** (5, unchanged -- explicitly liked) -> **Blazing Fire** (10, new: base `#FF8F00`, glow `#FFC107`, accent `#FFF59D`) -> **Raging Fire** (25, name kept, colors replaced: base `#FFA000`, glow `#FFCA28`, accent `#FFF9C4`) -> **Inferno** (50, new: base `#FFB300`, glow `#FFE082`, accent `#FFFDE7`, the last "natural fire" tier before Magic Fire takes over at 100). `first-streak` keeps mirroring `streak-2`'s exact colors rather than getting its own 8th slot, since it's the same real-world moment (a first 2-day streak) at global vs. per-task scope, not a distinct intensity tier.

**Root-caused *why* Raging Fire specifically read badly, rather than just re-picking numbers again.** Comparing the two tiers the user said looked fine against the one that didn't: Fire's accent (`#FFC400`) and Intense Fire's accent (`#FFEA00`) are both unambiguously *brighter* than their own base -- they read as a highlight/spark sitting on top of a darker body color. Raging Fire's old accent (`#FF1744`, a saturated red) was actually *darker*-reading than its own golden-amber base (`#FFA000`) -- the icon read as a dim smudge, not a spark, which is a real defect in the *relationship* between the two colors, not just a matter of the raw WCAG contrast number (which barely differed between the "fine" and "bad" pairs -- 2.29-2.45 for the liked ones vs. 1.88 for Raging Fire's old one). Every one of the five new tiers now follows one consistent rule: `accent` is always brighter than that tier's own `base`, escalating toward near-white as the tier count climbs (amber -> yellow -> pale yellow -> near-white), while `base` itself also warms/brightens tier over tier (deep red -> orange -> amber -> gold) -- so the *contrast ratio* keeps shrinking by the numbers (there's less room above an already-bright base at the top of the ladder) but the *qualitative* highlight-on-body relationship that actually reads as "looks right" holds all the way up, which is what the fix was actually for. `AchievementMeta.color`'s own doc comment was rewritten to state this rule explicitly, specifically so a future contrast-driven pass doesn't undo it by optimizing for the wrong metric again.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (174/174, unaffected -- pure hex-value/doc changes). Streak-100/1000 (Magic Fire/Cosmic Fire) and every milestone/exotic kind (Diamond, Bronze/Silver/Gold, Radiant Gold, Rebirth) untouched this round.

**Follow-up (2026-08-12): three more kinds switched to a proper gemstone theme, per explicit user direction ("use gemstone themes for first streak, perfect day, and comeback").** Asked directly beforehand ("Are we using gemstone themes for anything?") -- answer at the time was just Diamond (`new-best-streak`); the original theme brainstorm's other named gems (amethyst, ruby, sapphire, plus "cobalt") had never made it into the actual palette. This rounds that out:

- **`first-streak`** -- previously just mirrored `streak-2`'s exact colors (same real-world "first-ever 2-day streak" moment, global vs. per-task scope), now decoupled with its own **Ruby** theme (`base: '#C62828'`, `glow: '#EF5350'`, `accent: '#FFEBEE'`) -- a deep, rich gem red rather than `streak-2`'s own Fire flame (similar hue family, but Ruby's glow/accent lean toward a clean pink-white sparkle instead of Fire's warm amber/orange flame-light, so a polished gem reads distinctly from a flame despite the shared red). `streak-2` itself is untouched, still Fire, still the first rung of the fire-length ladder.
- **`perfect-day`** -- replaced **Radiant Gold** (`#FFAB00`/`#FFD600`/`#B71C1C`) with **Sapphire** (`base: '#1565C0'`, `glow: '#64B5F6'`, `accent: '#E3F2FD'`) -- a rich royal blue with an icy-white sparkle accent. Radiant Gold had already been flagged in an earlier round's design notes as sitting uncomfortably close to milestone-1000's own Gold; Sapphire sidesteps that entirely and is also a deliberately cool note in an otherwise mostly warm-toned palette.
- **`comeback`** -- **Rebirth** simply *renamed* to **Amethyst**, hex values completely unchanged (`#6A1B9A`/`#AB47BC`/`#FFD600`) -- the existing deep-violet-to-gold-dawn look already reads exactly like a purple gem with a gold facet-sparkle, so only the theme's name/story needed correcting to match the requested gemstone category, not the actual colors.

Both new pairs verified for contrast the same way the fire-ladder fix was: Ruby's `base`/`accent` measure ~4.9:1, Sapphire's ~5.0:1 -- both comfortably clear, and both follow the same "accent brighter than base, reads as a sparkle/highlight" rule established for the fire tiers.
