**Follow-up, same day: the Stats showcase now respects the "Celebrate Achievements" setting, per explicit user direction ("If the settings has achievements turned off, I want to keep the trophy case icon in the upper right but don't show the stats trophy showcase").** Both `TaskStatsView` and `DashboardStatsView` now read `achievementCelebrationsEnabled` (`useSettingsStore`) and wrap their own `<AchievementsPreviewCard>` in that condition -- turning celebrations off already meant no popup fires on a new unlock (see the achievements feature's own established "the celebration setting only gates whether the *popup* shows, never whether history is recorded" design), and this extends that same "don't push it at someone who's opted out" reasoning to the *unprompted* Stats-screen showcase too, without touching anything else achievement-related: the Trophy Case screen itself, its own data, and `DashboardScreen`'s header nav button into it (already unconditional, confirmed unaffected) all stay fully reachable regardless of the setting -- this only stops it from surfacing where the user didn't ask to see it.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (219/219, unaffected -- pure conditional-rendering change, no logic touched).

**Follow-up, same day: the card's own `marginBottom` widened from 12 to 20, per direct user feedback ("I want a little better vertical spacing in the stats page elements").** `AchievementsPreviewCard`'s outer `card` style had inherited a `marginBottom: 12` from `StatsCharts.tsx`'s own chart-card styling (the file this component's layout was originally modeled on) -- appropriate there, since those cards stack directly against each other within one "Activity" section, but a visible mismatch here: every other section-level element on both Stats screens (`statsDivider`, `chartSection`, etc.) uses a 20-24px rhythm, and this card sits *between* two whole sections (the tertiary stat strip above, the Activity chart section below), not stacked inside either one. Widened to 20 to match that outer rhythm instead of the tighter inter-card spacing it had been using.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (219/219, unaffected -- pure styling value change, no logic touched).

**Follow-up, same day: a first attempt at fixing the header's "+N" count (branching what it counts on `taskScoped`, falling back to "View All" only once the count reached zero) was tried, then simplified away entirely per direct user follow-up ("You didn't quite do what I meant with the view all option, but lets go with it. Just make it say View All for all contexts").** The original complaint was real (a per-task card's "+N" counted every still-locked kind in the whole task-scoped catalog, not just further-unlocked ones, so a task with 2 real trophies could badge itself a misleading "+13") and the fix's diagnosis was correct, but the conditional "+N or View All depending on the count" result wasn't actually what was wanted -- rather than re-deriving a more accurate count, the whole numeric badge was dropped in favor of a single, constant "View All" label in every context (task-scoped or aggregate, however many achievements exist). `remainingCount`/`totalCount` were removed from the component entirely -- there's no count left to compute -- and both header render paths (the fully-empty early-return branch and the main one) now render the identical static text, so the header's own wording never has to change based on data at all.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (236/236, unaffected -- pure simplification, no logic touched).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (236/236, unaffected -- pure display-logic change, no `utils/` logic touched).

**Achievement spam via undo/redo, fixed (2026-08-13), closing a standing `TODO.md` item raised via direct user investigation request.** A repeatable, task-scoped kind's threshold-crossing check (`streak-2` through `streak-1000`, `new-best-streak`, `comeback`) could re-fire every time the exact same completion was undone (`undoCompleteTask`) and then redone (`completeTask` again) -- each redo is a fresh, genuine `prevTask`/`nextTask` transition crossing the identical threshold again, with nothing actually newly earned, but the old dedup scope (a bare `taskId`) couldn't tell that apart from a real future crossing.

Investigated per-kind before proposing a fix, since not every repeatable kind turned out to actually be exploitable: `new-best-streak`'s own condition is self-protecting (`bestStreak` recomputes back down in sync with `currentStreak` on undo, since `bestStreak = Math.max(longestRunIncludingClose(...), currentStreak)` -- after undo, `prevCurrent === prevBest` again, failing the `prevCurrent < prevBest` guard on redo), but `streak-N`, `comeback`, `perfect-day`, and `perfect-week` all genuinely were exploitable -- each condition's own "before" state (a lower streak length, an expired status, an unmet due-day count) is exactly what an undo reverts back to.

**The fix, per the user's own proposed simplification ("Can't we just include the date earned for streak and comeback as part of the dedupe scope?") -- confirmed correct and more elegant than an earlier "totalCompletions advancement marker" idea, then implemented:**

1. **`app/utils/achievements.ts`**: a new, exported `REPEATABLE_TASK_SCOPED_KINDS` set (repeatable && task-scoped kinds -- the seven `streak-N` tiers, `new-best-streak`, `comeback`) plus a `dedupScopeFor(kind, taskId)` helper -- date-qualifies these kinds' own dedupScope (`` `${taskId}:${dateString}` `` instead of bare `taskId`) while leaving every other kind's scope untouched. A specific calendar date can only ever represent one genuine streak-threshold crossing for a given task, making date-qualification a sound discriminator between "replay of an earlier crossing" and "a real new one," not just a heuristic -- and every real undo/redo cycle realistically operates within the same calendar day anyway (confirmed by checking every actual undo call site). `perfect-day`/`perfect-week` were already date-scoped by their own pre-existing design, so they needed no scope change here -- only a dedup-*set* fix (below). The `comeback` block had a separate, real gap of its own: unlike the generic `FIXED_THRESHOLD_ENTRIES` loop (which already gated every push behind `isFirstEarn`), the bespoke `comeback`/`new-best-streak` blocks never checked `isFirstEarn` at all. `new-best-streak` correctly stays ungated (per the self-protecting reasoning above, and an existing test asserting exactly that), but `comeback` needed a genuine `isFirstEarn` gate added -- without it, date-qualifying its dedupScope alone would have been inert, since nothing ever consulted the scope to block a repeat. Caught directly by a new regression test failing on the first pass, not assumed.
2. **`app/stores/achievementsStore.ts`**: `recordCompletionAchievements`'s `alreadyEarnedScopes` builder dropped its `ONE_TIME_KINDS`-only filter entirely, now built from *every* existing achievement unconditionally. Previously this excluded every repeatable kind's own records from the dedup set outright -- which is what made the date-qualification alone insufficient on its own: even with a correctly date-scoped key, `isFirstEarn` had nothing in the set to actually match against, so it always returned true regardless. With date-qualification in place, including these kinds unconditionally is safe: a same-date replay hits the exact same key and is correctly blocked, while a genuine new crossing on a later date gets its own distinct key and still fires normally. This also incidentally closed an identical, previously-undetected live-path gap for `perfect-day`/`perfect-week` (both already date-scoped, but previously excluded from this same set the same way -- meaning completing an already-"perfect" day's tasks again via undo/redo, or any other same-date re-completion after the day already qualified, could have re-recorded a duplicate perfect-day/perfect-week live too).
3. **`app/utils/achievements.ts`'s `detectRetroactiveAchievements`**: a follow-on correctness issue caught while implementing the above, not requested but necessary to avoid introducing a real regression -- this function's own pre-existing counting-based dedup guard (`alreadyRecordedCounts`, needed since a repeatable task-scoped kind can legitimately be earned multiple times across a task's real history) was keyed on each existing achievement's *literal* `dedupScope` string. Once that string started varying by date for these kinds, the counting guard would silently stop matching against *any* already-recorded achievement of this class -- including every one that predates this fix and still carries the old bare-`taskId` format -- and the retroactive "check for missed achievements" scan would start re-awarding trophies the user had already earned. Fixed by re-keying `alreadyRecordedCounts` (both where it's built from existing achievements, and where newly-replayed items are checked against it) on plain `` `${kind}:${taskId}` `` instead of the full `dedupScope` string -- `taskId` is stable regardless of which dedupScope format produced a given record, so this is what actually answers "how many times has this task already earned this kind," independent of the string shape any individual record happens to carry.

**Tests**: a new `describe('undo/redo replay does not spam a repeatable task-scoped kind', ...)` block in `__tests__/utils/achievements.test.ts` simulates what the store actually does across two calls -- accumulating the first call's own emitted dedupScope(s) into the `alreadyEarnedScopes` set passed to a second, identical call -- covering `streak-N` (a same-date redo is blocked; a genuine later-date crossing for an unrelated task still fires), `comeback` (the specific gap this investigation found), and `perfect-day` (a second same-date completion doesn't re-record an already-perfect day). Two pre-existing assertions needed updating to the new date-qualified format (`dedupScope`/dedup-set literals in the `first-completion` and `streak-N` describe blocks) -- both genuine format updates, not behavior regressions, confirmed by re-deriving the expected values directly from `dedupScopeFor`'s own formula rather than just making the test pass.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (240/240, up from the prior count with 4 new regression tests covering this fix directly). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth prioritizing given the bug was only ever reachable through a real multi-step interaction sequence (complete, undo, redo): confirm a `streak-N` tier, `comeback`, and a same-day `perfect-day` re-completion each stop re-firing after this fix, and that a genuine new crossing on a later day still celebrates normally. Also worth a one-time check that running the Trophy Case's/Settings' "check for missed achievements" scan against any real, already-earned history from before this fix doesn't produce unexpected duplicate trophies -- the `taskId`-based re-keying above is designed to prevent exactly that, but hasn't been exercised against real pre-fix data on a device yet.

**Performance review (2026-08-13), prompted by a direct user report: "poor performance switching tasks (from the header) only when the stats view."** That the symptom is specific to the Stats tab -- not Calendar or Streaks, which share the exact same `TaskHeader` prev/next mechanism (`TaskDetailScreen`'s `router.setParams({ taskId })`, confirmed via direct read to swap `taskId` on the *same* screen instance rather than remounting it -- `Reanimated.View`'s own `key={activeTab}` is keyed on tab, not task, so `TaskStatsView` genuinely stays mounted and just re-renders with a new `task` prop, not a fresh mount) -- pointed straight at something Stats-specific added since the last chart-performance pass, and the achievements integration (built the same day) was the obvious suspect.

**Root cause, found and confirmed by reading the code, not guessed:** `AchievementsPreviewCard`'s `taskScoped` mode (used by `TaskStatsView`, `activeTasks` = a single-element `[task]` array) called `getGroupedAchievementCardStatuses`, which computes `getAchievementCardStatus` for **all 21 kinds in the catalog** via `getAllAchievementCardStatuses` -- only *afterward* filtering the result down to the ~13 task-scoped kinds actually worth showing (`isTaskScopedKind`). The 6 global kinds computed and then discarded on every single task switch weren't all free: `early-bird`/`night-owl`'s `time-of-day-ratio` strategy does `activeTasks.flatMap(t => t.completions ?? [])` then `[...allCompletions].sort(...)` -- with `activeTasks` being just the one switched-to task, this is that task's **entire** completion history, flattened and sorted, twice (once per kind, no sharing between the two calls), completely unbounded by how long the task has actually been tracked. `TrophiesScreen`'s own task-filter row had the identical bug (same post-filter-not-pre-filter pattern), just less visible there since selecting a task filter is a deliberate tap, not something that happens on every prev/next press.

**Fix**: `getAllAchievementCardStatuses`/`getGroupedAchievementCardStatuses` (`app/utils/achievements.ts`) both gained an optional trailing `kinds: AchievementKind[] = ACHIEVEMENT_KIND_ORDER` param -- backward compatible (every existing call site, including every test, omits it and gets byte-for-byte the same full-catalog behavior as before). A new exported `TASK_SCOPED_KIND_ORDER` (`ACHIEVEMENT_KIND_ORDER.filter(isTaskScopedKind)`, computed once at module load, not re-filtered per call) is passed as `kinds` by both `AchievementsPreviewCard` (when `taskScoped`) and `TrophiesScreen` (when a task filter chip is selected) -- the global kinds are now never computed at all in these paths, not just filtered out of the result afterward, eliminating both the wasted cheap computations and the two real O(n log n) unbounded sorts. `DashboardStatsView`'s own `taskScoped={false}` aggregate call, and both files' "no filter active" cases, correctly keep computing the full catalog (unaffected -- global kinds like Century Club genuinely need to show there). Both files' own prior post-filter `.map(...).filter(s => isTaskScopedKind(...))...filter(g => g.statuses.length > 0)` logic was removed outright, not just left dormant -- `isTaskScopedKind` is no longer imported by either.

**A secondary, lower-severity finding fixed in the same pass**: `AchievementBadgeCard` (`app/components/AchievementCard.tsx`) wasn't wrapped in `React.memo`, despite every one of its props already being referentially stable across an unrelated parent re-render (`status` objects are reused as-is by the now-memoized `groups`; `onPlay` is a stable Zustand action reference; everything else is a primitive). This mattered specifically on the Stats screen because `TaskStatsView`'s own `onScroll` handler (driving `LazyMount`'s visibility tracking, `scrollEventThrottle={100}`) re-renders the whole screen on every scroll tick -- without memoization, every visible achievement card (and `TrophyEmblem`'s own per-render `tinycolor(...).darken(...)` calls) re-rendered along with it for no reason, on every scroll frame. Wrapped in `React.memo` + `.displayName`, matching this codebase's own established pattern (`StatsCharts.tsx`'s chart cards, `ParticleSystem`/`ParticleComponent`).

**What was checked and found already fine, not touched**: `LazyMount` itself (once `hasBeenVisible` flips true, it stays true forever and adds no per-render cost beyond an early-return check -- ruled out as a *new* cost, though the chart-kit full-SVG-redraw it defers-but-doesn't-eliminate is a real, pre-existing, already-documented cost inherent to switching to a task with genuinely different chart data, not something this pass could or should try to fix); `StatsCharts.tsx`'s `React.memo` wrapping (confirmed intact); every `useAchievementsStore`/other store selector across the app (all plain primitive/stable-reference selectors, no broad-subscription re-render fan-out of the kind this app's own pre-Zustand `TaskContext` used to have); the notification-scheduling fire-and-forget calls in `taskStore.ts` (confirmed they only fire from real task mutations -- `addTask`/`completeTask`/etc. -- never from a plain `router.setParams` task switch, so irrelevant to this specific symptom).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (240/240, unaffected -- the `kinds` param is optional and additive, so no existing test needed updating). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is squarely a performance fix (the one class of change this app's own testing scope has no automated way to verify), so worth prioritizing: switching tasks via `TaskHeader`'s prev/next while on the Stats tab, specifically for a task with a long completion history (the scenario that made the eliminated sorts actually expensive), and a general scroll-smoothness check on the Stats screen once several achievement cards are visible at once.

**Follow-up (2026-08-14), prompted by a direct user question during a deeper walkthrough of the achievements engine's performance ("Why is calculating early-bird/night-owl so expensive?... Are we duplicating the logic around achievement completions? There's two code paths?"): early-bird/night-owl's "trailing time-of-day window" logic was genuinely duplicated across detection and display, and comparing the two side by side surfaced a real correctness bug in the display half.** `detectCompletionAchievements` (live, on every completion) and `getAchievementCardStatus`'s own `time-of-day-ratio` case (Trophy Case/Stats progress bars) each carried their own independent implementation of "flatten every active task's completions, sort by `completedAt`, take the trailing window" -- not the same function reused twice. Diffing them directly (rather than assuming they agreed) found they'd already drifted: detection gates on `recent.length >= TIME_OF_DAY_MIN_SAMPLES` (10) before considering *anything*, but the display branch only ever destructured `{ hour, direction, window }` off the strategy object -- `minSamples` was part of the `time-of-day-ratio` strategy's own type shape but never actually read there, so it only bailed out on *literally zero* completions. With as few as 1-2 completions, the progress bar could show something like "1/1" -- reading as fully achieved or right on the edge -- while detection wouldn't even consider Early Bird/Night Owl eligible to fire yet.

**Fix**: extracted the shared logic into two new module-private helpers in `achievements.ts`, both used by both call sites:
- `selectMostRecentByCompletedAt(items, n)` -- a single linear pass maintaining a small ascending-by-`completedAt` buffer (evicting the current oldest, `buffer[0]`, in O(1) when a newer item arrives), rather than a full `[...all].sort(...).slice(0, n)`. Since `n` (`TIME_OF_DAY_WINDOW`, 14) is always tiny, insertion into it is effectively a constant-time step relative to the size of `items` -- O(total history) instead of O(total history × log(total history)) to answer a "top 14" question. This also directly addresses the earlier-flagged "sorts everything just to get the top-k" inefficiency, not just the duplication.
- `getTimeOfDayWindow(activeTasks, window, minSamples, override?)` -- wraps the selection above, returning `{ recent, meetsMinSamples }`; `override` preserves `detectRetroactiveAchievements`' own already-windowed `recentCompletionsOverride` slice unchanged (see that function's own comment on why it precomputes this once per replay event rather than re-deriving it).

Both `detectCompletionAchievements`'s early-bird/night-owl block and `getAchievementCardStatus`'s `time-of-day-ratio` case now call `getTimeOfDayWindow` and gate on the same `meetsMinSamples` -- the display path's old, looser `allCompletions.length === 0` check was removed outright (subsumed by the stricter, now-shared `minSamples` floor), so the two can no longer independently disagree on what counts as "enough samples to show anything."

**Deliberately not done in this same pass, per explicit user direction after a design discussion**: caching/invalidating achievement progress so it's computed once per real data change (a completion, undo, task-settings edit, archive/restore/delete, or a day rollover) rather than fresh on every screen view. Discussed at length -- the plan, if picked back up later, is a single `useEffect` in `_layout.tsx`'s `AppGate` watching `[tasks, achievements]` (catches every task mutation for free via reference-identity, and catches day-rollover for free too, since `maybeRefreshStats`'s own day-boundary recompute already produces a new `tasks` reference) writing into a new non-persisted `achievementsStore` field, rather than wiring a refresh call into each of `taskStore.ts`'s ~10 individual mutators. Deferred because, after this fix, the remaining per-view cost is small and bounded (capped at the free-tier's 6 active tasks, mostly O(1)/O(#tasks) reads of already-cached `task.stats`) -- the performance argument that originally motivated it is largely already addressed by yesterday's fix plus this one; the architectural-consistency argument (matching how `task.stats` itself already works) is still valid but was treated as a separate, later decision rather than bundled in here.

2 new regression tests in `__tests__/utils/achievements.test.ts` (242 total, up from 240): the `minSamples` bug reproduced directly (5 qualifying completions, all before 7am, previously showed misleading progress, now correctly shows none) and a dedicated bounded-selection correctness test (20 completions, only the truly-most-recent 14 should count -- proving the new O(n) selection doesn't silently include stale ones or pick the wrong subset the way a subtly-broken bounded-buffer implementation could). One pre-existing test's fixture (5 total completions, below the real 10-sample floor) was updated to use a valid 12-completion sample, since it was unknowingly exercising the exact buggy under-threshold path being fixed here.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (242/242). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a look specifically at Early Bird/Night Owl's own Trophy Case card once a task has fewer than 10 recent completions (should now correctly show no progress bar at all, not a misleading fraction), and general confirmation that detection itself still fires correctly once real usage crosses the 10-completion floor.

**Follow-up (2026-08-14), same day: the two remaining "is this achievement achieved" duplications between live detection and the retroactive scan (surfaced by directly asking "is there a single source of truth?") were closed too, per explicit user direction after weighing both.** Both are the two kinds that *aren't* completion-driven -- `anniversary` (task-age) and `habit-collector` (active-task-count) can become true from pure time passing or a task being added, with no completion event involved, so `detectRetroactiveAchievements` was already evaluating them directly rather than via its usual "replay completions through `detectCompletionAchievements`" mechanism -- but `detectCompletionAchievements` *also* independently re-checked the same two conditions (so it can still catch them live, using a completion as a convenient trigger even though completion isn't what causes them). Two genuinely different shapes of duplication, fixed differently:

- **`anniversary`**: both call sites had typed out the identical `differenceInCalendarDays(referenceDate, parseISO(task.createdAt)) >= days` check independently -- low practical drift risk (simple date arithmetic), but still two hand-written copies of one condition. Extracted a new `hasReachedTaskAge(task, days, referenceDate)` pure predicate (placed right after `TASK_AGE_ENTRIES`'s own definition); both `detectCompletionAchievements` (passing `nextTask`/`date`) and `detectRetroactiveAchievements` (passing each `task`/`today` inside its existing per-task loop) now call it instead of inlining the comparison. Pure refactor -- the surrounding loop shapes (one task vs. a loop over `activeTasks`) are unchanged, only the boolean check moved.
- **`habit-collector`**: a more structural gap than anniversary's, not just typed-twice math. `detectRetroactiveAchievements` already looped over `ACTIVE_TASK_COUNT_ENTRIES` generically (the same `ACHIEVEMENT_META`-derived-list pattern `FIXED_THRESHOLD_ENTRIES`/`TASK_AGE_ENTRIES`/`TOTAL_COMPLETIONS_SUM_ENTRIES` already use everywhere else in this file, specifically so a new tier only ever needs a metadata entry, no code change) -- but `detectCompletionAchievements` had a single hardcoded `if (isFirstEarn('habit-collector', 'global') && activeTasksAfter.length >= MAX_ACTIVE_TASKS)`, never reading the generic list at all. Concretely: a hypothetical future second `active-task-count` kind would have been picked up automatically by the retroactive scan, but silently never detected live (the only path that actually matters day-to-day) until someone happened to run a manual "check for missed achievements" scan -- not a subtle drift risk like anniversary's, a real gap in one path's ability to see a whole future class of kind at all. Fixed by replacing the hardcoded `if` with the same generic `for (const { kind, target } of ACTIVE_TASK_COUNT_ENTRIES) { if (!isFirstEarn(kind, 'global')) continue; if (activeTasksAfter.length >= target) earned.push(...); }` shape the retroactive scan already used -- for today's catalog (one entry, `target === MAX_ACTIVE_TASKS` by definition in the metadata) this is byte-identical behavior for Habit Collector itself, confirmed by every existing habit-collector test passing completely unchanged; the actual fix is that detection now automatically participates in the same "add metadata, get it for free" mechanism every other multi-kind family already relies on.

No new tests were added for either -- both are behavior-preserving refactors for the one real kind each currently has, verified by the full existing suite passing unchanged (242/242, same as before this pass). Explicitly *not* attempted: a test proving "a hypothetical future active-task-count kind would now be caught live" -- that would mean injecting a fake entry into the real `ACHIEVEMENT_META` catalog, which isn't supported without adding test-only infrastructure disproportionate to this fix; the actual guarantee here is structural (matches the established generic-list pattern, verifiable by reading the code) rather than independently unit-testable against a kind that doesn't exist yet.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (242/242, unchanged count -- pure refactors, no new or modified test cases). Not yet confirmed on-device.

## Task reminders / notifications (2026-08-13)

Per-task escalating reminders, matching `TODO.md`'s own long-standing "Nag level" spec exactly (0=none, 1=dismissable, 2=repeated dismissable, 3=ongoing, 4=alarm+ongoing). `Task` gained an optional `notifications: { level: NotificationLevel; time: string; nagIntervalMinutes?: number }` (absent = off; existing pre-feature tasks are never silently upgraded, only new tasks default to Level 1 at 9:00 AM, per explicit user direction after weighing on-by-default vs. opt-in).

`app/utils/notifications.ts` is the whole engine: a pure, unit-tested `getNextReminderOccurrence(task, now)` (reuses `isDueOnDate`/`isTaskCompleted` from `streaks.ts` so a reminder's schedule can never disagree with what the rest of the app already considers "due") plus native scheduling functions. Deliberately schedules only the **single next due-and-incomplete occurrence** per task (a one-shot `DateTriggerInput`, not an OS-level recurring trigger) and recomputes it fresh on every mutation that could affect it (`taskStore`'s `addTask`/`updateTask`/`completeTask`/`undoCompleteTask`/`uncompleteTask`/`restoreCompletion`/`archiveTask`/`deleteTask`/`restoreTask`/`restoreDeletedTask`, all fire-and-forget) plus a bulk self-healing sweep in `_layout.tsx`'s `AppGate`, riding the same `AppState`-active/hydration trigger `maybeRefreshStats` already uses. Deterministic identifiers (`reminder:${taskId}`, `reminder:${taskId}:nag${n}`) mean nothing needs to be persisted to know what's currently scheduled -- cancelling/dismissing a nonexistent identifier is a safe no-op.

Two Android channels: `reminders` (default importance, levels 1-3) and `reminders-alarm` (max importance, strong vibration, level 4 only) -- `sticky`/`autoDismiss` are per-notification content flags shared across levels 1-3, but importance/vibration are channel-level and fixed at creation, so only level 4 needed its own channel. **Level 4 is explicitly scoped short of a true alarm** (confirmed with the user directly): it buzzes once on delivery, then just sits non-dismissable -- no continuous looping vibration/sound, no full-screen wake, no lock-screen bypass, no `SCHEDULE_EXACT_ALARM` (Play Store restricts that permission to actual alarm/calendar-core apps). A real continuous-buzz alarm would need a foreground service, which `expo-notifications` doesn't expose at all.

New deps: `expo-notifications`, `@react-native-community/datetimepicker` (+ its auto-added config plugin), plus an `expo-notifications` plugin entry in `app.json`. Both are native, so this required the project's first EAS dev-client build (`eas build --profile development --platform android`) -- Expo Go can't load `expo-notifications` at all (SDK 53+ removed it there entirely) and crashes at `_layout.tsx`'s top-level import.

**`AddTaskScreen.tsx` redesign (2026-08-13), per explicit user direction, landing alongside the notifications UI itself:**
- Every form section (Task Name, Icon, Color, Frequency, and the renamed "Nag Level") is now wrapped in a new shared `app/components/CollapsibleSection.tsx` -- a header row (title + chevron) with a divider underneath, tap-to-toggle, content only mounted while expanded. All sections start expanded by default (collapsing is a per-visit convenience, not a way to hide required fields like Task Name).
- The old "Reminders" section, renamed **"Nag Level"**, replaced its pill-row level picker with a hand-built discrete "slider": five icon stops (`bell-off-outline`/`bell-outline`/`bell-ring-outline`/`bell-alert-outline`/`alarm-light-outline`) connected by a track, with a `selectedColor`-filled portion up to the current level. Deliberately **tap-to-select only, no drag gesture** -- a real pan-gesture slider was considered but skipped to avoid shipping untested gesture/snapping math; visually it still reads as a slider (connected track + stops), just without the drag interaction. No new native dependency was pulled in for this (avoided `@react-native-community/slider` specifically so as not to force yet another dev-client rebuild right as the user was mid-testing the one from the notifications work itself).
- The description below the slider is now one dynamic sentence per level with **inline, tappable settings** embedded directly in the text (e.g. "One reminder at **9:00 AM**." / "A reminder at **9:00 AM**, plus every **30 minutes** until you complete it.") rather than a static description plus a separate settings row. Built via RN's native support for nesting a pressable `<Text onPress={...}>` inside a parent `<Text>` (the same inline-tappable-span technique already established elsewhere in this app, e.g. achievements' `DescriptionText`). The time span opens the real native time picker on native platforms; the nag-interval span cycles through fixed presets (15/30/45/60 min) on tap -- both styled in the task's own `selectedColor`, underlined, to read as clearly interactive.
- This required promoting the nag interval from a fixed 30-minute constant to a genuine per-task setting: `TaskNotificationSettings.nagIntervalMinutes?: number` (optional, `DEFAULT_NAG_INTERVAL_MINUTES` exported from `notifications.ts` as the fallback/prefill), with `scheduleTaskNotifications` reading it instead of a hardcoded value.
- The web-only time-input fallback (`@react-native-community/datetimepicker` has no web implementation) survives as a small separate row below the description on web specifically, since a plain `TextInput` can't be nested inside the inline `<Text>` sentence the way the tappable span can.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- the pure `getNextReminderOccurrence` tests don't touch the interval/UI changes at all). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this whole feature can only be verified on a real dev-client build, per its own section above.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): collapsed sections now show the current value instead of the plain title, per explicit user direction ("The collapsed view should show the selected values rather than the title").** `CollapsibleSection` gained an optional `summary?: React.ReactNode` prop, rendered in the header in place of `title` while collapsed (expanded always shows the plain title, since the content below already shows the live value at that point; falls back to the title if a caller omits `summary`). Each of the five sections in `AddTaskScreen.tsx` now passes one: Task Name shows the trimmed name (or "Untitled"); Icon shows the actual glyph in `selectedColor`; Color shows a small filled swatch circle; Frequency reuses the existing `formatFrequencyLabel` (`utils/formatFrequency.ts` -- the same human-readable "Daily"/"3x weekly"/"4x daily" formatter `TaskHeader`'s own frequency badge already uses, rather than inventing a second copy of that logic); Nag Level shows `"${levelLabel} at ${time}"` or `"Off"`.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): the Alarm level's description reworded for clarity, per direct user feedback ("it isn't clear what it does").** The prior copy ("...that stays until the task is done -- louder and harder to miss") was vague marketing language that didn't actually say what's different from Persist. Replaced with something concrete and honest about the real mechanism and its limits: "...that stays until the task is done, with a stronger buzz and higher priority than a normal reminder -- though it won't ring continuously or wake a locked screen like a real alarm" -- directly reflecting the scoping decision above rather than implying more than what's actually built.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): that same description immediately simplified again, per direct user feedback ("the alarm text is over the top").** The clarity pass above overcorrected into a run-on explanation. Replaced with the user's own proposed phrasing almost verbatim: "A buzzing alarm at {time} and a reminder that stays until the task is done." -- shorter, and deliberately drops the earlier caveat about not ringing continuously/waking a locked screen (the user's own replacement text omitted it, read as a direct request to cut it, not an oversight to restore).

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): Nag Level's collapsed summary text "Off" changed to "No reminders", per direct user feedback that it "lacks context."** A bare "Off" reads ambiguously in a collapsed section header with no other context nearby (off *what*?); every other level's summary is a full clause ("Once at 9:00 AM"), so the off-state needed to match that same self-explanatory shape rather than being the one terse exception.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): both Nag Level's and Frequency's collapsed summaries gained a leading icon, per direct user feedback that all the text-only summaries "lack context."** Nag Level now shows that level's own icon (the same glyph the icon-slider itself uses, `bell-off-outline` through `alarm-light-outline`) ahead of the text -- computed once into a new `currentNotificationLevelOption` variable (`NOTIFICATION_LEVEL_OPTIONS.find(...)`) shared between this summary and `timeLabel`'s own neighboring usage, rather than repeating the `.find()` call. Frequency (referred to by the user as "repeatability") gained a `repeat` icon -- the same glyph `TaskHeader`'s own frequency badge already established as this app's icon for "how often." Both icons are tinted `selectedColor`, matching the Icon section's own summary (the task's actual icon rendered in its actual color) and this form's general pattern of accenting active/current values with the task's own color. A new shared `summaryRow` style (`flexDirection: 'row', alignItems: 'center', gap: 6`) wraps icon+text for both.

**A real bug found and fixed the same day: permission was only ever requested reactively, never at save time.** `ensureNotificationPermissions()` was only called from the Nag Level slider's own `onPress` -- but a brand-new task already defaults to Level 1 with no tap required to reach it, and editing an existing level>0 task's *other* fields (reported directly: just the reminder time) never touches the slider either. Either path could leave Android's permission stuck at "never asked" forever, and `scheduleTaskNotifications`'s own `if (!granted) return` would then silently no-op with zero indication anything was wrong. Fixed in `handleSave`: permission is now also requested whenever the level being saved is nonzero, regardless of how the user arrived at that level -- a no-op cost on the already-working path, since `ensureNotificationPermissions` itself already short-circuits to an immediate `true` once permission is granted.

Alongside this, the fire-and-forget scheduling calls in `taskStore.ts` (`rescheduleFor`/`cancelFor`) and `_layout.tsx` (`rescheduleAllTaskNotifications`) were swallowing every failure with a bare `.catch(() => undefined)` -- real diagnosability had been a genuine gap (this exact bug needed to be root-caused by reading code alone, since nothing had ever surfaced a failure signal). All three now `console.warn` on a genuine error instead, costing nothing on the happy path.

**A dev-only debug screen added the same day, per direct user request ("is there a way we can debug and see what scheduled notifications the app has?").** New `app/screens/DebugNotificationsScreen.tsx` (+ thin route `app/debug-notifications.tsx`, registered in `_layout.tsx`'s `Stack` matching every other simple-push route) reads directly from `expo-notifications`' own inspection APIs -- `getPermissionsAsync`, `getAllScheduledNotificationsAsync`, `getPresentedNotificationsAsync` -- rather than trusting anything this app itself believes it scheduled, specifically so it can't lie the way the silently-swallowed failures above already did once. Shows permission status, every currently-scheduled request (identifier, content, raw trigger JSON), and everything currently sitting in the notification shade, each with a pull-to-refresh-equivalent manual refresh button. Reachable from a new "Developer" section in Settings, gated on `__DEV__` (a real RN/Metro global, `false` in a release build) so neither the row nor the screen ships to a real user.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- the permission fix and debug screen are both additive/pure, no `utils/` logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is the direct next step for actually diagnosing the original "permission granted, no reminder" report.

**The Trophy Case's locked-card preview feature (see its own long history earlier in this doc) also gated behind `__DEV__` the same day, per direct user follow-up ("can we also make it so that the ability to preview locked achievements is also guarded behind dev").** This feature had already gone through testing-only -> removed -> explicitly restored as permanent (2026-08-12); this follow-up doesn't remove it again, just narrows who it's for. `AchievementBadgeCard` gained `canPreviewLocked = __DEV__` and `isDisabled = !unlocked && !canPreviewLocked` -- a locked card is tappable (preview) only in dev builds, and a plain no-op in production, matching this screen's own original pre-preview-feature behavior for a real user. `TouchableOpacity` picked up `disabled={isDisabled}` + a matching `accessibilityState`, and the accessibility label now has three states instead of two (`Replay ...` / `Preview ...` / `${title}, locked`) so a screen reader correctly reports a genuinely locked-and-inert card differently from a previewable one. `buildPreviewAchievement`'s own comment updated to record this scoping change directly, following this doc's own convention of noting *why* a past decision changed rather than silently rewriting the earlier entry.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- pure conditional-gating change, no logic touched).

**How the "next occurrence" scheduling actually behaves for a multi-day frequency, clarified directly in response to a user question (2026-08-13): it does NOT pre-schedule every future due day.** At any moment, at most one "main" notification (plus up to `NAG_MAX_REPEATS` follow-ups for Level 2) is ever scheduled *per task* -- never a whole week's worth of weekday occurrences in advance. `getNextReminderOccurrence` only ever returns the *single* next due-and-incomplete moment; `scheduleTaskNotifications` cancels whatever was previously scheduled and replaces it with just that one, every time it runs. Completing a day *does* correctly cancel/dismiss that day's now-moot reminder -- `cancelTaskNotifications` calls both `cancelScheduledNotificationAsync` (a not-yet-fired pending one) and `dismissNotificationAsync` (an already-delivered one still sitting in the shade) for every identifier this task could have, so this works whether the completion happens before or after that day's reminder already fired.

**The real, currently-unaddressed limitation this surfaces**: advancing to the *next* occurrence only happens when something re-triggers `scheduleTaskNotifications` -- a task mutation (complete/edit/etc.) or the app coming to the foreground (`_layout.tsx`'s `AppState`-active sweep). If the user never reopens the app and never touches the task between occurrences, only the one single reminder that was already scheduled at the last save/foreground moment will ever fire -- nothing beyond it gets queued, since there's no OS-level recurring trigger backing it up (a deliberate architectural choice, made specifically to keep per-day cancellation simple -- see this section's own "Scheduling architecture" reasoning above). Whether this needs a real fix (e.g., pre-scheduling several future due days at once, with per-day identifiers so an early completion can still cancel just that one day) is an open design question, flagged directly to the user rather than silently built -- the fix is a genuine architecture change (multiple concurrently-tracked future occurrences instead of one), not a small patch.

## ColorPicker and IconPicker: dropped internal scrolling in favor of just growing (2026-08-13)

Per explicit user direction ("it should not have any scrolling... it should just grow in size"), `ColorPicker.tsx`'s color grid no longer lives inside its own `ScrollView` (`maxHeight: 200`, with a scroll-to-end effect firing whenever "Show more colors" revealed another batch). It's now a plain `View` -- tapping "Show more" just grows the grid's own height as more color batches wrap onto new rows, relying entirely on the screen's own outer `ScrollView` (`AddTaskScreen`) for any actual page scrolling, rather than nesting a second, independently-scrolling region inside it. The now-unneeded `scrollViewRef`/`scrollToEnd` effect and the `scrollView`/`scrollContent` styles were removed outright, not just left unused.

**Follow-up, same day: `IconPicker.tsx` got the identical treatment, per direct user follow-up ("The Icon Selector should also have no scroll").** Its own `ScrollView` (`maxHeight: 300`) is gone too, along with both effects that existed purely to manage it -- scroll-to-bottom when "Show more icons" reveals another page, and scroll-to-top when a new search query's results come in. Neither effect has anything left to do once the grid just grows in place instead of scrolling, so both were deleted outright (not left as dead code), along with the `scrollView`/`scrollContent` styles and the now-unused `scrollViewRef`. `searchInputRef` (a separate ref, for focusing the search field -- see this file's own `autoFocus` bugfix earlier today) is untouched.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- pure layout changes, no logic touched; `IconPicker`'s one lint warning, a missing `searchHeight` dependency on its search-reveal animation effect, is pre-existing and unrelated to this change). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a look that tapping "Show more" repeatedly (revealing every batch/every icon page) doesn't push either section's card so tall that the rest of the form feels awkward to reach, now that neither has a cap holding it to a fixed viewport height.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): a "discard changes?" confirmation added on the way out with unsaved edits, following Material Design's own full-screen-dialog convention (researched and confirmed directly, not assumed) after a short design discussion.** Material's guidance for exactly this kind of screen (keyboard-input form, changes that don't apply instantly) is explicit: nothing saves until Save is tapped, and leaving with pending changes should confirm before discarding -- which also validated keeping this screen's existing explicit-Save architecture rather than switching to live/auto-apply editing (the right pattern for independent toggles, like Settings' own switches, not a multi-field record like this one).

Implemented via React Navigation's `usePreventRemove(preventRemove, callback)` (confirmed present and re-exported from the already-installed `@react-navigation/native`, no new dependency) -- intercepts any removal action (back button, header back, swipe-back gesture, and this screen's own programmatic `router.back()`/`dismissTo` calls alike) while `isDirty` is true. `isDirty` is a plain (deliberately non-memoized -- cheap enough not to bother) comparison of every field's current value against a `useRef` snapshot of its own starting value, captured once by mirroring each `useState` initializer exactly (so it's correctly "no changes" relative to either a blank new task or an existing task's real values, matching whichever mode the screen opened in).

The real design subtlety: `usePreventRemove` would otherwise ALSO catch this screen's own *intentional* exits -- most obviously Save's own `router.back()` right after a successful save (which always leaves `isDirty` still `true` at that instant), but also Delete/Archive/Restore's own `router.dismissTo('/')`/`router.back()` calls, which already have their own confirmation (or, for Restore, deliberately none by existing design) and shouldn't get a second, redundant "discard changes?" stacked on top. Solved with a `bypassLeaveGuardRef`, flipped to `true` immediately before each of those four intentional exit points -- checked inside the `usePreventRemove` *callback itself* (not by trying to time when the boolean `preventRemove` argument updates relative to the navigation action, which would depend on React's render-timing and isn't guaranteed to resolve before a synchronous `router.back()` call processes) -- when set, the callback just re-dispatches the original blocked action (`navigation.dispatch(data.action)`) immediately with no prompt at all, sidestepping any render-order race entirely.

The Alert copy ("Discard changes?" / "You'll lose the changes you made to this task." / Cancel · Discard) matches this file's own existing `handleDelete`/`handleArchiveToggle` Alert conventions (a title, a clarifying sentence, `Cancel` + a destructive-styled action) and picks up the same `notificationAsync(Warning)` haptic on the Discard tap.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- pure UI/navigation-flow addition, no `utils/` logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- this is genuinely new interaction territory for the app (first use of `usePreventRemove` anywhere in the codebase) and worth a careful pass: editing a field then backing out shows the prompt; Cancel keeps you on the screen with the edit intact; Discard actually leaves; an untouched screen (or one that was already saved) never shows it; and Delete/Archive/Restore/Save all still exit cleanly with no double-prompt.

**TODO item addressed (2026-08-13): switching Frequency to "Specific Days" now defaults to all 7 days selected, instead of silently carrying over whatever `daysOfWeek` the previous frequency left behind.** For a task that was never Specific Days to begin with, `daysOfWeek` could be an empty array (never set/used by any other frequency type) -- switching to Specific Days rendered that as "no days selected" rather than a sensible starting point. New `handleSelectSpecificDays` (replacing the pill's own inline `() => setFrequency(...)`) sets the frequency and, only when `daysOfWeek` is currently empty, also resets it to all 7 -- deliberately conditional, not unconditional, so a real non-empty selection (the task's own original days, or one already chosen earlier in the same edit session before toggling to another frequency and back) is preserved rather than blown away every time the pill is tapped.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (219/219, unaffected -- pure UI logic, no `utils/` behavior touched).

**TODO item addressed (2026-08-13): "habit" vs "Task" terminology inconsistency across user-facing copy -- resolved in two passes, the first one backwards.** First pass (superseded almost immediately) read the TODO's own example ("Add a Habit" being the outlier against the rest of the app's "Task" wording) as license to standardize on "Task" everywhere, and changed `HomeScreen`/`achievements.ts` accordingly. The user corrected this directly and explicitly: **"Habit" is the intended user-facing term app-wide, not "Task"** -- "I would be okay with some places using task if the context makes that flow better, but overall we want to call these Habits," and separately, "Calling it 'habit' is purely a user facing term" (i.e. the data model -- `Task`, `TaskCard`, `useTaskStore`, `addTask`/`updateTask`, every file/route/type name -- stays exactly as-is; only what a human actually *reads* changes). Asked directly for a genuine opinion on the two options (not just asked to implement), and gave one: "Habit" is the more semantically accurate term regardless of preference, since "task" implies a one-off to-do that gets checked off and is done, while this app's entire premise is the opposite -- something done again and again, where the recurrence itself is the point. "Habit" already carries that connotation; "task" fights it. Matches the genre's own established vocabulary too (Streaks, Habitica, Way of Life) and this app's own tagline.

The full, corrected sweep touched every screen with user-facing copy -- `AddTaskScreen.tsx` (screen title "Add/Edit Habit", "Habit Name" section, placeholder, duplicate-name error, Save/Add button now reads "Add Habit", Archive/Delete/Restore alert titles and button labels, the discard-changes alert body, the Nag Level description sentences, the save-limit/save-failure toasts), `_layout.tsx`'s route-level fallback title, `HomeScreen.tsx` (empty states, FAB/button labels and their accessibility labels, the streak-filter bubble accessibility labels/hints), `SettingsScreen.tsx` ("Habit Cards" section, "Show Habit Name" switch + its accessibility label, "Archived Habits" nav row, the import-count toast), `ArchivedTasksScreen.tsx` (header title, empty state, edit-habit accessibility hint), `TaskHeader.tsx` (Edit/Previous/Next accessibility labels), `DashboardScreen.tsx`'s task-filter onboarding hint, `DashboardStatsView.tsx`/`DashboardStreaksView.tsx`/`DashboardCalendarView.tsx` (accessibility hints, empty states), `AboutScreen.tsx`'s privacy-policy sentence, and a large pass through `achievements.ts` -- every *factual* `describe`/`triggerStandalone`/`numberBlock.unit`/`ribbon.unit` string that names the tracked item (Century Club, Habit Collector, Early Bird/Night Owl, Perfect Day, Perfect Week), plus the `taskName()` helper's own fallback string ("This habit", for the rare case a per-task achievement instance has no `taskName` recorded).

**Deliberately left saying "task"/kept as-is**, per the user's own "some places using task if context flows better" allowance:
- Every internal identifier, file name, route name, type name, and code comment (`Task`, `useTaskStore`, `taskId`, `habit-collector` as a persisted kind string, etc.) -- purely a user-facing-copy change, zero data-model impact, confirmed by construction (nothing in `app/types`, `app/stores`, or any `utils/` module's actual logic was touched).
- The achievement's own stylized proper-noun title/eyebrow "Habit Collector!" -- a badge *name*, not a literal description, same category as "Night Owl"/"Early Bird"/"Century Club" (none of those are literal either).
- Flavor text like Night Owl's "one habit at a time" -- deliberately casual/idiomatic copy by this file's own established convention, not something forced into strict terminology consistency.
- `AboutScreen.tsx`'s tagline "A playful habit & streak tracker" -- already said "habit," needed no change; kept as the app's own genre/category descriptor.
- The `console.warn`/thrown-`Error` diagnostic strings (`_layout.tsx`'s "Failed to reschedule task notifications", `importExport.ts`'s "Invalid task list") -- developer-facing log/error text, never actually shown to a real user (the import one is caught and replaced with a friendly toast before the user ever sees it).

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (219/219, unaffected -- confirmed no test asserts on any of the changed copy strings before editing, both times).

## Task action buttons: visual hierarchy, "Add Task" label, and task-colored Save (2026-08-13)

(Superseded by the later habit/task terminology sweep above -- the button/labels described below as "Add Task"/"Archive Task"/"Delete Task"/"Restore Task" now read "Add Habit"/"Archive Habit"/"Delete Habit"/"Restore Habit". The visual hierarchy/color decisions in this section are unaffected and still accurate.)

Per explicit user direction ("better visual hierarchy (create/save is the most important)... use 'Create' or 'Add' in the label during creation... style the button after the user's task selections"), three related changes to `AddTaskScreen.tsx`'s bottom action area:

1. **Save/Add is now visually the dominant control, Archive/Delete visibly secondary.** Previously all three were equally-weighted solid-fill full-width buttons stacked vertically (blue Save, then gray Archive, then red Delete, all identical padding/font-size/shape) -- nothing distinguished "the one action you're here to take" from "the two rarer, more consequential ones." Redesigned around Material's own filled-over-outlined hierarchy: `saveButton` kept as the one full-width, solid-filled, *now also elevated* (a real shadow, matching `card`'s own treatment) button, with bigger padding (18 vs. the old 16) and bolder/larger text (17px/700 vs. 16px/600). `manageButton` (Archive/Delete) flipped from solid-fill to **outlined** (`borderWidth: 1.5`, transparent fill, colored border+icon+text instead of white-on-solid), shrunk (12px vertical padding vs. 16, 14px/600 text vs. 16px/600, 18px icons vs. 20px), and laid out **side by side** (`manageSection` flexDirection `row`, each button `flex: 1`) instead of stacked -- both smaller footprint and a visually quieter tier, exactly matching the "filled > outlined > text" weight progression Material's own button hierarchy guidance already describes (the same guidance just researched for the discard-changes work above). Their labels were also shortened ("Archive Task"/"Delete Task"/"Restore Task" -> "Archive"/"Delete"/"Restore") now that the smaller, paired layout doesn't have room to spare and the icon already carries some of that meaning.
2. **Label reads "Add Task" while creating, not "Save Task."** Matches the screen's own existing header title (`Stack.Screen`'s `title: isEditing ? 'Edit Task' : 'Add New Task'`) and the app's established "Add Task" terminology elsewhere, rather than "Save" implying something already exists to save. Editing still reads "Save Changes," unchanged.
3. **Save/Add's background color is now the task's own `selectedColor`** (set inline, replacing the previous flat hardcoded `#007AFF`) -- continuing the exact "tie this control to the user's own current selections" treatment already used throughout this same screen (day-of-week buttons, frequency-type pills, the nag-level slider's filled track/stops) rather than introducing a one-off exception. The disabled state (`saveButtonDisabled`) is untouched -- still a flat neutral gray regardless of `selectedColor`, correctly not implying "this color is the reason it's disabled." Archive/Delete's own colors (`ARCHIVE_COLOR`/`DELETE_COLOR`, new module-level constants replacing the old inline hex literals) are deliberately *not* tied to `selectedColor` -- they're fixed semantic colors (gray/red) unrelated to which task color happens to be chosen.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- pure styling/copy change, no logic touched). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a look that the outlined secondary buttons still read clearly as tappable (border-only buttons can sometimes look inert) and that the elevated/shadowed Save button doesn't look out of place against this screen's existing flat `card` sections.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): the two-tier hierarchy above was actually meant to be three, per direct user clarification ("add/save is 1, archive is 2, delete is 3").** The prior pass gave Archive and Delete the *same* outlined tier, side by side -- differentiated from Save but not from each other. Split apart: Archive keeps the outlined-button treatment from the entry above (now alone on its own full-width row, `manageSection` reverted from `row` back to a stacked column now that there's no sibling to share it with), while Delete drops down to the third rung of the same filled-over-outlined-over-text hierarchy already cited above -- a plain text link (`deleteLink`/`deleteLinkText`: no border, no fill, smaller icon/text, `paddingVertical: 12` only) sitting quietly below Archive. Deliberately the *quietest* control on the whole screen despite being the most consequential one -- the point of demoting a destructive action's visual weight (rather than making it loud/alarming) is to stop it from competing for attention or inviting an accidental tap, while its color alone still unambiguously marks it as "danger" the moment someone actually looks at it. Both buttons' labels reverted to their fuller original wording ("Archive Task"/"Restore Task"/"Delete Task") now that neither needs to fit into a shared half-width row anymore.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- pure styling change, no logic touched).

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): sections now default to collapsed when editing an existing task, expanded when creating a new one, per direct user direction after a short design discussion.** `expandedSections`' initial state changed from a flat `true` for every key to `!isEditing` for every key -- creating still opens every section immediately (nothing to review yet, collapsing first would only cost extra taps while filling the form in for the first time), while editing now opens with every section collapsed, relying on the collapsed summaries (name/icon/color/frequency/nag level, all added earlier today) to let the whole existing config be scanned at a glance without scrolling past sections that aren't being changed. This resolved a real tension with Task Name's own `autoFocus` (added the same day) without needing any special-casing: since `autoFocus` only fires when the `TextInput` actually mounts, and mounting is already tied to a section's own expand transition, collapsing Task Name by default in edit mode simply means nothing autofocuses on open (correct -- there's no reason to assume the user opened an edit screen specifically to retype the name), while manually expanding it later still triggers the identical autofocus behavior it already had.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): Task Name auto-focuses whenever its section expands, per direct user direction.** A one-line fix (`autoFocus` added to the Task Name `TextInput`) that works cleanly *because* `CollapsibleSection`'s content is conditionally mounted, not just visually hidden (`{expanded && <View>...}`) -- toggling the section from collapsed to expanded genuinely (re)mounts the `TextInput`, so RN's own mount-time `autoFocus` behavior naturally re-fires every time, with no extra ref/effect code needed. This is also true on the very first render, since every section starts expanded by default -- so opening Add/Edit Task focuses the name field immediately, the originally-intended behavior the earlier `IconPicker` `autoFocus` bug (see that section's own history above) had been accidentally stealing.

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): a subtle, fixed category icon was added to each section's header (shown only while expanded), then reverted the same turn per direct user pushback ("get rid of the expanded header icons").** Briefly added an optional `titleIcon?: MaterialCommunityIconName` to `CollapsibleSection` (Task Name `pencil-outline`, Icon `shape-outline`, Color `palette-outline`, Frequency `calendar-sync-outline`, Nag Level `bell-cog-outline`), then removed entirely -- `CollapsibleSection.tsx` is back to just `title`/`summary`, and none of the five sections in `AddTaskScreen.tsx` pass a `titleIcon` prop (the prop itself no longer exists). The autoFocus fix from the same turn (see the entry above) is unaffected -- it was independent of this.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected -- the autoFocus change is additive/pure UI, and this revert is a clean removal, no logic touched).

**Follow-up (2026-08-13): a live-value summary for the Icon/Color sections was tried, then explicitly reverted in favor of the two just showing their plain "Icon"/"Color" title text like every un-summarized section already does.** First pass added a name alongside each section's existing glyph/swatch summary (a new `COLOR_NAMES: Record<string, string>` lookup in `constants/task.tsx`, promoting the hex→name pairs that had previously only existed as source comments on `DEFAULT_COLORS`/`EXTENDED_COLOR_BATCHES`, plus a humanized icon name via the same `icon.replace(/-/g, ' ')` transform `IconPicker`'s own accessibility label already uses) — reverted twice in quick succession, first back to the original bare glyph/swatch summary, then a second time (per direct follow-up, "Instead just show 'Icon' and 'Color'") to no custom `summary` prop at all, letting both fall back to `CollapsibleSection`'s own default (the plain section title) exactly like a section with nothing meaningful to preview would. `COLOR_NAMES` was removed from `constants/task.tsx` entirely rather than left unused — nothing else in the app ended up reading it. `ColorPicker.tsx`'s own accessibility label reverted alongside it, back to its original honest hex fallback (`` `Color ${color}` ``) — untouched otherwise.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (222/222, unaffected throughout all three passes -- pure copy/summary changes, no logic touched).

**Correction, same day: the third revert above went too far -- "Instead just show 'Icon' and 'Color'" meant *pair* the plain label with the existing glyph/swatch chip, not drop the chip entirely.** Per direct follow-up ("the collapsed view of icon and color should still have the color chip and icon, you should not have removed them, I just wanted to also add the 'Icon' and 'Color' labels"), both sections' `summary` prop is back -- a `summaryIconRow` (icon glyph in `selectedColor`, or a `summaryColorSwatch` circle filled `selectedColor`) followed by the literal word "Icon"/"Color" via the existing `summaryText` style. This is deliberately *not* the same as the very first (also-reverted) pass: that one paired the chip with a dynamic, per-value name (a humanized icon name, a real color name via the since-removed `COLOR_NAMES` table); this pairs it with the static section label instead -- the chip alone already communicates the actual current selection visually (the real icon glyph, the real color swatch), so the plain word next to it is just enough text to identify *what* the chip represents without needing to name the specific value.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (219/219, unaffected -- pure copy/summary change, no logic touched).

## Auto-suggested icon from the habit name (2026-08-13)

Closes a standing `TODO.md` item. New `app/utils/iconSuggestions.ts`, `suggestIconForName(name): MaterialCommunityIconName | null` — a pure, unit-tested function (`__tests__/utils/iconSuggestions.test.ts`, 8 cases) matching this app's own established `utils/` testability convention. A hand-curated, priority-ordered `ICON_STEMS` table (fitness, mind/wellness, learning, food/drink, sleep/hygiene, chores, hobbies, health, pets/plants, money, social) maps a word *stem* to an icon, checked via `word.startsWith(stem)` against every word in the (lowercased) name — deliberately not a plain substring search across the whole name, which would false-positive on "read" inside "already"/"spread" or "cat" inside "vacation"; requiring the match to start at a real word boundary while still covering common inflections (run/runs/running, meditate/meditation/meditating) avoids that class of bug entirely. Every icon name in the table was verified two ways before committing to it: cross-checked directly against the installed `@expo/vector-icons` glyph map JSON, and — since `MaterialCommunityIconName` is a strict type derived straight from that same glyph map (`keyof typeof MaterialCommunityIcons.glyphMap`) — a plain `tsc` run would have caught any typo as a compile error regardless, so the type system itself is the final backstop here.

**Wired into `AddTaskScreen.tsx`** via a new `iconManuallySet` state (`useState(isEditing)`) and a `useEffect` keyed on `[name, iconManuallySet]`: while `!iconManuallySet`, every name change re-runs `suggestIconForName` and applies a match directly to `selectedIcon`. Two deliberate scope limits, both matching this app's own established "smart defaults never silently override an existing choice" precedent (see e.g. the notification-level default's own "existing tasks are never silently upgraded" reasoning):
- **New tasks only.** `iconManuallySet` starts `true` immediately when `isEditing`, so the effect's own early-return means editing an existing task's name can never reach in and swap out an already-established icon, no matter what the new name happens to contain.
- **Stops the instant the user picks one themselves.** `IconPicker`'s `onIconSelect` now routes through a new `handleIconSelect` (replacing a bare `setSelectedIcon` prop) that also flips `iconManuallySet` to `true` — from that point on the effect's own guard keeps it from ever touching `selectedIcon` again for the rest of that session, satisfying the TODO's own "should still leave the icon freely overridable via the existing picker" requirement: a manual pick always wins, permanently, not just until the next keystroke.

`selectedIcon` is deliberately excluded from the effect's own dependency array (with an inline eslint-disable, matching this file's already-established pattern for the identical "would refire itself" class of exclusion elsewhere) -- including it would make every auto-applied suggestion immediately re-trigger the same effect that just set it.

No interaction with the discard-changes guard needed special-casing: `iconManuallySet` is pure transient UI state, never part of `initialFormValues`/`isDirty`'s own comparison, while `selectedIcon` itself already was -- so a name-driven auto-suggestion correctly marks the form dirty exactly like a manual icon change would (the icon genuinely did change from its starting value), with no new logic required there.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (227/227, up from 219 with this feature's 8 new tests). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a look at: typing a recognizable habit name on a fresh Add Task screen actually swaps the icon live, that picking a different icon manually afterward sticks regardless of further name edits, and that editing an *existing* task's name never touches its icon.

**Real bug found and fixed the same day, reported directly against the feature above: an auto-suggested icon didn't appear selected in `IconPicker`'s own grid.** Root cause was in `IconPicker.tsx`, not the new suggestion feature itself: its `icons` list (what actually renders as tiles) is seeded once via `useState(() => getInitialIcons(selectedIcon))` -- `getInitialIcons` already has its own established behavior of prepending the current selection when it isn't in the default page (the same "always keep the current pick visible/selectable even if it predates the shown list" pattern `ColorPicker`'s own `colors` memo uses), but a `useState` lazy initializer only ever runs once, at mount. It doesn't re-fire just because the `selectedIcon` *prop* changes afterward -- which is exactly what the new auto-suggestion does, updating `selectedIcon` well after this component already mounted. An auto-suggested icon outside the initially-shown page therefore applied silently with no visible selected tile anywhere in the grid, even though the actual underlying value was correct.

Fixed with a new `useEffect` watching `[selectedIcon, debouncedQuery]`: whenever `selectedIcon` changes to something not already in `icons`, it's prepended the same way `getInitialIcons` already does at mount -- re-applying that same "keep the current pick visible" rule reactively instead of only once. Skipped while a search is active (`debouncedQuery` truthy), since `icons` holds live search results in that mode, not the "always show the current pick" browse list -- splicing an unrelated icon to the front of search results would read as a stray, unexplained result rather than a helpful highlight. This also transparently fixes the identical gap for any *other* way `selectedIcon` could change after mount without a matching tile tap, not just this one new auto-suggestion feature.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (227/227, unaffected -- the one pre-existing `searchHeight` exhaustive-deps warning on this file's own unrelated search-animation effect is untouched, confirmed via `git diff` before treating it as a non-issue).

**Unified with `IconPicker`'s own manual search box, same day, per explicit user direction ("I want both suggestion and search to use the same method -- prefer the stems, but also search against icon name. Try to make the search stems as robust as you can").** Before doing anything, checked directly whether the installed icon library ships any search-term/tag/alias metadata of its own that either feature could have leveraged instead of a hand-built table -- it doesn't: `@expo/vector-icons`' bundled `MaterialCommunityIcons.json` is a flat `{name: codepoint}` map, 7,448 entries, nothing else (confirmed by inspecting the file directly, and by checking whether the upstream Pictogrammers MDI project's own richer `meta.json` -- which does have real tags/aliases -- was pulled in anywhere in `node_modules`; it isn't, that data lives in a separate `@mdi/svg` package this project doesn't depend on). So the stem table is the only source of synonym-aware matching either feature can have.

`app/utils/iconSuggestions.ts` renamed to `app/utils/iconSearch.ts` (better reflects its now-dual purpose; `__tests__/utils/iconSuggestions.test.ts` renamed alongside it) and substantially expanded -- `ICON_STEMS` grew from ~35 single-icon entries to ~115 entries spanning fitness/movement, mind/wellness, learning, food/drink, sleep/hygiene, chores, hobbies, health, habits/vices, pets/plants, money, social, and productivity, with each stem now mapping to `MaterialCommunityIconName[]` (one or more relevant icons, ordered by relevance) instead of a single icon -- necessary once the same table needed to power a multi-result *search* as well as a single-best-guess *suggestion*. Every one of the ~150 icon names in the new table was batch-verified against the real glyph map via a throwaway Python script *before* writing any of the table by hand (catching and swapping out a handful of plausible-sounding but nonexistent names -- `treadmill`, `boxing`, `mop`, `sugar-off`, `climbing`, among others -- for real equivalents), with `tsc` itself as the final backstop either way (`MaterialCommunityIconName` is a strict union derived straight from those same glyph-map keys, so any typo that slipped through is a compile error, not a silent bad suggestion or dead search result).

**Two exported functions now share the one table**: `suggestIconForName` (unchanged signature/behavior, used by `AddTaskScreen`'s auto-suggest -- takes the *first* icon of the first stem that matches any word in the name) and a new `searchIcons(query, allIcons)` (used by `IconPicker`'s own search box) -- stem matches come first (every icon a matching stem maps to, across every word in the query, table-order, deduplicated), followed by a plain substring match against `allIcons`' own literal names (requiring *every* query word to appear in the icon's name -- the exact AND-across-terms rule the search box already used before stems existed) for anything the curated stems don't cover, with anything already surfaced via a stem never repeated. `IconPicker.tsx`'s own inline substring-filter logic was deleted and replaced with a direct call to this shared function; its local `searchIcons` *state* variable (holding the current result list) was renamed to `searchResults` first to avoid colliding with the newly-imported `searchIcons` *function* -- a real naming collision caught immediately by `tsc`, not discovered later.

**A specific ordering risk worth documenting**: since matching is "does the word start with this stem," a short, generic stem can accidentally mask a more specific one that's also its own prefix -- concretely, `work` (→ a briefcase icon) is a literal prefix of `workout` (→ a dumbbell icon), so if `work` were checked first in the table, typing "Workout" would incorrectly suggest the briefcase. Fixed by placing `workout` ahead of `work` in the table (confirmed directly with a dedicated regression test, `'prefers "workout" over the shorter, more generic "work" stem it would otherwise be masked by'`) -- and by grouping broad, common habit categories (fitness, wellness, learning) near the top of the whole table and narrower/rarer ones (social, productivity) further down, as a general mitigation for the same class of collision elsewhere, even though it isn't exhaustively proven safe for every possible pair.

`__tests__/utils/iconSearch.test.ts` grew from 8 to 17 cases -- the original `suggestIconForName` coverage (updated for two behavior changes the expanded table introduced: `'Vacation'` alone replaces the old `'Plan a vacation'` false-positive-guard fixture, since `plan` is now itself a real matched stem; `searchIcons('RUN', ...)`'s case-insensitivity test switched to the single-icon `yoga` stem instead of the two-icon `run` stem, to isolate case-insensitivity from the separate already-covered multi-icon-per-stem behavior) plus new coverage for `searchIcons` itself: empty/whitespace query, stem-match ordering ahead of name matches, the plain name-substring fallback, AND-across-terms for that fallback, no duplicate entries when an icon matches both paths, combining stem matches from multiple distinct query words, and case-insensitivity.

Verified via `tsc`/`eslint`/`jest` (236/236, up from 227 with this pass's 9 net-new tests). Not yet confirmed on-device -- worth a look at: the search box's results now leading with clearly-relevant stem matches before the raw name matches (e.g. searching "run" should show the running-shoe icon first, not buried alphabetically among every icon whose name happens to contain "run"), and that the expanded stem vocabulary doesn't introduce any jarring auto-suggestion surprises on a range of realistic habit names.

## MVP status

All items from the original MVP checklist are implemented as of 2026-08-01:

- ✅ Naming resolved (kept "Streakaholic")
- ✅ Reanimated migration (committed, user-verified on-device)
- ✅ Frequency-aware streak logic (`utils/streaks.ts`) + `daysPerMonth` field
- ✅ Times-per-day tracking (increment-based completions, progress ring, verified end-to-end)
- ✅ Real edit-task flow (verified: prefills, updates in place, no duplicates)
- ✅ `TaskCard`'s dead `onPress`/`onComplete` props removed
- ✅ Archive + restore (verified end-to-end)
- ✅ Initial empty state (two variants: no tasks / filter matches nothing)
- ✅ Settings screen, gear icon fixed
- ✅ Dark mode (verified visually across all screens)
- ✅ Export/import data with schema/versioning + merge-or-replace (2026-08-06: added `TasksExport` wrapper with `schemaVersion`/`exportId`/`exportedAt`, a merge mode alongside replace, and staleness/already-imported prompting — see `app/utils/importExport.ts`). Export verified on web (correct JSON shape) and the merge/parse/validation logic verified via a standalone script; the native file-picker UI itself still can't be driven via browser automation, so **do one manual smoke test**: Settings → Import Data → pick an exported file → confirm the Merge/Replace All prompt behaves as expected (including re-importing the same file to see the "already imported" message).

Remaining backlog is MVP+/MMP/Future tier per `TODO.md` (calendar year map, sharing, reminders, monetization, widgets, sync, onboarding, timeline/heatmap views, points/ranks, social features) plus the gaps listed above.
